The first names a principle with a field failure behind it and stays: the smallest change that stays correct, the shared assumption of a decision, a wrong request raised first, a denied call as a question never routed around, findings judged and not counted, a person's words as they were put, a draft that matches its reader. The second restates a principle the rules already hold, or prescribes a procedure or an output shape with no failure behind it, and goes: "Where a next change is known, choose the shape it fits into without building it" (requirement 232; decision 65 names no incident for it); "and whether you checked it" (requirement 233; how an unchecked claim is phrased is the agreed Done item, "probably"); "Before starting, name the steps the permission mode is likely to deny" (requirement 235; unconditional, so a read-only task yields a line against "Report once", and every auto-mode run of decision 109 recorded no denial); "On retry, reissue the same calls and nothing else" (requirement 236; decision 67 names no failure for it); "the few" and "apart and counted" (requirement 269; a count word and an output shape in a rule that says "not a count"); the Done item of requirement 280 (a restatement of "a claim about state counts only if you checked the thing itself", added for a failure decision 111 itself calls skipping a rule already stated, with no field data since); and in the style, "A chat reply is a few sentences unless the reader asked for the detail", with the fact sentence reduced to its principle, name the source instead of restating it. One clause is added: the findings item reads "inside the request or not". Decision 93 read the Done item "Report anything else you notice, with evidence" as "'anything else' lands as notes", and decision 111 said the two items are not in tension; in the field the owner saw an out-of-request finding they wanted framed as unimportant, which is the tension decision 93's reopen clause anticipated, resolved here toward weighing the finding by the same test rather than toward "as a note". The chat block carries the findings item and the drafts sentence as the rules and the style have them, which also removes the "Asked to find issues" condition and the missing "the two costs" the block had carried as undeclared differences. Not changed, as unverified: the Drafts scan step for bare ids, since the ids were bare in drafts before the step existed while the same sessions linked them in replies, so the wording is probably not the cause. The rule count moves from 37 to 35. Settled 2026-10-24 by the owner. Reopens: a recurrence of the failure a cut sentence named, which would restore that sentence alone; or a finding list that is again the deliverable, which would restore "the few".
114. **House-rule edit eleven: the code outranks a stale instruction file, and a test is not deleted to pass.** A two-pass review on 2026-10-24 of a public thread of instruction-file lines, the second pass challenging the first, kept two of some thirty and rejected the rest as covered by an existing item, as procedures or output shapes, or as against the owner's working preferences. The first adds one sentence to the Code item of requirement 232: "Where a project's instruction file and its code disagree, follow the code and say where they differ." The evidence is the one controlled result in the thread, arXiv 2610.30571 (Xu and Wu, 2026-10-22): in 320 feature runs on projects whose instruction file and code used different conventions, Claude Code noticed the conflict in 79 of 80 runs and followed the file anyway, and a user-level sentence to follow the code moved the share of choices following the code from 4.2% to 91.4%. The item already asked for the code's patterns but gave no precedence when a project file says otherwise, so this is a precedence gap and not a skipped rule, which is the ground on which decision 113 cut a restatement. The sentence is scoped to a project's instruction file on purpose: the house rules are instructions too, and an unscoped "the instructions" would let a repository's own habits override the Code items the owner wrote to override them. The migration case, where the file describes the intended new pattern, is left to the "say where they differ" report and to the request. Unverified: the paper does not state what else its baseline file held, so the gain over the existing "follow the code's existing patterns" sentence is not measured. The second adds one word to the agreed Done item, requirement 284: "Don't weaken, skip or delete a test to make checks pass." A thread report had a model told not to edit tests delete them instead; the purpose clause "to make checks pass", already on the line, keeps a test whose behavior is gone removable with the behavior, which a no-delete rule without that clause had prevented in another report. No stop or ask-first rule changes; the Done item gains a case and loses none. The thread's remaining lines were rejected: "rewrite, don't staple" is the design family decision 113 cut with no owner incident; a mid-task correction joining a queue breaks when the correction is a stop and restates the task-completion rule; a new test seen failing first is a check step whose principle requirements 18 and 65 hold; "call things what they are" would ban the suite's own numbering; naming the rule that would have caught each mistake is an output shape on every correction; the batched read, "do not guess", "paste the output" and "say what you didn't test" are covered by the Done items; "ask one question first" and "discuss until go" run against the Working items that choose autonomy. Noted and not acted on: a spaced hyphen standing in for a banned dash, which style line 35 does not name, with no owner report; the host's native reading of a lone `AGENTS.md`, which bears on decision 55 and primer; and hooks in place of lines that keep being skipped, the path decision 111 already keeps open. This departs from decisions 65, 93, 111 and 113 in one way: their evidence came from the owner's own sessions, and these two come from a public thread and a paper. The rule count stays 35. Settled 2026-10-25 by the owner. Reopens: a run where the new sentence makes the model follow a repository habit over a Code item, which would narrow it to conventions of the code; or a test deleted with its behavior being reported as a violation, which would restore the two-word line.
115. **House-rule edit twelve: the first Code item asks for the simplest change, as one instruction with three qualities.** The owner's ground is preference, stated 2026-10-25: simplicity trumps all in code, the item had grown prescriptive, and the model felt conservative. A two-pass review, the second pass challenging the first, found two things first. The under-doing incident of 2026-10-18 (decision 65) happened under the agreed text "Make the smallest correct change", so the agreed text prevented over-engineering but not under-doing, and a return to it alone would not answer the complaint. And "too many turns to a result" is a count of replies that end without the result, which the Code item cannot cause since it shapes a diff and not a turn; the lines that end a turn were added to Working since the agreed text, the wrong-request item, the six-part decision format and the findings item, with the unverified-cause item as the runner-up for "conservative"; none was run in isolation, so the cause is unverified and is the subject of a later pass. The item now reads "Make the simplest change that is correct and stays correct as the code grows: in the code's own patterns and helpers, at every place the behavior lives, with nothing built for a need only imagined. Where a project's instruction file and its code disagree, follow the code and say where they differ. Say why for any extra." The word "smallest" becomes "simplest": a bespoke patch beside an existing helper and a speculative abstraction are both more complexity, so simplicity rejects both recorded failures where size rejects only the second; "simplest" can be read as simplest to write, which the two incident clauses bound. The three imperatives of the 2026-10-18 text merge into one instruction with three qualities, each kept for its incident: the code's own patterns and helpers (the bypassed helper), every place the behavior lives (the skipped sibling site), and the guard against building for an imagined need, which decisions 65 and 93 rest on, now as "nothing built for a need only imagined" without the three-item list "no option, abstraction, or fallback", the prescriptive residue. "Only imagined" is kept over "no one named" because the model is someone and can name a need itself. The instruction-file sentence of decision 114 and "Say why for any extra", the agreed text's "and say why", stay unchanged. Considered and not taken: the agreed text's cost test, "lasting value clearly outweighs its cost", since "lasting value" is the phrase a model writes to justify a fallback and the agreed text's real bound was "stays in scope"; "complete and no larger" as the headline, since "complete" invites handling every case; the shape half of future sight, "shaped so the work already known fits, with nothing built ahead of need", which would restore in principle form the sentence decision 113 cut and so needs its own decision on preference if the owner wants it; and a separate item for the instruction-file sentence, which decision 114 placed beside the patterns clause on purpose. Decision 93 quotes the old guard verbatim; its rationale holds with the new words. The rule count stays 35. Settled 2026-10-25 by the owner. Reopens: a change that builds for an unnamed need under the new words, which would restore the three-item list; or the simplest-to-write reading producing a bespoke patch beside a helper, which would restore "Follow" as its own imperative.
116. **House-rule edit thirteen: a wrong request is said first, then the alternative is done.** The item of requirement 234 read "When the request itself is wrong, that is the first decision to raise: the evidence, and what you would do instead." A two-pass review of the Working section on 2026-10-25, the second pass challenging the first, found the stop not in "raise" but in "decision": the word attaches the item to "A decision reaches my reply only when the next step turns on the answer" and to the decision format, and for a wrong request every step turns on it, so "Do the work that doesn't depend on the answer first" yields no work and "Report once ... when the next step turns on my answer" makes the question a legitimate end of turn. The item now reads "When the request itself is wrong, say so first, with the evidence and what you would do instead, then do that; ask-first rules still hold." It keeps the fix of decision 66, a wrong request is never carried out in silence, drops the word "decision" so nothing attaches, and "then do that" follows the Working items that decide and go on, with the Ask-first section still gating anything hard to undo; the softer "and go on", which does the request as asked after the warning, was offered and not taken. The ground is preference, as for decision 115, framed by decision 66's own reopen clause, "a raised decision that holds up work the request plainly wanted". The owner's counts of assistant records over 14 days outside this repository, run by hand on 2026-10-25, rank the Working lines: the wrong-request item 6 records, the unverified-cause item about 18 that name the check it could not run, the read-only review item about 21, most of them the rule stated in a review the owner asked for; records, not turns, so a ranking and not a measurement. No single Working line carries the turn count the owner reports, so the rest of Working stays: the unverified-cause item does its job and a relief clause would trade it for acting on unverified causes; the read-only review item is agreed text and a stop, which decision 4 leaves to the owner, and the owner's own workflow relies on it; the "my preference" ask ground had no records; the decision format costs length, not turns. A sharper count the review wanted, the same phrases with this repository's sessions excluded, was denied by the permission classifier in the session and run by the owner instead. Settled 2026-10-25 by the owner. Reopens: a case where "then do that" builds the alternative when the owner wanted the literal request, which would make the ending "and go on".
117. **House-rule edit fourteen: the report-once item loses its progress-notes clause.** The item of requirement 20 read "Report once, when the task is done or when the next step turns on my answer; no progress notes unless I ask for them." The owner's review of the instruction files on 2026-10-25 classed the second clause as an update suppressor, a line written against a model that narrated as it worked, and reported the reverse in the field: with it present the model under-reports, while the first clause already says when a report is wanted, at the end of the task or when the next step turns on the answer. The clause goes by the test of decision 113: it restates a bound the same line already holds, and no owner incident names a progress note the first clause let through. The item now reads "Report once, when the task is done or when the next step turns on my answer." The Concise Plain style's Leave out list covers the same ground for replies and is not changed; the two were a working redundancy, not a conflict. The ground is the owner's reading of recent sessions, with no count behind it, so the under-reporting is unverified; the drop rests on the restatement test, which holds without it. The rule count stays 35. Settled 2026-10-25 by the owner. Reopens: progress notes returning to replies in a task the owner did not ask them for, which would restore the clause.
118. **Spike 1: a headless `code-review` returns findings as data, at a cost the gate can carry.** `claude -p "/code-review high main...HEAD" --output-format json` in a throwaway repository with a seeded off-by-one (`sorted[rank]` for a 1-based nearest-rank percentile) ran 130 s and cost $1.14 (29,666 input, 8,228 output, 181,210 cache-read and 31,092 cache-creation tokens); `num_turns` was 0 and the top-level `usage` zeros because the skill runs as a fork, and `modelUsage` carried the tokens. The `result` was text ending in a fenced ```json array of `{file, line, summary, failure_scenario}` objects, eight of them, with repository-relative paths and no category tags; the first was the seeded bug with a reproduced failure scenario. The fork's prompt began "Review target: `main...HEAD`" and "`high effort → 8 inline angles → dedup (no verify) → ≤10 findings`", so the typed level reached the review. `--max-findings 2` in a second run was honored (two findings, 80 s, $0.82) and wrote no `codeReviewLastMaxFindings` key. An `xhigh` run on a ten-line diff took 120 s and cost $1.23 with "10 inline angles → dedup (no verify) → sweep". Three Bash calls outside the allowlist were denied and listed in `permission_denials`; the review finished and reported the checks as not run. The result's shape varied: a bare fenced array in one run, a scope note before the array and "Notes outside scope" after it in others, so `findings.mjs` takes the first fenced array and keeps the rest as notes. The in-session comparison could not run on the same diff, since the skill reviews the session's own repository; the fork's `modelUsage` is what the skill costs in-session, and the child session adds its own turn, measured in item 123 at $0.28 cold and $0.0064 warm with 2.3 to 3.2 s of wall time. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: a host release whose headless `result` loses the fenced array, which `findings.mjs`'s fixture test shows on the first run.
119. **Spike 2: a typed level in a child session leaves the remembered level alone.** `~/.claude.json` `codeReviewLastEffort` read `medium` before the first and after the last of the pass's eight child sessions, which typed `high`, `xhigh` and `ultra`; the bundle names the function that writes it after arguments the user typed, which was read there and not spiked; the before and after reads are the evidence. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: a host release that writes it from `-p`, which acceptance item 51 checks at each gate release.
120. **Spike 3: `/gate` is free, so the plugin and its skill are both `gate`.** `claude -p "/context"` from an empty directory listed no bundled skill named `gate`; with a throwaway plugin whose skill `gate` replies with a sentinel, `/context` listed `gate:gate | Plugin (gate)` and `claude -p "/gate ping" --plugin-dir <path>` returned the sentinel, exit 0, $0.17, one turn, 4.4 s. The skill is therefore `gate`, answering as `/gate` and `/gate:gate`, the form item 2 verified for `rules`; the `run` name and the `/gate:run` form the spec held in reserve are not used. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: a host release that bundles a `gate` skill, which acceptance item 47's reading block shows.
121. **Spike 4: a path target reviews against the default branch; the range form carries the base and a path.** In a clone whose `origin/HEAD` pointed at `feat/percentile`, on a branch `feat/variance` cut from `develop`, `/code-review high src/stats.mjs` reviewed the merge-base diff against `feat/percentile` and reported `develop`'s own commit as part of the change: the host resolves the base with `git symbolic-ref --short refs/remotes/origin/HEAD`, falls back to `main`, and its usage string has no base flag. `/code-review high develop...HEAD` left `develop`'s commit out, and `/code-review high develop...HEAD src/stats.mjs`, with a second changed file holding an inverted `isEven`, reached the fork as "Review target: `develop...HEAD src/stats.mjs`", ran `git diff develop...HEAD -- src/stats.mjs`, reported only that file's findings and named the other file's bug as out of scope. The path restriction is honored by the reviewer from its prompt, not parsed by the host, so the gate types `<base>...HEAD <path>` for a split group and never a bare path. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: a run whose split review reports a finding outside its group, which acceptance item 47 records.
122. **Spike 5: the model cannot launch `ultra`, `max` is unaffordable, and the owner drops both.** On this host the `code-review` description carries no `ultra` clause: the host offers the cloud review only when a server flag is on, the API is first party and the session is not remote, and its own guidance says the review is user-triggered and billed and must not be launched via Bash or otherwise. The skill's fallback builder holds four notices; headless `/code-review ultra develop...HEAD --max-findings 1` here produced "(ultra (cloud review) isn't available in this environment — see [link cut]. Falling back to a local max-effort review.)", forced the level to `max`, ran 17 min 18 s and cost $17.36 on a ten-line diff, and where the cloud review is available and the model invokes the skill the notice reads "Claude can't launch the cloud review directly — type `/code-review ultra` to run it." Availability is therefore detectable before launch only from the skill's description in the session, and a launch by the gate could never run the cloud review. The owner's decision, asked on 2026-10-25 with a handoff as the recommendation: cap the `code-review` level at `xhigh` and drop both `max` and `ultra`. So the gate types `xhigh`, `high` and `medium` only, the loop maps the effort word "max" to `xhigh` for the skill while Agent calls keep `max`, no `ultra` phrase, key or pass exists, and the spec's ultra acceptance item is not written. Settled 2026-10-25 by the owner. Reopens: a host release that lets a session launch the cloud review and read its findings as data, and an owner who wants it.
123. **Spike 6: the Windows child-session cost is not measured here, and the engine does not wait for it.** No Windows machine was available to this pass. On macOS a trivial `claude -p` run took 3.22 s cold ($0.28, 13,900 cache-creation tokens) and 2.34 s warm ($0.0064), and a headless run waits three seconds for stdin unless it is redirected from `/dev/null`; item 105 measured process creation under Git for Windows at about 200 ms per `sh` and 70 ms per `git`. Against reviews of 80 to 150 s at `high` and 120 s at `xhigh` on a ten-line diff, the child's start is under five percent of a pass even at several times the macOS figure, so the engine is settled without the measurement, and acceptance item 51 records the Windows numbers at the first gate release. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: a Windows measurement above 30 s per child session, which would move a split pass to one child for every group.
124. **Engine: a child session per review, with the Agent stand-in behind it.** The spec's recommendation stands on items 118, 119 and 123: findings arrive as a fenced JSON array with paths, the typed level reaches the review and leaves the remembered level alone, and the start cost is small against the review. The in-session candidate was rejected on the spec's own ground, that a level typed through the Skill tool overwrites the user's remembered level, which this pass did not spike and acceptance item 46 checks on the loop's Skill-tool pass, and because the findings would arrive as a turn's text the orchestrator must copy. The child runs with `CLAUDECODE` unset, since a nested launch refuses otherwise, stdin from `/dev/null`, `--permission-mode default`, and an allowlist of Read, Grep, Glob, Skill, Agent, TaskStop, the read-only git forms and the repository's own `checks` commands, so a denied call is recorded from `permission_denials` and never prompts; in the spikes, run under `acceptEdits`, the reviewer's git commands came as compound lines starting with `cd` and were denied against `Bash(git *)`, and `npm --prefix <path> test` was denied against `Bash(npm test*)`, so the skill names the exact forms and the report counts the denials. The review runs in the background under the Bash foreground cap and is waited for; the stand-in is an Agent at `opus` over a patch file with the level as `effort`, chosen once per run when `claude` is not on the PATH or the child reports the skill missing. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: item 118's reopen, or a run where the allowlist's denials cost findings the owner wanted, which widens the list in a decision.
125. **Defaults, each with its ground.** `split` 1500 changed lines: the spec's number; the review budget is what it protects, and the first `xhigh` review over a group that size is the measurement that moves it. `max-passes` 4: the plain sequence of item 130, `xhigh`, `high`, `high`, `medium`, is four passes; the one-time `xhigh` re-review can stretch a run to six, which the cap ends `unconverged` with its open findings listed, and a later run resumes from the recorded level. Budget 120 minutes: four reviews at the 30-minute review budget would exceed it, so a slow run ends on the budget, not the pass cap, with its open findings listed. Per review 30 minutes, per implementer call 20, per check 15: the loop's figures (requirement 67). The skill's own finding cap stands (10 at `high`), since a hidden finding returns in the next pass and the triage cost of `--max-findings all` is not measured. Models: implementer `sonnet` and stand-in `opus`, the loop's. The measured reviews, all on diffs of 7 to 15 lines: `high` 80 to 150 s and $0.82 to $1.14; `xhigh` 120 s and $1.23; `max` 17 minutes and $17.36. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: an `xhigh` review of a group near `split` running over 30 minutes in acceptance item 47, which lowers `split` first.
126. **Identity: file path plus normalized summary, with the orchestrator matching what the script cannot.** The spec's rule is kept and made testable: lowercase, backticks and quotes removed, every run of characters outside `[a-z0-9]` one space, trimmed, cut at 120 characters, the line number left out because a fix moves lines. The evidence that the rule alone is weak: the same `variance` defect was summarized three ways by three `high` runs on this host, "variance has no guard on the n-1 denominator, so single-element input returns NaN and empty input returns -0 instead of a defined value or an error", "variance divides by values.length - 1 with no guard for fewer than two values, returning NaN or -0 instead of a defined result" and "variance divides by values.length - 1 with no length guard, so single-element and empty inputs produce NaN and -0 instead of a defined value or an error", which no normalization of the summary equates. So `findings.mjs` matches by identity and marks the rest `new`, and the orchestrator may match a new finding to a fixed or rejected entry by reading both, recording the match and its reason in the ledger; the suite's fixtures hold the three wordings. Settled 2026-10-25; the owner is told in the pass report that the alternative is file path plus the hunk of the first changed line. Reopens: a run where a carried rejection is re-triaged as new in two passes running, which moves the match to the hunk.
127. **The owner's answers: fix commits on any pull request, the deferred list in the comment only.** Asked on 2026-10-25 with the recommendations, the owner chose both: a gate run on a pull request the loop did not open may commit and push to its branch, since invoking by name is the approval and the house rules' ask-first items still prompt where they apply; and `pass-with-deferred` publishes the deferred list in the comment and the report, never in the body. With no output that edits a body, the body edit the spec listed in the approval scope is not granted, and the loop's `pr` key in `steps.json` is not read. Settled 2026-10-25 by the owner. Reopens: the owner asking for the deferred list in the body, which adds the body edit for loop-opened pull requests only.
128. **The loop's step 5 is one pass with the `code-review` skill, and the higher-risk rule retires.** The spec's change: one pass at the risk-floor level, the three-bucket triage, the fix bucket applied once, no second round, the SendMessage continuation gone. What follows in the texts: the fresh `fable` reviewer reviews the plan only; the higher-risk rule (requirement 70) selected the step 5 role and nothing else, so with one role for every run it has no effect and retires, the risk floor alone setting the level; the round cap (72), re-evaluation (106), checks reuse (113) and repair (127) lose their step 5 rounds; the manifest gains `reviews`, which the audit keeps as given and the gate does not read; and the report's `Gate:` line suggests `/gate` for 500 or more changed lines before the audit. The shipped loop 0.1.5 texts match the documents on step 5, roles and the manifest, so the change is planned against both. A fix at step 5 is no longer seen by a second review pass: the orchestrator reads the fix diff and step 6 runs the checks, and a converged review is the gate's job. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: a field run where an unreviewed step 5 fix shipped a defect the gate would have caught, which adds one `high` pass after a non-empty fix bucket. Amended 2026-11-05: the plan reviewer runs at `opus` (decision 182).
129. **The audit needs no change for the manifest's new keys.** Stage 1 of the shipped audit reads the manifest's named keys and names only `forge_exports` and `_test` as kept; nothing else in the manifest is read and no text makes an unnamed key an error, so `gate` and `reviews` are ignored, and requirement 145 says so; whether the normalized `manifest.json` carries or drops them is not spiked and acceptance item 52 records it. The `gate` key is an object keyed by each bundle's `repo`, since a manifest with several repositories holds one gate record per bundle. No audit version moves for the gate. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: a stage that validates the key list strictly, which adds the two keys to its list and bumps the audit.
130. **The next-level rules read as one sequence.** The spec's first rule, "fix bucket empty: stop", and its fourth, "a `high` pass whose fix bucket was empty but whose reject bucket was not, twice in a row: one `medium` pass", cannot both hold as written, since the first would stop before the fourth could see a second pass. The pass reads the first rule as an empty fix bucket with nothing new rejected, carried rejections counting as nothing, so a `high` pass that still disputes findings runs once more at `high`, then `medium`, then stops, the stop after `medium` coming before the `xhigh` re-review rule so that nothing follows a `medium` pass; the plain sequence is `xhigh`, `high`, `high`, `medium`, four passes, the `max-passes` default. After the `medium` pass the verdict is `pass` or `pass-with-deferred` when its fix bucket was empty and `unconverged` otherwise, since no pass verified its fixes. "When the defer bucket is not empty" is read as any pass of the run having deferred something. The spec's four-pass acceptance item is not written as such: a planted false positive returns carried, so no fixture yields a new rejection in two passes running, and no fixture makes a run take four passes on purpose; item 47 proves convergence in two passes, item 49 the cap, and `next.mjs`'s suite the four-pass sequence. Settled 2026-10-25 and marked for the owner in the pass report. Reopens: the owner reading the first rule literally, which drops the `medium` rule and ends every run on the first empty fix bucket.
131. **"no fixes" runs one pass.** Without fixes a second pass would return the first pass's findings, so the mode runs pass 1 at `xhigh`, triages, and ends `pass` or `pass-with-deferred` on an empty fix bucket and `unconverged` otherwise, naming the fix bucket as what a fix run would do. Settled 2026-10-25. Reopens: the owner wanting the levels sampled without fixes.
133. **Eval cases: three, apart from the trigger suite, with the runner's grants, settled by their first run.** The spec named four cases under `tests/evals/gate/`; the `medium` case is dropped for item 130's reason and the rule lives in `next.mjs`'s suite. `claude plugin eval --help` on 2.1.310 gates Bash, Write and Edit behind `--allow-tools`, runs scaffold scripts only under `--scaffold`, confines shell tools to an OS sandbox, and runs each case as a full child on the user's credential; a gate run fetches, commits, pushes and starts its own child session, none of which the trigger suite's fixed command of requirement 273 allows, and a case under that command would also gate this repository's own branch. So the cases live under `tests/gate/evals/` with their own command, `npm run eval:gate`, a scaffold script that builds the fixture with a bare `origin` remote and writes a manifest, and a prompt that gates that manifest. Whether the sandbox lets the case's child session commit and push to that remote was not spiked; the work on gate 0.1.0 runs the `converges` case first and alone, ships the three cases when it passes, and otherwise drops them, records the refusal here, and keeps items 47, 49 and 54 as the hand-run cover. The first run, 2026-10-26 on Windows with 2.1.310, the case's scaffold having built the fixture, was refused before any model call and cost nothing: "A shell tool (Bash or PowerShell) was granted but this machine cannot confine it (no sandbox backend on this platform, or it is not installed), so the run was refused rather than run unconfined", with "sandbox required but unavailable: sandbox is enabled but the Windows sandbox is not active on this session (feature gate off)". The runner's documentation says native Windows has no sandbox backend and shell-granting suites run under WSL2, and that under the sandbox the home directory and the host's configuration are unreadable and the network reaches only granted domains; whether a sandboxed host lets the case's child session reach its credential, commit and push stays untested. The three cases are dropped, `eval:gate` is not added, and items 47, 49 and 54 stand for them. Settled 2026-10-26. Reopens: a run of the cases under WSL2, macOS or Linux with the sandbox backend installed.
134. **The gate's level has a floor of `medium` and a ceiling of `xhigh`.** The owner's decision, stated 2026-10-26 after the pass: the gate's levels are capped at `xhigh` above and `medium` below, in line with the loop's `xhigh` cap of decision 122 and with how the owner works, who never uses `low` or `max` and sees no case for `ultra`. The gate's next-level rules already produced only `xhigh`, `high` and `medium`, so requirement 298 now states the floor and the ceiling as the rule and `next.mjs` rejects any other word. The loop is left as decision 122 set it: requirement 285 still types the effort word's level in force, `low` included, capped at `xhigh`, and the owner asked for the loop to stay untouched. Settled 2026-10-26 by the owner. Reopens: the owner wanting the loop's `code-review` level floored at `medium` too, which amends requirement 285 and the mapping in `risk.md`.
136. **A trailer the host adds stays.** Gate 0.1.1 gave Step 2 the loop's "Type no attribution trailer." Acceptance on 2026-10-26 showed what the bare line does: before it, three of six gate runs on profiles that set no attribution dropped the host's trailer, citing the commit rule that names no model, and three kept it; after it, the one run, item 47 at gate 0.1.1, dropped it. Requirement 11 leaves the trailer to the host's setting, so the line overrode the user's choice. The owner's decision, stated 2026-10-26: the gate's commit step, the loop's publish step and the loop's pull request text type no trailer of their own and keep one the host's attribution setting adds, even where it names a model or tool; a user who wants none turns the setting off. Requirement 11 says so for every plugin, and gate 0.1.2 and loop 0.1.7 ship it. Settled 2026-10-26 by the owner. Reopens: wanting the suite to remove the host's trailer whatever the setting says, or a run that still drops it under this wording.
137. **A push to `main` keeps its run.** The workflow's concurrency group cancelled a run in progress whenever a newer push reached the same ref, `main` included, so a merge commit pushed just before its changelog heading lost its run: item 21 of 2026-10-24 and of 2026-10-25 records merge-commit runs cancelled that way, and a tagged commit could be left with no green run of its own (issue 1, item 2). `cancel-in-progress` now holds only for pull requests. A later push to `main` still replaces a run that is waiting to start, so three pushes in quick succession can leave the middle one without a run. Settled 2026-10-27 by the owner. Reopens: runs queued on `main` that hold up pull request feedback.
138. **The fixture's diff holds eight or more files, and its check visits every slot.** The gate's acceptance runs recorded items 47, 48, 49 and 54 as passed in part for two reasons in the fixture. Every fix of the seeded bug changed two files of a two- to four-file diff, which counts as over a quarter, so pass 2 ran at `xhigh`. The seeded `every()` check skipped sparse holes, which drew a second fix and follow-on findings. The owner first kept the fixture as it was, then asked for the two to be addressed. The feature branch of every variant but `split` now also adds a README, a changelog, a page under `docs/`, an index module with its test, and a version in `package.json`, so its diff holds 8 or 10 files and a two-file fix is not over a quarter; the check loops over every index. `split` keeps its three files, since its item checks only the split. Settled 2026-10-27 by the owner. Reopens: a fix of the seeded bug that changes more than a quarter of the files, or new rejections in pass 2 that come from the added files.
139. **The chain backlog: two acceptance runs now, three findings later, one never.** A backlog review on 2026-10-27 made five findings against the loop, gate and audit chain, and a grooming pass the same day settled each one from the documents, without a run. (1) Rule 3 of requirement 301, `over-quarter`, waits: decision 138 widened the fixture, and the runs of 2026-10-27 show pass 2 at `high` in every item. It reopens on a real small pull request where the `xhigh` second pass costs enough to matter. (2) One record location across the three plugins waits: the audit's read-only boundary and decision 6 keep the audit from writing `.git/info/exclude`, and after a loop or gate run `.reimagine/` is already ignored, so the audit already picks it. It reopens with decision 6's own condition, a host feature that gives plugins a per-repository ignored directory, or an audit run after a loop or gate run that still falls back to the plugin data directory. (3) A shared identity for a change across the phases waits, and with (2) waiting it is not needed: the manifest is the record that carries a change from phase to phase, and the gap is the head sha and the date in the `gate` key of requirement 315, a key the audit ignores (decision 129). It reopens with decision 129's own condition, a stage that reads the `gate` key, or a gate record that has to be matched to a head other than the one it reviewed. (4) The two open acceptance runs are made now. Item 46 was recorded "not met" on 2026-10-26 because a "do not publish" run makes no commit, so `audit-manifest.json` and its `reviews` key were never seen in a real handoff (requirements 120 and 141); its two commands now publish, and item 9 keeps its own, since it predates the key. Item 51 passed at gate 0.1.1 on 2026-10-26 and asks for a run at each gate release, and gate 0.1.2 has none. It reopens on one of the two runs failing an expectation, which is then a finding of its own. (5) The audit's cost on a one-ticket bundle gets no change: requirement 144 already defaults the tier to `low` for one bundle, one ticket and under 500 changed lines. It reopens on a field run at that tier whose cost is out of line with the change, which would amend requirement 144's tiers rather than add a mechanism. Settled 2026-10-27. Reopens: as each verdict says.
140. **The CI poll waits with `sleep 30` in the background.** Step 7.3 waited between polls with `sleep 30` in its own Bash call, and the host blocks that call with "Blocked: standalone sleep 30. To wait for a condition, use Monitor with an until-loop (e.g. `until <check>; do sleep 2; done`). To wait for a command you started, use run_in_background: true. Do not chain shorter sleeps to work around this block." Item 40's run on Claude Code 2.1.310 met the block and then waited in a foreground until-loop of `sleep 5` on `date`. Item 46's second run on 2.1.311 met it too and waited with a background until-loop on `date`. Both forms fall outside the commands the skill pre-approves, and the first is the chain of shorter sleeps the host rules out. On 2026-10-27 a headless probe on 2.1.311, with only `Bash(sleep *)` and `Bash(date *)` allowed, had its foreground `sleep 30` blocked. The same command with `run_in_background: true` ran without a denial, and the session resumed when it finished. So the poll keeps `sleep 30`, the command requirement 59 pre-approves, and runs it in the background; requirement 126 says so, and loop 0.1.8 ships it. A Monitor with an until-loop was not chosen: the poll waits on the clock, not on a condition, and an until-loop is not a pre-approved command. Settled 2026-10-27 at the owner's request. Reopens: a host release that blocks a background `sleep`, or a run that still waits some other way under this wording.
141. **House-rule edit fifteen and Concise Plain 4.8: process detail leaves published text, leftovers are listed as they are made, one symptom per issue.** Three pieces of field feedback of 2026-10-27, read in a two-pass review the same day, the second pass challenging the first. First, a pull request description written under the style after several code reviews said how many rounds ran, at which levels, with how many findings of each kind, and a commit subject named the review round. The style's "How you got there" principle held for replies and lost in the draft to the task's own vocabulary, and the style excludes commit messages on purpose. The style now names the categories the principle covers, passes, levels, verdicts and finding counts, and never the host's level words or skill names, so the text stays general; the Drafts scan step, which already works for bare ids because it is a step and not a principle, scans for the same categories and replaces each with the activity and date or drops it; and the commit rule in `core.md` says the subject and body are about the change, never about the session that made it, since the style's scope line stays as it is. The loop's publication texts already banned a model, an agent type, a tool and a run-internal path for every published text, so the feedback's claim that the body template lacked the line was wrong at source; the gap was rounds and levels, and `pr-body.md` and Step 7.1 now name them. Second, long sessions leave scratch files, scripts, worktrees and branches behind, and the offer to clean up fades late in a session; the failure is recall at the end, so the scratch item records each leftover in one list as it is made, and when done says which are safe to delete and why, and nothing more, so the owner is not read a report to delete files; the deletion itself waits on a yes. That ask stays inside the Working item rather than extending the ask-first item on deletion, which requirement 21 keeps unchanged. Third, an analysis of a bug backlog, taken as de-identified, proposed four house rules and a style line on what a defect ticket is; the generic and cheap part is kept, one symptom per issue with a side finding in its own linked issue, on the item about opening an issue, and the style's verdict-first line says a defect report opens with the wrong outcome, where it was seen, then the cause. Not taken, as team norms that would narrow the findings item: a ticket only for an outcome a run showed, severity bands, and a filing rule against another team's backlog; the ask-first rule on a new issue already makes each ticket the owner's call. The rule count stays 35; the chat block mirrors the three style changes. Settled 2026-10-27 by the owner. Reopens: a published text that still carries a pass count or level under the categories, which would name the host's words after all; or a session that deletes a leftover without the yes, which would move the ask to the ask-first section with requirement 21 amended.
142. **House-rule edit sixteen: a scratch file that existed before the session is changed, not made.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28, read in a two-pass review the same day. A session had rewritten a setup file that existed before it started and logged the rewrite; after a summary dropped that word, the session listed the file as safe to delete and withdrew the offer in a later reply. The same session listed its scratch logs as globs that also matched an earlier session's files. The scratch item said "you make", which was read as "you touched". The feedback proposed a five-rule section for the loop's and the gate's `record.md`, with a pointer from each report. Taken, as three phrases in the Working item: the list records each entry as it is made or first changed, by exact name, noting which existed already; the offer comes from that list and the disk, and names only what the session made. Not taken: a copy in each `record.md`, since the house-rules block loads in every session, the loop's and the gate's included, and a second copy duplicates it; the recheck as a sentence of its own, since the Done item already counts a claim about state only when the thing itself was checked; and a rule to prefer a run-owned copy over rewriting a shared file, which is new behavior whose general form collides with ordinary work, and which a session can choose for itself once it has noted that the file existed. The list stays one list rather than a file. The ask stays inside the Working item, as decision 141 settled; its reopen clause, a deletion without the yes, was not met. The rule count stays 35. Settled 2026-10-28 by the owner. Reopens: a session that offers a pre-existing file for deletion under this wording, which would make the list a file in the scratch directory; or a session that rewrites a shared setup file, which would add the run-owned copy.
143. **The gate's identity stays file plus normalized summary; a deferral carries and a new finding names its candidates.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on gate 0.1.2, verified on 2026-10-29 against the shipped texts on `main`, by reading and by a run: `findings.mjs`, given a ledger with one `rejected` entry on a file and a pass 2 review whose finding is the same defect on that file reworded, printed the finding `state: new`, `bucket: null`, with nothing pointing at the rejected entry. A deferred finding that returns had no carry at all, so it arrived `new` every pass and was triaged again, and a missed match on a rejected one counts as a new rejection, which keeps the run going under the `rejects-at-high` rule. Decision 126's reopen clause, a carried rejection re-triaged as new in two passes running, was met in the field. Not taken: the hunk identity that decision named, since `findings.mjs` reads review outputs and has no diff, and a hunk identity would change every ledger id, so no record kept so far would carry. Taken: `reseen` carries a `deferred` entry with the bucket `defer` and the reason of its last defer pass, its state unchanged; and every `new` finding of the printed summary lists `candidates`, the same file's entries in a fixed, rejected, carried, deferred or regressed state with their latest line and the line distance, nearest first, so the orchestrator still matches but reads a short list instead of the whole ledger (requirements 302 and 306). The child session's prompt stays as it is: requirement 305 pins it to the skill line, and text after the range would be read as a target. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a run where a candidate list is present and the orchestrator still misses the match twice, which moves the identity to the hunk after all.
144. **The gate's checks come from the request, the manifest bundle or the configuration, and the reviewer's notes go into the record.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading: the only source of the fix round's checks was `.reimagine.json` `checks`, so a user who cannot add that file to a repository got "no check ran" in the report, while the child reviewer's own test run was denied by the allowlist and the fix round ran nothing. The request phrase "checks <command>[; <command>]", which runs to the next comma that another recognized phrase follows, else to the end of the request, since a command may hold a comma, and the manifest bundle key `checks`, an array of command strings, now come first, in the order request, bundle, configuration; the one list feeds the fix round, the child's `Bash(<check>*)` entries, the permission statement and the report, which names the source (requirement 300). The text the reviewer writes before its fence is where it says what it could not run; `findings.mjs` already printed it as `notes.before`, and it now goes into each `reviews[]` entry and the report's Log (requirements 311 and 317). Not taken: telling the reviewer what it may not run, since the allowlist already denies without a prompt and the denials are counted. `next.mjs` does not read `reviews[]`, and the loop neither reads nor writes the bundle key; the audit ignores it as it ignores `gate` (decision 129, requirement 145). Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a run whose child session needs a command the fix round does not run, which would split the reviewer's allowlist from the fix round's list.
145. **The gate splits at the shallowest depth that gives more than one group.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading: Step 1.2.3 grouped by the first path segment, so a diff whose files all sit under one folder, as in a repository that keeps its code under `src/` or one package directory, formed one group however large, and "a group alone over `split` is still one review" sent the whole change to one review under its 30-minute budget. The grouping depth now starts at 1 and grows while every file has the same key, a file's key being its first `d` segments when it has more, else `.`; the slugs carry the shared prefix, and the `.` key at depth 2 or more is `<prefix>-root` (requirement 297). A worked example in Step 1.2 shows a diff under one folder splitting at depth 2. Not taken: a `groups.mjs` script, since the grouping is a short rule that a worked example makes exact, and a script brings a suite the rule does not need; nor a split of a group still over `split` after grouping, which would review part of a directory without the rest of it. The `split` fixture variant is unchanged, since its files sit under two top-level folders and depth 1 already gives two groups. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a run that groups a diff differently from the rule, which would move the rule into a script with its suite.
146. **A new local branch is judged, not blocked, and resume keeps a finished scope's output when its stage never closed.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on audit 0.1.7, verified on 2026-10-29 against the shipped texts on `main`, by reading and by a run: after `git branch new-x`, `git config branch.new-x.remote origin` and a `git worktree add`, `readonly.sh check` printed `blocked config + branch.new-x.merge=...`, `blocked config + branch.new-x.remote=origin` and `blocked refs + <sha> commit refs/heads/new-x` and exited 1, while the worktree itself left no line. Refs and config are shared by every worktree of a repository, so a user who makes a branch in another worktree during a run ended it `blocked` and lost every unfinished stage, though the audited checkout's HEAD, index and tree were unchanged. A refs line present only now, for `refs/heads/<name>` other than the checkout's own branch, and a `+` config line `branch.<name>.<key>=` for such a name, now print as `local-branch` and exit 3; the orchestrator asks once per branch and commit, as it asks for a moved remote-tracking ref, and records the yes as a `local-branch` approval (requirements 149, 178 and 179). A deleted or moved existing branch, any other config line, and the index and the tree stay `blocked`, since those change what the audit reads or what the user's branches hold. Separately, the stage 4 and 5 entries are written only after the stage's check passes, so a block at that check reran the whole stage on resume and superseded every finished pass-one and pass-two file; resume now keeps a file whose last line is `status: complete` and whose `consumed:` hashes still hold, when the stage's own inputs have not changed, and relaunches only the scopes without one (requirement 161). Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a run where a branch made outside the run changes what an agent read, which would make a new branch blocked again; or a kept file that a rerun of its scope would have changed, which would drop the keep.
147. **The report's copied sections come from `report.sh`.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading: stage 8 wrote the whole body and `claims-verdicts.md` with the Write tool, so at high tier with a long prose claims file the model emitted every claim line twice, once in section 10 and once in the verdicts file, and the body reached hundreds of kilobytes. Decision 112 made the Write tool the writer because a body carried in a shell heredoc passed the process argument cap; a script that reads the run directory's files passes nothing through an argument, so this keeps decision 112's reason and replaces its mechanism for the sections that are copies. `report.sh parts` writes section 8's decision ledger and raised tickets, section 10 and the verdicts body from the files whose line forms `common.md` and `stages/1-orient.md` fix; the orchestrator writes the judgment sections as part files with the Write tool, plus three short lists the script needs and no file holds in a fixed form: the claims files with their recorded hashes, the ids each `C<n>` absorbs, and the verdicts it decides itself; `assemble` joins the parts and `claims-verdicts` writes the verdicts file with the revision (requirements 160 and 322). Not taken: section 3, because the pass-two line that marks a Verified OK item challenged has no fixed form, and section 9, because a Live checks status joins the approvals in `stages.json`, the gate reasons and the result copies; both stay the orchestrator's parts. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a fixed form for the challenged line or the live status, which would move that section into the script; or a run where the script's sections and the orchestrator's disagree on a claim, which would move the override list into the script's own reading.
148. **The agent definitions restate the hard rules, act shows a defect before it edits, and claims that name no file spread across a bundle's groups.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on audit 0.1.7, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading. First, hard rules 2 and 4 lived only in `common.md`, and in a long run agents called a second-opinion tool the host offered, ran `mkdir -p`, ran an interpreter with a heredoc that never closed and left a shell waiting on input, wrote outside their scratch folder, and one rewrote its finished output after its stage had completed, which changes a recorded input of the report. Each agent definition now restates the rules in its own words after its opening paragraph, `common.md` rule 2 allows `mkdir -p` of the agent's own scratch folder and bars an inline program, a heredoc and a command that waits on input, an output is final at `status: complete`, and a late completion notice changes nothing (requirements 162 and 180). Second, act edited, staged and only then reran the checks, so an approved item whose `verified fact` came from reading reached a commit on a premise no run had tested; act now runs a covering check or the finding's described test at baseline when it can show the defect within the check budget, stops on a result that contradicts the premise, and carries both results into the commit body (requirement 194). Third, stage 1 sent every claim that names no file to its ticket's group, else the first group holding a file of its bundle, so a prose claims file put most of its claims on one auditor, whose run bounded the whole stage; such claims now go in turn across the bundle's groups, and a cap of 80 per group moves the rest on, recorded in the brief's Claims section, which already counts claims per target (requirement 165). Not taken: a mechanical block on an agent's Bash, which the host does not offer per agent, so detection stays `readonly.sh`'s; and a demonstration for an item whose evidence is already a run. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: an agent that breaks a restated rule, which would move the rule into a hook; a demonstration that costs more than the check budget on a real run, which would make it opt-in; or a group still over the cap after the moves, which would split its claims into their own scope.
149. **An implementer call is judged by its completion notification's duration, in the loop and in the gate.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on loop 0.1.9 and gate 0.1.3, verified on 2026-10-29 against the shipped texts on `main` by reading. The loop's Budgets and rounds and Step 4.2, and the gate's fix round, timed each Agent call by `date` before and after it. A parallel batch is sent in one message, so it has one before-time for every call: in the field run the twenty briefs of one batch took eight minutes to write, and those minutes counted against each call's 20-minute budget. The host also wakes an implementer again when a background command it started finishes, and reports a cumulative duration for a call that did no new work. The audit already records `duration_ms` from the completion notification and treats a second notification for a finished agent as nothing new (requirement 180). For Agent and SendMessage calls, the subagent budget is now judged on the notification's `duration_ms`, with `date` before and after the call only when the notification carries none. A notification for a call the orchestrator did not send, after that call already returned, is not a new call; it changes nothing unless the snapshot comparison finds a file changed after the first return. The brief tells the implementer to start no background command. `date` still brackets the batch for `run.md` and the gate's Log (requirements 67, 71 and 300). Not taken: continuing a call that passed its budget, which would reverse the rule that such a call ends the run `blocked`. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a notification that carries no `duration_ms` in a field run, which would move the measure back to `date` per call; or an implementer that still starts a background command under this brief. Amended 2026-10-29 after a review of the branch: no snapshot existed at a call's first return, so "a file changed after the first return" could not be judged. Each implementer call now writes `post-<n>` files, its checkout's status and hashes, when it returns, and a late notification ends the run `blocked` only for a path that differs from them and that no later call, edit or commit of the run changed (requirements 67 and 300).
150. **The gate groups a group still over `split` again, one segment deeper.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on gate 0.1.3, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading. Decision 145 moved the split to the shallowest depth that gives more than one group and kept "a group that alone is over `split` is still one review". In the field run a 115-file diff grouped at depth 1 into 108, 6 and 1 files, about 5,300 of its 5,500 changed lines in one group, so the split added two reviews without shrinking the largest, which took 17 of its 30 minutes. A group still over `split` whose key is a directory is now grouped again by the same rule over its own files, starting one segment deeper, until no group is over `split` or the one over it is the `.` key or a single file (requirement 297). Decision 145 did not take this, since it reviews part of a directory without the rest of it. That cost is the one the first split already pays between top-level folders: every review still sees whole subtrees, the `.` key's review sees the files that sit directly in the directory, and the size that made the group too large for one review within its budget is the reason to split at all. Slugs follow the final keys, and the `.` keys of several prefixes come after every directory key in byte order of their prefixes. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a defect that spans two sibling subdirectories and that the split reviews miss while an unsplit review finds it. Amended 2026-10-29 after a review of the branch: regrouping a directory whose files all sit directly in it gave one `.` group of the same size under a new slug and target, so such a regrouping now leaves the group as it was.
151. **The gate's call budgets come from the configuration, with the loop's `timeouts` as the fallback.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on gate 0.1.3, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading: SKILL.md fixed each check at 15 minutes and the configuration had no key for it, so a repository whose suite takes longer, where the loop runs the same command under `timeouts.check`, could not complete a gate fix round. The `gate` key now takes `timeouts` with `subagent`, `review` and `check`. A key absent there takes the root `timeouts` value of the same name, then the default, so a repository that set the loop's budget once has the gate follow it, and `gate.timeouts` can differ when the two need to. The reading block's Budget line prints the three in force (requirements 291, 303 and 313). Not taken: `ci` and `run`, since the gate watches no CI and its run budget is `gate.budget`. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a repository that needs the loop's and the gate's figures apart and finds the fallback in the way, which would drop it.
152. **A slice's file hashes are compared with the tip through the checkout's filters.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on loop 0.1.9, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading and by a run. `record.md` stored each uncommitted slice file's `git -C <checkout> hash-object -- <path>` and compared it with `git rev-parse <tip>:<path>`. With `core.autocrlf=true` the two never agree for a file whose index and working copies differ in line endings. For a file kept as CRLF in the index, `hash-object --` hashes the LF form, and only `--no-filters` matches the tip. For an LF file with a CRLF working copy the opposite holds, so no single form of `hash-object` predicts the blob. In a scratch run on 2026-10-29 the tip blob was `c30dea8a`, `hash-object --` gave `422c2b7a` and `--no-filters` gave `c30dea8a`; for the LF file the tip was `422c2b7a` and `--no-filters` gave `c30dea8a`. The pair Step 0.3 already uses for a flagged path matched the tip in both cases: `git cat-file --filters <tip>:<path> | git hash-object --stdin --no-filters` against `git hash-object --no-filters <path>`. The record now stores the second and compares it with the first, which also makes item 3 agree with item 4's hash rule (requirement 89). Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a filter other than line endings, such as a smudge filter, whose output the working copy no longer holds. Amended 2026-10-29 after a review of the branch, checked by a run: the filtered pair alone missed the opposite case, an LF working copy committed under `core.autocrlf=true`, whose raw hash `422c2b7a` equals the committed blob while the filtered tip gave `c30dea8a`; and a path absent at the tip piped nothing into `hash-object`, giving the empty blob `e69de29b`, which matched an empty file the slice created. A path now counts as held when `git cat-file -e <tip>:<path>` succeeds and the recorded hash equals the committed blob or the filtered one.
153. **The gate names a branch target's repository with "repo <path>".** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on gate 0.1.3, verified on 2026-10-29 by reading: the recognized phrases had no way to name another checkout for "this branch", and the host returns the shell to the session's start folder after a call that ends outside it, so a `cd <path>` in one call did not carry and the gate resolved "this branch" against the session's checkout. Only a manifest bundle had a `repo`. The phrase "repo <path>" now names it, checked as the loop checks "primary <path>", with every command in the forms a manifest bundle uses (requirement 323). The word is "repo", not the loop's "primary", because a gate target has one repository and nothing beside it to be primary over. Not taken: a pull request target in another repository, which requirement 290 keeps to the session's, since its number resolves against the session's remote. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a request for a pull request in another repository, which would widen the phrase to pull request targets. Amended 2026-10-29 after a review of the branch, checked by a run: comparing `--show-toplevel` with the typed path as strings failed a lowercase, `~` or `/c/...` spelling of a valid toplevel, while `git -C <path> rev-parse --show-prefix` printed an empty line for each of them, a subdirectory printed its prefix and a folder outside any repository exited 128; the check now uses it. `git -C <path> rev-parse --git-path info/exclude` printed `.git/info/exclude`, relative to `<path>`, so the exclude file is now named with `--path-format=absolute`. A second review the same day found that a `.git` folder also printed an empty prefix, with `--is-inside-work-tree` printing `false` there and `true` in a toplevel, a subdirectory and a linked worktree, so the check reads both; that `--path-format=absolute` needs git 2.31, so the printed exclude path is read relative to the repository's folder instead; and that the checks, `.reimagine.json` and the repository's instruction files were still read in the session's folder, so they now come from `<path>` too. A third review found that the `~` spelling resolved only unquoted: `git -C "~/<dir>"` exited 128 with "cannot change to", while `git -C "$HOME/<dir>"` printed `true` and an empty line, so a leading `~` is written as `$HOME`.
154. **A match is written with its bucket, and `findings.mjs` warns about a match written without its link.** Field feedback of 2026-10-28 on gate 0.1.3 (issue 43), seen in runs of acceptance items 55 and 57 on 2026-10-29: the triage gave a reworded rejection `reject` and `carried` with the reason "same defect as the pass-1 rejection", but `findings.json` and `passes.json` held it under its new id with no `match`, so the ledger kept two entries for one defect. Step 1.6.2 asked for the match and Step 1.6.3 then wrote the bucket and state, so the bucket was written and the match was not. Step 1.6.3 now writes the match in the same write as the bucket and state. When `findings.mjs` ingests the next pass, it prints one warning line on stderr for each entry new in the latest pass the ledger holds that is `carried` or `regressed` with no `match`, and exits 0 (requirements 302 and 306). The field feedback asked for `deferred` too. Not taken: a new finding the triage defers holds `deferred` with no match by right, so that check would warn on every ordinary deferral, while only a match makes a new finding `carried` or `regressed`. Not taken either: failing the run on the warning, since the counts the report gives stay right and the warning names what to repair. Settled 2026-10-29. Reopens: a run that still records a match without its link under this wording, which would move the match into the script's own write. Amended 2026-10-29 after a review of the branch: the warning fired only when the next pass was read, so a match left without its link in the last pass of a run was never named, and its repair left the finding under the new id in `passes.json`. `findings.mjs --check` now runs after each triage write and the repair moves the `passes.json` key too (requirement 306). A third review found that `--check` took the highest pass the ledger holds, which after a pass with no findings is the one before, so it warned again about an entry that pass's repair had left; it now takes the pass number. A scratch run showed the repeat warning before the change and none after.
155. **A headless session need not print the reading block or the permission statement.** Issues 42, 49 and 50: in non-interactive runs of gate 0.1.3 and 0.1.4, loop 0.1.10 and audit 0.1.9 on 2026-10-29, the sessions printed no reading block and no permission statement, though each skill asks for them before any other work, and the loop wrote its statement to `run.md` only. On 2026-10-30 gate 0.1.3 ran on the `converges` fixture in the owner's profile, with the same output style and permission mode each time: an interactive session printed both before the first fetch, and a headless one with no cost cap printed neither, its first text coming after preflight had fetched and written the record. The two runs differed only in the session mode. Across the seven headless runs of gate 0.1.2 and 0.1.3, these and the five of pull request 46, the reading block printed once, in a 0.1.2 run, and the permission statement never, so a headless session skips them and the text is not the cause. The plugins are left as they are: an interactive session, where a person reads the statement before the run acts, prints it, and the record holds what each run did. Not taken: writing the reading block and the statement to each plugin's record as well, which would change three plugins and their requirements for runs whose owner already reads the record. Settled 2026-10-30. Reopens: an interactive session that skips them, or a need to audit a headless run's predicted prompts from its record.
156. **Concise Plain 4.9: a draft is written for the person who will use it, and a revision comes back whole in the register last accepted.** Field feedback compiled 2026-10-29 read every correction of a draft meant for someone else in a two-pass review on 2026-10-30, the second pass challenging the first. The rate of corrections per draft was flat across rules 0.1.1 to 0.1.12, so no version made it worse; what recurred were the reasons. Two lines in the Drafts section take them. First, the reader bullet's test "technical detail only when they need it" failed repeatedly: the ask named a reader who would not use them and the draft still carried ticket ids, build numbers and test counts, because "need" leaves the model to decide that such a reader needs a build number. The bullet now says the effect, the next step and who it waits on, and admits an id, count or internal name only when that person will use it, called what they call it, never a label this session coined; the last clause takes a one-sighting case where a draft named a task by the shorthand the session had given it. Second, a new bullet after the sentence-length line: a revised draft comes back whole, what changed in one line above it, in the register and detail of the version the owner last accepted, and a cut takes filler, restated detail and internal mechanics before the facts the reader acts on and the sentence that gives them context. Behind it are several chains, under three rules versions, where an ask to shorten removed the fact the message existed to convey and the redraft swung back and forth, two turns in one session where a reply after an edit showed only the changed line and the owner asked for the draft again, and one where an edit after a register change came back in the first draft's voice; the style's "fewest lines" and "no recaps" produce the delta-only reply unless the word "whole" is explicit. The feedback's most frequent correction, "less verbose", is the failure decision 113's reopen clause names for the cut chat-reply length sentence; it is not restored, since the feedback's own table shows the same rate while the sentence was present, so the sentence did not prevent it and the clause does not bind. The shortening half of the new bullet is what that correction gets. Not taken: a default size for a draft and a register per job title, which the reader bullet states as a principle; and a line asking the owner to paste the thread's last messages, a procedure asking for input. The chat block carries the two lines word for word, so its list of deliberate differences does not change (decision 111). The rule count stays 35. Settled 2026-10-30 by the owner. Reopens: a first draft that still carries an id or count its reader will not use, which would make "will use" no better a test than "need"; or a revision that still comes back as a delta, which would move the whole-draft line into the style's opening paragraph.
157. **The record states the failure and the fix, not where or for whom a session ran.** A review on 2026-10-30 of the design documents and the changelog, the second pass challenging the first, found entries that located the sessions behind field feedback: a reader's role in the changelog line for rules 0.1.13 and in decision 156, words quoted from other people and from drafts and a board column name in decision 111, an operating-system edition, a host and a test stack in decision 112, a host and a team in decision 141, and the phrase for the owner's second machine in requirement 1 and decision 58. None of it carries an argument: each decision rests on the failure and the fix, and decision 1 already enters lessons as rules with no source line. Each is rewritten to the kind of failure or the neutral word; a count stays only where an argument turns on it, as decision 116's ranking of the Working lines and decision 156's rate comparison do. The hub's constraint had dropped "acceptance" from decision 1's "records an acceptance run made elsewhere", which read every field-feedback entry as a violation; the word is restored and a sentence follows it: a changelog or decision entry records the failure and the fix, and nothing in it says where, for whom or with whom a session ran. The rules-feedback skill carries the same principle. Lint's owner-specific rule reads the shipped files only (decision 63), so the sentence is held by review and by the owner's private deny tokens, which found every hit above in one run. Requirement 44 said the chat block names no vendor or product while the block has carried the two Links bullets since decision 68; the requirement now says no chat vendor or product, and the skill's principle says no repository, team or session, since the bullets are the link forms of two public hosts and name no one's organization. The same sentence stays in the public body of the pull request that shipped rules 0.1.13 and in history; the body is edited and history is left, since a rewrite of `main` draws more attention than the phrase. Settled 2026-10-30 by the owner. Reopens: a shipped or design file that locates a session in a form the deny tokens miss, which would add the form to lint's owner-specific rule.
158. **House-rule edit seventeen and Concise Plain 4.10: the decision item loses its shape words, and two Drafts bullets go.** A two-pass review on 2026-10-30 of the rules for prescription, the second pass challenging the first, on the owner's stated ground that the rules should read as principles as models take on longer autonomous work. Three cuts. The decision item of requirement 233 drops "the question in one sentence", "what it blocks" and "one numbered message": decision 66 gave reasoning for the shared assumption and named no incident for the shape, decision 113's classification of it as failure-backed cites none, and decision 116 found the format costs length, not turns; what stays is the shared assumption, what each option does, the recommendation first and what would overturn it, and open decisions raised together unless one depends on another. The style's verdict-first bullet goes: its first sentence restates "Lead with what the reader needs", and its defect-report sentence came from the backlog analysis of decision 141 with no incident of the owner's and no reopen clause naming it. The Drafts scan step goes, its first sentence kept as the principle that the Content and Links rules hold inside a draft shown for pasting, with the bare-id exception: decision 113 called the step's effect unverified and decision 141 asserted it worked with no new data between, so the step rests on no recorded failure either; decision 111's reopen clause now reads on the principle sentence. Left as they are, each with a dated failure or a mechanism the general rules do not imply: the scratch item (decisions 141 and 142; a principle wording, "record what you leave behind as you go, separating what you made from what you changed, and at the end offer only what you made for deletion", drops "by exact name" and with it the glob failure of 142, and waits for a trial), the unverified-cause item (decision 116, five days old), the exit-code sentence, the finding-shape bullet, and the commit stop, which requirement 21 keeps and whose approval already covers every commit in a task. The chat block mirrors the two style cuts and already carries the recommendation line, so its list of deliberate differences does not change. The rule count stays 35. Settled 2026-10-30 by the owner. Reopens: a decision raised without its options or its overturn condition, which would restore the shape words; a bare id or a pass count in a shown draft, which would restore the scan step; a defect report that opens with the cause, which would restore that sentence. Numbering returns, in the style and not in the decision item, by decision 327.
159. **House-rule edit eighteen: prose written for an agent states principles, and adds a procedure or an output shape only for a failure.** A two-pass review on 2026-10-31, the second pass challenging the first, on the owner's stated ground of decision 158 that the rules should read as principles as models take on longer autonomous work. The failure is the prescriptive residue decisions 113, 115 and 158 found three times in the house rules: a procedure or an output shape with no failure behind it, kept as length that fixed nothing, as the three-item list of decision 115 and the shape words of decision 158 were. The rules-feedback skill held the test for edits to the house rules alone; the fix is one item under Code, after the reusable-files item, that holds it for any prose a session writes for an agent: "Prose written for an agent, such as skills, prompts, and instruction files, states principles in the fewest words that get the job done. A procedure or an output shape the request didn't ask for is added only for a failure that happened." Neither the reusable-files item, which is about what is specific to a repository, team or session, nor the comment item, which is about code comments, states it; the first Code item reaches a speculative line in a skill's diff already, so what the new item adds is the principle form and the bar on an unrequested procedure or shape. Cut from the draft, each by the item's own test or a decision: "a line that would change nothing if deleted goes", since decision 148 restated rules in each agent definition because rules that lived in one file were ignored in long runs, so a line that looks redundant can be the fix, primer's shapes file deletes no real constraint to shorten a file, and the fewest words that get the job done already catches a true redundancy; "else it is a note", which the findings item and the Done item "Report anything else you notice" already say; "a failure you can name", since decision 115 kept "only imagined" over "no one named" because the model can name a need itself, and it can name a failure as easily, so the item asks for a failure that happened, not a recorded one, which would lean on a record only this repository keeps; "still", which changes nothing; and the six kinds of file, which become the three-example "such as" form the reusable-files item uses, with "written for an agent" leaving out a README and a pull request body. "Each line" was considered and not taken, since it tests every existing line of a file and so reaches past the request. The bar reads on additions: an edit to existing prose adds a procedure only for a failure, and a request for a new skill covers that skill's own steps. No clause on files the request did not name, since "Stay inside the request" and "A review is read-only unless I ask for changes" already hold. Not taken: a skill in the rules plugin, which applies only when it fires; a section in primer's `shapes.md`, which binds only repositories that adopted primer; and a lint rule, since lint holds no style words (decision 10). The chat block does not mirror the item: it carries no Code item and already cuts words that add nothing when it edits the user's text. The rule count moves from 35 to 36. Settled 2026-10-31 by the owner. Reopens: an agent-read file written under the item that a recorded failure later shows too short or missing a stop, which would restore the cut words or add a procedure line.
160. **A prose cut is checked by the acceptance items its change owes, compared with a run at the version before.** A review read every file an agent reads against requirement 324 and found about 50,400 words per medium audit run, and smaller amounts in the other plugins, that repeat another file or that no step reads. Nothing in the suite shows that a rewrite kept behavior: the evals assert only that a skill fired and the form of its first line, the tests pin sentences and not what a run does, and the second reading of the review found a case, a clause or a stop held only by the text proposed for a cut in every area.
