After a change, the acceptance items whose rerun clauses it meets are run and compared item by item with a recorded run at the version before; where that record is older than the plugin's last change, a fresh before-run comes first. A cut that only removes text no step reads may rest on review, since there is no behavior to keep. A test whose only subject a cut deletes goes with it in the same commit, with the reason in the body and the behavior's own check named, since an assertion on text that no longer exists tests nothing; no other test is weakened, skipped or deleted. Not taken: gating prose on the eval runner's `baseline` grader, which has never run here and whose sandbox refused the gate's cases (decision 133). Settled 2026-11-01 by the owner. Reopens: the `converges` case and the `baseline` grader passing an unchanged gate twice, which makes that the check for the gate and the loop.
161. **The audit's cuts start with a pilot of the files every launch reads.** Of the audit's measured saving, about 90 percent sits in the agent bodies, `common.md` and `audit-evidence.md`, read at every launch, and item 15 gives a count of planted cases to measure them by. Audit 0.1.10 takes audit-1 to audit-13 of the review; audit-14 to audit-27, about 5,000 words per run in the orchestrator's own reads, wait for the pilot's result; issue 203 lists those that still hold. Items 14 and 15 were last recorded before audit 0.1.9, so both run at 0.1.9 first; item 15 finding fewer than the recorded 18 of 20 planted cases would mean the baseline is too unstable to measure the pilot, and the audit part stops. audit-13 drops the brief from the merger's inputs on review: no step of the merger cites it, and the finding's own test, the same converged items after a rerun, cannot be met, since unchanged runs of this fixture have given 37 and 39 items. audit-1 leaves the four sections of `audit-evidence.md` that no step names: the auditor body holds the matcher and open pull request rules, the stage 4 `tests` row holds the weak-test and test-account rules, and `audit-evidence.md` keeps the bars on a finding in `### Leads a finding must clear`, since the adversary and the second opinion read neither home. audit-9 moves the auditor and adversary schema out of `common.md`, as requirement 173 says it should be. The second-opinion launch reads the rules from the sentinel copies, so decision 21's proof covers them. Settled 2026-11-01 by the owner. Reopens: a run at 0.1.10 that finds fewer planted cases than the run at 0.1.9, or a merger that needed the brief.
162. **The gate cuts `record.md` only; the loop and primer wait for an edit of their own.** The gate's findings save about 2,400 words per run against the audit's 50,400, so the gate is not the pilot. gate-7 and gate-8 still ship, in gate 0.1.6: `record.md` describes each field again after the step that writes it, and its copy of `over-quarter` already lacks the counting rule Step 2 gained, so a copy has drifted once. The other gate findings wait. The loop's and primer's findings ride the next release that edits those files for another reason, when their pins and acceptance items are owed anyway; issues 204, 205 and 207 list those that still hold, the gate's, the primer's and the loop's, and issue 206 the one in the rules' chat block. Issue 207 is closed, since the loop's are cuts for length and those are set aside. Settled 2026-11-01 by the owner. Reopens: the `converges` spike of decision 160 passing, which makes the gate the cheapest place to try the method and brings its other findings.
163. **House-rule edit nineteen: the bug item loses the sentence the next item holds, and the `/rules` skill and chat block lose small copies.** The review's notes on the rules went through the rules-feedback skill or, for the skill itself, which is outside that skill's scope, straight to the owner. rules-1: "If you can't reproduce it, run a check that confirms or rejects your explanation." restates for one case the unverified-cause item that follows it, in every session and launch; it has been there since the first commit, with no failure or decision behind it, and it is no stop, so requirement 21 holds. Its cost is that it was the only line giving "show the failure before you fix it" an exit, and the exit is now the stricter "a run that changes only the suspected factor". The loop keeps its own copy for its own step (requirement 97). rules-7: the chat block's Mention bullet lacked "repo,", left from when the block assumed no repository; decision 68 removed that assumption and declared what still differs, and this was not on the list. rules-4: the skill's Step 1 ran `status` only to say what Step 2's verbatim report prints first; `status` stays a user verb. rules-5: two sentences repeat the chat-block file the skill points to, and two bullets are identical but for their label. Requirement 325 records the edit. The rule count stays 36; the chat block carries no such item. rules-2 stays, since no clean merge that broke a passed fix is on record or recalled; rules-3 and rules-6 wait for another edit of their files. Settled 2026-11-01 by the owner. Reopens: a session that treats a bug it cannot reproduce as blocked, which would restore the sentence; rules-2 on a recorded case.
164. **This repository's own prose: the hub and the plugins rule cut what lint and the documents hold, and the release skill follows the release flow in use.** The release skill's steps pushed the bump and the heading to `main`, while since 2026-10-25 both ride the milestone's branch and the tag is made from the owner's merge, and a literal run left `npm run test:rules` red, since it omits the catalog's `metadata.version` (decision 32) and the version literals of the rules tests. The skill now says so; the three asks stay, and the merge is named as the owner's. The hub's line on commit messages stood behind requirement 11 as amended after decision 141, and now says "and never a review pass or its level". The cuts: the hub's sentence on the design documents, since each document states its rank at its own line 3, with the first sentence split so that requirement 238's two sentences stay; three hub clauses that `package.json`, the suites or lint hold; the requirement-numbering line, which copies the top of `docs/requirements.md`; the plugins rule down to what no lint rule checks; the rules-feedback skill's read of the Mechanism section, the `/rules` script's own rules; and detail the release tool's output shows. Item 30, owed by any hub edit, had no run since 2026-10-21 and still named the old marketplace; its text moves to the current name with its record. No release, since nothing under `plugins/` changes. Settled 2026-11-01 by the owner. Reopens: a reused requirement number, which would restore the line.

165. **CI fails a pull request whose commit messages hold a session attribution line.** A branch carried such a trailer on most of its commits, which the rule on commit messages forbids; every check passed, review found it only after the push, and removing it took a rewrite and a force push. A hook on one machine catches this before the commit but not on another machine or in a child session, so CI holds the rule for every pull request. It reads only the pull request's range, so a line already on `main` can never fail later work, and it matches the three fixed forms in `tools/commit-messages.mjs`, not the wider rule on naming tools, which needs judgment. Reopens if a further form is recorded on a branch.
166. **`/primer:fold`: a read-only review of a repository against the Claude Code documentation as it reads on the day of the run.** `shapes.md` and `placement.md` are the suite's fold of the documentation read on 2026-10-20, and nothing rereads it; a spike found them already behind, since a skill with `disable-model-invocation: true` has no line in the startup context's skills table, while `placement.md` said a skill's description is always listed. That sentence is corrected in the same release. Fold is primer's second skill, since primer already owns the forms a fold lands in; R237 admits the sibling, and its test reads `skills/primer` alone. It reads the index and the pages the situation calls for live and holds no documentation content, so a rerun is a fresh review. One subagent reads each page, in the shape of the audit's digester, since the hooks reference is 284k characters and the skills page 119k. It writes nothing: a fold into an instruction file goes through `/primer`, one into code, configuration or a manifest through the loop or by hand. `disable-model-invocation: true` keeps its description out of the startup context and prose from invoking it. Its `allowed-tools` hold no `Bash(claude --version)`, so the version read of R328 prompts under the default mode; a listing would cover only the turn that invokes fold (decision 215). Not taken: a plugin of its own; a stored copy of the documentation; a record keyed by date to diff two runs; effort or budget words, until a run shows the need. Reopens: a run that misses a practice on an unlisted page, which adds the page; a run whose cost is out of proportion to its findings, which moves the page reads to the orchestrator; the bare `/primer` reaching fold (item 71).
167. **`/rules:refactor`: a read-only review of a whole repository against the house rules as shipped, handed to the loop as tickets.** The loop, the gate and the audit review a bundle against its tickets; nothing reviews a tree against the rules, or a repository that never used the suite. The skill sits beside `/rules` and reads the `core.md` that `/rules` ships, so a house-rule edit is the next run's rule set with no change to the skill, and it reads the repository's own instruction files as that repository's rules. It reads nothing else, since no shipped file holds a text called the principles: the README's three ideas describe the suite, and `placement.md` and `shapes.md` are primer's. It applies the items that describe a file, a test or a diff, stated as that principle rather than a list. A second pass challenges each finding, since decision 160 records a first reading that found a case held only by the text it would cut. It drives the refactoring by writing, on a yes, one ticket file per bundle and printing the loop command for each, one bundle per run on a new branch: the loop already plans, builds, reviews, checks, publishes and records, the gate and the audit then apply unchanged, and a write path in the skill would carry its own copy of the loop's open idempotency problem. `disable-model-invocation: true` keeps its description off the startup context, as `/rules` has none there. Not taken: a `principles.md` read by this skill alone; invoking `loop:run` from the skill, which would run the loop's reading block, questions and ask-first stops inside another skill's turn; refactoring in the session with subagents and commits, which duplicates the loop's build and checks with none of its record; checking commit history against the commit item; a record across runs; effort or budget words. Reopens: a principles file the owner writes, which the skill then reads beside `core.md`; a repository where the loop cannot run at all; a run whose cost is out of proportion to its findings.
168. **The growth test of `units` and `imports` compares median batches of CPU time, not best batches of wall-clock time.** Issue 45 left open whether its failures were timing noise or a slow path, and the work on audit 0.1.9 kept the test and its threshold unchanged until a slow path was found. None was: alone, `imports` on the stray-tick input took 12.6 ms at 8,000 lines and 49.8 ms at 32,000, a ratio of 4.0, and the test passed alone and with the rest of its file. It failed only under `npm test`, where other test files run beside it; with both clocks read in the same run, the best-batch wall-clock ratio was 14.88 and the CPU ratio 3.83. Busy-loop processes beside the test alone did not make it fail, so the cause is the time the process waits under the suite, not its share of the processor. CPU time alone did not hold: on the Windows runner the best-batch CPU ratio reached 10.67, since a batch there reads in clock ticks of about 15.6 ms and a single small run varied from 63 to 203 ms, and on the macOS runner a best-batch ratio reached 7.32. Over every reading taken, on the three runners and locally, the median-batch CPU ratio stayed at 6.87 or below, 6.87 being that Windows run. So the test reads `process.cpuUsage()`, compares the median batch of each side, and keeps its threshold of 10, between linear growth near 4 and quadratic near 16; a quadratic function under the changed test gives 15.82 and fails. A batch runs at least 100 ms, up from 50, to shrink the tick error.

169. **Placement sends an enforced behavior to its settings key and a skill's own failure into its step; two other gaps are not taken.** Four gaps in the suite were judged against the shipped texts; two are taken and two are not. First taken: a behavior the host enforces with a settings key had no placement item. Requirement 328 names "a hook or setting proposed" as a destination fold gives by `placement.md`, and no item of `placement.md` led to a setting. Item 2 now sends a tool call that must be stopped or asked about in every task, or a trailer the host adds, to the settings key that enforces it, permissions deny or ask, or attribution; the plan proposes the key and primer never writes it, as for a hook, and until it exists the piece is placed by the items below. The item covers only keys that enforce: an allow entry enforces nothing, so "always run the tests" stays a hub command under item 4, and a run's checks come from the request or the configuration, as decision 144 has them. The trailer case agrees with decision 136, which leaves the host's trailer to its attribution setting. No item is added, since items 10 and 11 are cited by number in `placement.md` and requirements 241 and 252, and the test asserts items 1 to 11. Second taken: a failure seen only when a skill runs had no route into that skill. The tasks test puts a fact that only one skill's runs need in that skill, and the bullet "A fact stays a rule even when a skill uses it" decides a fact other tasks also obey, not this case. A bullet after it sends the failure and its fix into the step that fails. Requirement 254 already gives a step the slot, what its output must show, so no shape changes and no failure field is added. Maintain step 4 said "never run a skill or change one this run did not raise", and "raise" there and in requirement 253 names a skill candidate, so on that reading a `feedback` note step 3 promotes into an existing skill was forbidden; the sentence now reads "never run a skill, and change a skill this run did not raise only by a line step 3 promotes", and requirement 255 carries the same exception. The Every mode line of `SKILL.md` that had item 1 or 2 propose only the check now names the hook or settings key, and requirement 246 with it. Not taken: that nothing in the suite runs the thing being changed. The gap is overstated: requirement 97 reproduces a bug before planning, the loop's Step 6.4 requires a test for a behavior change in a repository with a suite, house-rule edit nineteen (requirement 325) shows the failure first, and the gate's checks accept any command (decision 144). Not taken either: hooks for mechanical stops a run skips, and a length limit on the global instruction file. At default permissions a git write that no allow entry covers already prompts, decision 109 showed auto mode asking before the commit, the push and the pull request, and no skipped stop is on record, so decisions 111 and 114 stand. The length claim has no controlled evidence; the paper of decision 114 measured precedence, not length. Reopens: an allow entry or a command proposed under item 2, or a skill's failure placed outside its skill, which would show the new wording does not hold; a loop or gate run that published green checks with broken behavior, which reopens the first gap not taken; a skipped stop on record, which reopens the hooks, in the shape of a PreToolUse hook that returns an ask decision rather than a deny, so a false positive, the objection decision 111 raised against its reply hook, costs one prompt and blocks nothing; that shape is unverified against the hooks documentation.

170. **Primer and the audit's act stage keep the host's attribution trailer.** Decision 136 and requirement 11 say no plugin types a trailer of its own or removes one the host's attribution setting adds, and gate 0.1.2 and loop 0.1.7 shipped that wording. Primer's commit rule, "carries no `Co-Authored-By` or other trailer that does", and the act stage's "with no trailer that does" were not changed with them, so with the setting on, a primer or act commit loses the trailer a loop or gate commit keeps. Both lines now read as the gate's and the loop's do: type no attribution trailer of your own, and keep one the host's attribution setting adds, even where it names a model or tool. A user who wants no trailer turns the setting off, and that now holds for every plugin alike. The rule that the text a plugin writes names no model, agent or tool does not change. Reopens: as decision 136.

171. **CI fails a pull request whose description holds a session attribution line.** A session link reached a pull request description, and the check of decision 165 reads only commit messages, so every check passed. A hook on one machine stops this before the command runs, but not in a session elsewhere or in a description edited on the forge, so CI holds the rule here too. `tools/commit-messages.mjs --description` reads the description on stdin, matches the same three forms and names each line it fails on, so the two checks cannot drift apart. It runs in its own workflow on a pull request's opened, edited, reopened and synchronize events: `ci.yml` cancels an older run on the same pull request, so `edited` there would restart the test matrix on every edit of a title or description. The description reaches the command through an environment variable and is never typed into it, since anyone who opens a pull request writes it. Reopens: as decision 165.

172. **The first fold and refactor review of this repository: what holds is acted on, every finding gets a verdict in the pull request, and the review recurs on the owner's call.** The fold and refactor skills had run on this repository only as acceptance items 69 and 73, whose results keep counts and the first item or two, so nothing acted on what they found: the fold's character cap and its Windows hook item came back in the run of 2026-11-03 as the runs of 2026-11-02 had found them. In that run the fold's three recommended items held against the documentation. First, the host adds an attribution trailer by default, and a session link from some sessions, which this repository's commit rule forbids and CI fails only after the commit is made (decisions 165 and 171); the tracked `.claude/settings.json` now sets `attribution` in the object form, `commit` and `pr` empty and `sessionUrl` false, since the bare `false` needs 2.1.281 and an older host skips the whole file that holds it. The setting is a first guard, not the enforcement: the object passed to a headless session by `--settings` removed the trailer, but a headless session in a fresh clone, with only the tracked file to read it from, still added it, so the commit and description checks of CI stay what enforces the rule. Second, from 2.1.309 the host gives only the start of a nested `CLAUDE.md` or a rule that loads on demand when it is over 40,000 characters on a 200K context window, and primer counted only lines, so a rule of long lines passed its 200-line cap and lost its end; `shapes.md` and the drift hook now cap a nested file and a rule at 40,000 characters too, the smaller of the host's two limits, counted as UTF-8 characters by deleting continuation bytes under `LC_ALL=C` and counting the rest, since `wc -m` depends on the locale. The cap covers every rule, not only path-scoped ones, as the simpler rule; an unconditional rule that long costs its size at every startup anyway. Third, the drift hook's shell form with no `onFailure` can block every shell call on Windows without Git Bash; requirement 5 makes Git for Windows a prerequisite and the check needs Windows, so it is issue 69 and not taken here. The refactor's six bundles held in part: about half of their sixteen findings stood when each location was opened. Taken: lint and release list the files git sees and scan `plugin_version` literals with two copies that already differ on a symbolic link, so both now share one module; the release test indexed three audit files at fixed lines that three audit changes had to move, so it now finds each line by its content; requirement 319 still described the gate's eval cases that decision 133 dropped; and the record phrases that locate a session, an operating-system edition, a named machine and a reader's words, are cut by decision 157's principle wherever they stand. Declined, each refuted at its location: a suite without `jq` exiting 0, since `sh.test.mjs` requires its ok line; two findings under the rule on literals, a copy compared with its source and a filter compared with a literal; the gate's spike fixtures read as runs made elsewhere, since they are this suite's own spikes; the chat block's version label, which every change has moved with the style; and a step number in a warning that names a step that exists. The audit README's tool list is right to fix, but it costs an audit release for one line and stage 1 says the same at run time, so it waits for the next audit release. The refactor's second pass let through findings whose rule did not say what they claimed and missed a test and a decision that held the text, so its brief now names both: a test or a recorded decision that holds the text, and a quoted rule that does not cover the case when no other item does. One rerun cannot show the effect, since two runs share few findings (the runs of 2026-11-02), so items 73 and 74 rerun as a regression check only. Three more defects are issues: a hub sentence the tree contradicts listed as a note rather than for `/primer` (issue 70), bundles that do not fit one loop run (issue 71), and both skills quoting the reviewing session's own instructions (issue 72). The review recurs on the owner's call, at least every ten merged pull requests after the last, with no schedule built, since the failure was findings left unacted, not a review left unrun. The pull request that carries a run of item 69 or 73 on this repository lists every recommended item and bundle with its verdict and the change that acts on it or the reason it is declined; the raw reports stay in the ignored scratch directory. Settled 2026-11-03 by the owner. Reopens: a review whose findings again go unacted, a run that shows the second-pass brief drops a finding that holds, or a run that shows when the host applies the tracked attribution setting.

173. **Refactor and fold rest a finding on the repository and their own sources; refactor sends a contradicted instruction sentence to `/primer` and sizes a bundle by area and release.** The first fold and refactor review of this repository (decision 172) showed three defects of the skills themselves. Both put the reviewing session's own instructions into their reports as if they were facts about the repository: fold's first recommended item cited the session's attribution instruction, and a refactor note weighed a check against the trailer the session's environment adds. Each skill now says that a finding rests on the repository and the source the skill names, the documentation for fold and `core.md` with the repository's instruction files for refactor, and never on the reviewing session's own instructions, settings or environment; refactor's brief carries the sentence to each reviewer and each refuter, since both load the same instructions. Refactor listed a hub sentence that the tree contradicts throughout as a note and named nothing for `/primer`, although the skill already sends a disagreement between an instruction file and the code there; the skill and each brief now name that case, and it is never a note. Refactor also wrote one bundle that spans three areas with no shared change, and one that changes a plugin directory without naming the release lint then requires; a bundle now holds the files of one area and that area's tests, a change to a part the repository versions and releases on its own is its own bundle, and the bundle and its ticket name the release it needs. Reopens: a run of item 73 or 74 whose report cites the session's instructions, lists a contradicted instruction sentence as a note, or gives a bundle that spans areas or needs a release it does not name.

174. **A skill description writes a placeholder in square brackets.** The host shows `<` and `>` in a skill's `description` as `&lt;` and `&gt;` in the skill list it matches requests against, so the gate's trigger phrases read there as `gate PR &lt;n&gt;` and `gate the manifest &lt;path&gt;`. The gate's description now writes `[n]` and `[path]`, and `shapes.md` says a written `description` holds no `<` or `>`, so primer carries the rule to other repositories. Whether the escaped form ever made a request miss the skill was not checked; the brackets cost nothing either way. The README keeps `<n>`, since Markdown shows it as written. Reopens: a host release that shows angle brackets in a description unescaped, or one that reads square brackets there as something else.

175. **The cleanup of `revert-tests.sh` retries a work dir removal that leaves it.** Case 15c of the script's suite left its work dir about once in 100 to 200 runs. A trace of the script's cleanup showed it ran with the work dir present, and its own `rm -rf` left the work dir behind, holding one empty copy directory, with no error; a second removal moments later removed it. The work dir otherwise stayed until something else removed it, so a longer wait in the test would not help. The cleanup now removes the work dir again each tick for up to 10 seconds while it is still there, and, on a run that otherwise succeeds, names on stderr a work dir still there after that, with the last removal's error; a failed run keeps its own message as the last line. Case 22 holds the retry on every system: a shim `rm` first on the PATH recreates the copy directory after its first removal of the work dir, which leaves the work dir without the retry. Case 23 holds that a removal that also fails with an error line does not send that line to the run's stderr, and case 24 holds that a work dir that stays is named on stderr, and case 25 that a failed run still ends with its own message. Which process held the directory was not found; the retry does not depend on it. Reopens: a work dir the cleanup still names on stderr after the retry window.

176. **Stage 7's slices and the merger read a code fence as the ledger does.** The slice commands for `ledger/5.md`, `ledger/6.md` and `ledger/7.md` started and ended a section at any line that looked like a heading, and the merger's rule for reading a section by Grep did the same. A finding that quotes code holds fenced lines such as `### X9:` or `## Not a heading`: such a line cut the slice short, so the merger saw a finding without its tail, and it started a section for an id the ledger does not hold, so the slice could name an id with no finding behind it. `ledger.sh` `classify` already treats a line inside a fence as no heading, so the slices disagreed with the check that reads the same files. The commands now start and end a section only at a line outside a fence, a fence line being one that starts with three backticks after leading spaces and tabs, print fence lines with the section that holds them, and, when a copied section reaches the end of the file inside an open fence, end the copy with a closing fence line so the rest of the slice is read outside it. The existence check of `ledger/6.md` and `ledger/7.md`, like that of `ledger/5.md`, exits 0 whenever a section for the id starts outside a fence, since no slice asks for a block `ledger.sh` leaves out: `load_l6` leaves out of the gate universe a `ledger/6.md` X block that `extent` ends at the end of the file inside an open fence, accepted only when stage 6 failed, while a `## <id>` section that ends inside the fence stays in it, and the `gate` op fails on an unclosed `ledger/5.md` or `ledger/7.md` before any slice is built. The merger keeps its own end-of-file rule, because it reads raw ledgers in single mode: a `### <id>: ` block is no block when the file ends inside an open fence and, outside a fence, no line after its heading starts with `### ` or `## ` or is an end line, which is the block `ledger.sh` leaves out. The merger's text carries the same rule and a way to tell a fenced Grep hit without reading the file whole: count the lines that start with three backticks above the hit, and an odd count means the hit is fenced. A check in `tests/audit/lint.sh` takes the commands from the stage file by their content and runs them on temp ledgers holding a fenced `### X9:` line, a fenced `## ` line and a `### X3:` section whose fence stays open to the end of the file, checking that its existence check exits 0 and that the copies end with the closing fence, comparing the output and exit status with literal text. The commands for `live/findings.md` are unchanged: the readers of that file track no fence, so a slice cut by a different rule would disagree with them. Not taken: a check that skips a `### <id>: ` section reaching the end of the file inside an open fence, which would disagree with `extent`, since `extent` ends a block at any `### ` line, `## ` line or end line, so a section whose open fence follows a `### Evidence` sub-heading would be skipped by the slice and kept by `ledger.sh`. Reopens: a reader of `live/findings.md` that tracks fences, or a ledger file whose fenced line a slice still cuts at.

177. **The rules skill names the Bash tool for its script.** In one `/rules` run, the session called `rules.mjs` with the PowerShell tool first. The skill's `allowed-tools` pre-approves Bash forms only, so the call failed, and the session then used Bash and finished. The skill now says to run each command with the Bash tool, which its `allowed-tools` pre-approves. This was seen once and the sentence was not checked in a run: whether it stops the first choice is unverified, and the failed call costs one retry, not a wrong result. Reopens: a run that still calls the script with PowerShell, which would send the fix to the `allowed-tools` grant rather than to the sentence.

178. **Lint fails a skill description that holds an angle bracket.** Decision 174 moved the gate's placeholders to square brackets and put the rule in `shapes.md`, but nothing in this repository checked it, and the release skill's description shipped with `<plugin>` and `<version>`: the same defect a second time. Lint now fails any `SKILL.md` under `plugins/*/skills/*/` or `.claude/skills/*/` whose description holds `<` or `>`, with `<file>: description holds < or >; write a placeholder as [name]`, and the release skill writes `[plugin]` and `[version]`. The lint suite has a negative case for a plugin skill and one for a project skill. Not taken: a check of the body, where a placeholder in angle brackets is shown as written. Reopens: a host release that shows angle brackets in a description unescaped (decision 174).

179. **A continued branch is planned and reverified at its tip, the commit the build starts from.** On a continued branch the planning snapshot is the branch tip, so step 3.7.1 ran and compared the snapshot with the base commit. The diff showed the branch's own work reversed, while the build starts from the tip, so the plan was revised against a state the build never sees. A run in a worktree failed the other way: the worktree was created at the base commit, so the plan read the base, step 3.7.1 saw no difference and skipped, and the build then switched to the tip, which the plan never read. Step 0.2 now defines the start commit, the commit the build starts from: the base commit for a branch that does not exist yet, the tip for a continued one. The worktree is created at the start commit, Step 0.4 compares the planning snapshot with it and step 3.7.1 reads it; `run.md` records it beside the base commit. The baseline stays on the base, so a failure an earlier commit of the branch introduced is not taken for a baseline failure. Three tests in `tests/loop/preflight.test.mjs` pin the forms: the worktree line, the snapshot comparison and the reverification. Not taken: skipping step 3.7.1 on a continued branch, which leaves a worktree run planning at the base commit and names no commit for the plan to read; comparing the snapshot with the base commit, which reverses the branch's own work. Reopens: a run whose build starts somewhere other than the base commit or the branch tip.
180. **Concise Plain 4.11: a draft matches its thread, and mechanics stay out of a message to a reader who won't open the code.** Field feedback of 2026-11-04 on one ticket comment that took many redrafts, read in a two-pass review the same day, the second pass challenging the first. Five changes to the Drafts and Tone sections, each behind a redraft the feedback names. First, a draft to readers who would not open the code carried file, branch and pull request detail, because the finding-shape bullet pulled every draft toward a code review and outranked the reader bullet in practice; the finding bullet is now scoped to a reader who will open the code, and the reader bullet names what the other reader never gets, file, branch, line, pull request or check output, and what they get instead, what they see and what will change. Decision 156's reopen clause fires on its face, but the cause is the competing bullet and not the "will use" test, so that test stays; decision 141 is the precedent for naming categories where a principle lost to a task's vocabulary, and decision 158 kept the finding bullet for its mechanism, so it is scoped and not cut. Second, redrafts swung between terse and chatty under asks to sound more like a person and then to be more concise, since nothing observable anchored the register; the Tone bullet now takes length and register from the messages already on the thread, plain and professional only when there is none. Decision 113's reopen clause fires for the ask to shorten, but decision 156 found the cut length sentence did not prevent that failure while it was present, so it is not restored; this is a different mechanism and gets its trial. The feedback's measure, the median length of the last few comments, is a procedure and is left out. Third, a draft rebutted a point nobody on the thread had made and did not open by answering the last comment; a reply now answers the last message to the sender and takes up only what the thread raises. This differs from the line decision 156 declined, which asked the owner to paste the thread; here the thread is already in context. Fourth, one draft promised work the owner had not approved, and others opened by grading the request or closed by grading the other person's analysis; the settle bullet now commits the sender to nothing they haven't decided and grades neither the request nor the reader's work. The writer's own effort was already covered by "about the change, not about you", so only the grading half is new. Fifth, a draft the owner pastes by hand carried raw mention identifiers; the mention bullet now writes the name where the mention goes in a pasted draft and says the owner picks it in the platform, the raw form only when a tool posts the draft, the same exception the links line already makes for a bare id. Decision 158's reopen clause does not fire: a mention is not an id of the Links section. Not taken: dropping the clause that lets a draft ask the reader for what the sender needs, since the failure, a settled choice shown as a question, is already forbidden by the next sentence and dropping an agreed clause for a violated rule is what decision 113 rejected; a line against bold lead-ins, which "no label on a paragraph or bullet" already states; "tag the others after the first sentence", "in its order" and the two-case mention wording, shapes with no redraft behind them; moving the finding shape to a review-report rule, since the style has none and requirement 332 holds the refactor's own; and a one-line readiness test before a draft is shown, a check step of the kind decision 158 cut. The chat block carries the five lines word for word, so its list of deliberate differences does not change. The house-rule count stays 36. Settled 2026-11-04 by the owner. Reopens: a draft to a reader who won't open the code that still carries a file or branch under the named categories, which would cut the finding bullet from the style; a thread with messages to match whose draft still swings in register, which would restore a default size; a mention still raw in a pasted draft.

181. **`guard`: a sixth plugin that writes the auto mode classifier's configuration for a list of repositories the user names.** Auto mode sessions were denied routine work often enough that sessions moved to bypass mode, where nothing is checked. The transcripts of those sessions held seven External System Writes denials, one Merge Without Review on `gh pr merge` in a repository whose owner merges their own pull requests, two Credential Exploration denials on reads of settings files, and Self-Modification denials on writes to configuration. Of the seven, four were pull request writes in the session's own repository: a `gh pr create --body-file -` with the body on stdin, and `gh pr edit --body-file` on a file the same compound command had just written, three times, twice on a pull request the session had not opened. The rule's text excludes a write whose text cannot be derived from the transcript, which a body file is, so an environment entry alone would not have cleared them. The other three were a nested headless session, an issue label on a throwaway repository and a push from a sandbox clone, which no entry should clear. The host reads `autoMode` only from the user settings file, managed settings and `--settings`, never from project settings, and a plugin can ship hooks but no settings, so the configuration has to be written into the user settings file. Spikes on Claude Code 2.1.318: `claude auto-mode defaults` printed 21 environment lines, 16 allow, 72 soft deny and 1 hard deny rules; a `--settings` file holding `$defaults` and one `Organization:` line gave 22 environment lines, two of them Organization lines and one of those `None configured`, so an added line does not replace the slot it names, and a full list of 21 with that slot's text replaced gave 21; `claude auto-mode config` read the `settings.json` of `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` in a new directory with no login, in about 0.3 s, and left `.claude.json` and `backups/` there; `--settings` adds to the user file rather than replacing it, 23 lines for one `$defaults` and one added line in each, so a dry run puts the candidate in a scratch configuration directory instead; Node's `execFile` ran the `claude` executable directly. Guard is its own plugin. Its later work, a deny set that holds in bypass mode and a denial logger, is hooks, and a hook in `rules` would run for every user of the house rules; `rules.mjs` is at 675 of its 700 lines (requirement 38), so a second writer is a second script either way; and the rules README's promise never to edit `settings.json` stays true. The script follows `rules.mjs`: plan then apply, a hash check, a backup, an atomic write and a lock, and the skill shows and asks. The repository list is `reimagine-guard.json` beside the settings file, since the plugin data directory is deleted when the plugin is uninstalled from its last scope and the list would go with it; paths differ per machine, so the list is per machine. Guard owns the `autoMode` key: a configuration installer that merges a shared settings file into the user file should not name it, since such a merge replaces arrays and the paths differ per machine. Ownership is marked in the entries themselves, each ending ` (reimagine guard)`, as the house rules block is marked by its lines, so an entry without the ending is the user's and stays, whatever the state file holds. The environment list is written whole, with the text of the slots guard does not change taken from `claude auto-mode defaults` at each run and never from a shipped copy, and a stale copy is refreshed from the state file's record: while repositories are listed and the state file records copied default lines, a default line the host adds later is added at the next run, and when the list empties and the copied lines are every current default, they go back to one `$defaults`. Guard changes two slots: Trusted repo keeps the host's text and gains a sentence naming the checkouts, and Source control is guard's own, since the host's says no other orgs are configured. Three allow entries cover the denials above: publishing in the listed repositories, a body file or stdin body the agent or its subagents wrote counting as derived from the transcript and a pull request the user named counting as one to work on; reading settings files as not credential exploration; and merging, only in a repository the user marks `merge`, which is an exception to the one rule whose purpose is human review and so is off by default and never for a repository whose pull requests others review. Nothing guard writes clears Self-Modification: guard's own write is that rule's case, so the skill pre-approves only the script's `status`, `list` and `plan` verbs, unlike `/rules`, since a pre-approved command can skip the classifier in auto mode the way a narrow allow rule does; `apply` meets the session's mode, and the skill asks for a reply that names the change. A plan checks its candidate in a scratch configuration directory and `apply` checks the real one, both with `claude auto-mode config`; neither sees server-managed settings that are not cached there, so the check after the write is the one that holds. A machine where another scope adds `autoMode` entries is refused as `managed`, since the whole list would duplicate them. Not taken: a skill and script in `rules`, for the reasons above; a skill in primer, which never writes settings; a script in a private configuration repository, which the suite could not test or ship; `/auto-mode-setup` alone, which works on one project at a time, interactively, accepted or discarded whole; `$defaults` with added slot lines, which leaves the default line beside the added one; the list inside `settings.json`, where an unknown key may be reported as an error; the list in the plugin data directory; an allow entry that clears guard's own write; a second environment form for a managed machine, until one exists. A write and its body in one compound command hide the body's source from a classifier that sees commands and not their output, which is a matter for the loop's and the gate's publish steps, not for guard. Settled 2026-11-04 by the owner. Reopens: a host release that merges environment lines by slot, reads `autoMode` from another place, or changes what `claude auto-mode config` prints; a listed repository's publishing still denied with guard's entries applied (item 79); a pre-approved `apply` shown not to skip the classifier, which would let the skill pre-approve it (item 77); a machine refused as `managed`.
182. **No default or fallback selects the top tier; `fable` stays an override by name.** The loop's plan reviewer, the audit's second opinion and the audit's swap rung all selected `fable` by default, and several runs at once exhausted its usage limit, which a run of acceptance item 14 on 2026-10-29 had already met: the second opinion failed on that limit and was swapped to `opus`. The plan reviewer now runs at `opus` and retries once at the `fallback-reviewer` model, `sonnet` by default, or at `sonnet` when that model is the reviewer's own, or at `opus` for a reviewer on `sonnet`; the loop's `opus` stand-in retries once at `sonnet` for the same reason; the second opinion runs at `opus` and relaunches a failed batch once at `sonnet`, or at `opus` when it was overridden to `sonnet`; the other roles relaunch on their own model, then on `opus`, or on `sonnet` for a role already on `opus`. The swap rung still changes model rather than retrying on the same one, since the failure on record is a usage limit, which a same-model retry meets again. `fable` stays an accepted value in every `models` key and prose override, so a user who asks for it by name gets it, and the gate's deny list keeps the word. Not changed: the gate's reviewer, a `claude -p` child session that takes the model the session or its settings set; the gate selects no model, and a `--model` flag would override a choice the user already made. Decision 15's retry at `opus` gives way to the retry at `sonnet`; its mechanism, a fallback written into the skill, stands. Loop 0.1.15 and audit 0.1.14; the gate's text does not change and keeps 0.1.7. Acceptance item 14's expected text names an `opus` second opinion and awaits a rerun at audit 0.1.14. Reopens: a plan review or second opinion at `opus` whose `sonnet` retry loses what the run needed, which would make the retry a second `opus` relaunch; a gate run whose cost the owner wants set apart from the session model, which would add `models.reviewer` and `--model` to the child session.
183. **Concise Plain 4.12: an ask names the decision's owner, and a status note to a reader who won't open the code starts from what changed for them.** Field feedback of 2026-11-04 on two emails drafted under 4.10 and 4.11, a reply on a thread and a status note to two readers who won't open the code, seven redrafts between them, read in a two-pass review, the second pass challenging the first. Four changes to the Drafts section, each behind a redraft the feedback names. First, a draft put a decision the team shares to the two readers as theirs, and the thread reply's ask went through four forms on the same slip; the settle bullet now sends an ask to whoever owns the decision and puts a shared one as the team's, never as the reader's because they are the reader. Second, the status note's first draft opened with release mechanics and was cut by about half on each of three turns before it read as the reader bullet already describes; the reader bullet now names the three things a status note to that reader answers, what changed for them, what happens next and what they decide, and nothing on how the result was reached. Decision 180's reopen clause fires on its face, since a branch name reached a reader who won't open the code under 4.11, but its remedy rests on the finding bullet outranking the reader bullet, and a status note carries no findings; the mechanism here is a draft written down from what the session knows rather than up from what the reader needs, so the finding bullet stays. The feedback's self-check before a draft is shown is not taken: decision 180 declined a readiness test as a check step of the kind decision 158 cut, and its principle is the clause on how the result was reached. Third, the whole-draft return was skipped twice when the owner asked for one paragraph and the paragraph was accepted both times; the bullet now allows the one paragraph asked for. Fourth, the sent reply wrote a pull request id with its URL in parentheses, since pasted email renders no Markdown link; the last Drafts bullet now names that form where links won't render, in place of the bare id. Not taken: a per-paragraph reader level and a rule on review suggestions that re-enter forbidden content, which the feedback summarizes without a record. The chat block carries the four lines word for word, so its list of deliberate differences does not change. The house-rule count stays 36. Settled 2026-11-05 by the owner. Reopens: a status note to a reader who won't open the code that still opens with mechanics, which would cut the finding bullet as decision 180 named; an ask still put to the reader for a decision they do not own; a pasted draft that still writes a bare id where a URL would render as text.
184. **A usage limit in a child review is an infrastructure failure, and `findings.mjs` names every bad output.** A child session that stopped on a usage limit returned either `is_error` false with a `result` holding only the limit message, or `is_error` true with `api_error_status` 429. `findings.mjs` read both as failed reviews and stopped at the first bad output, so the pass was recorded `failed`, the run ended `unconverged`, and the finished reviews of the same pass never reached the ledger, since the step forbids a finding list of the orchestrator's own: in one pass 18 of 29 children returned the limit message, and a split pass lost its one finished review. The script now reads every output before it fails, prints one line per bad output, and gives a limit its own line, `usage limit`: an `api_error_status` of 429, or a child session's JSON result of at most 500 characters with no findings array whose text matches the limit pattern, since a review that discusses a rate limiter is longer; the host's exact wording is not on record, so the pattern sits in one constant, and a result that carries an array is never a limit. `num_turns` stays no signal (decision 118). The step reviews each limited group once more through the stand-in at `models.fallback-reviewer` for that group alone, then reads the finished outputs and the stand-in replies together; a stand-in reply that is itself a limit, or any other failure, records the pass `failed` with the reason and ends the run `unconverged`, so the ladder has one rung. Each `reviews[]` entry records the models the child's `modelUsage` names, and a limited output's entry records the failure (requirements 305 to 307, 311 and 317). Not taken: a `models.reviewer` key passed as `--model`, which decision 182's reopen clause ties to cost set apart from the session model, not to a limit; a retry after a wait, which spends the review budget on a limit that resets on the host's schedule; a pause for the user, which a headless run cannot answer. Reopens: a limited group whose stand-in review also meets the limit in two runs, which would add `--model`; a limit message the pattern misses.
185. **A new finding's candidates include entries on other files that share an identifier.** Candidates offered only the same file's settled entries, so a defect deferred against a design document and reported in a later pass against the schema file that holds the same construct printed `candidates: []`; one design question sat in the ledger as three entries over two files, the report listed it three times, and each new id counted as new under `rejects-at-high` and `nothing-to-fix`. Decision 143's reopen clause, a missed match with a candidate list present, is not what happened: the match was never offered. The identity stays file plus normalized summary. Candidates now end with cross-file entries in a settled state that the pass did not report and whose raw summary shares a key identifier with the new finding's, a backticked span or a camelCase or snake_case token of three characters or more, each naming its file and the shared identifiers; the orchestrator still decides the match by reading both, and `--check` already accepted a match to any id (requirements 302 and 306). Not taken: a match to any id with no candidate offered, which leaves the reading of the whole ledger the candidates were made to avoid; shared plain words, which would offer most of the ledger. Reopens: a cross-file candidate list long enough that the triage stops reading it, or a reworded finding with no shared identifier missed twice.
186. **A reviewer's notes keep the text after its fence.** Decision 144 put the text before the fence into each `reviews[]` entry, as the place where the reviewer says what it could not run. In one run every reviewer wrote that text after its fence, so `notes` stayed empty in the record and the report while the summary carried it under `notes.after`. `findings.mjs` now prints `notes.text`, the text before and the text after joined with a blank line, each kept only when it is not empty, and the record and the Log take that (requirements 306, 311 and 317). Reopens: a reviewer whose notes and findings interleave so that neither side holds them whole.
187. **A fix round that touched only documentation sends the run to `medium`.** On branches that changed a design document beside a small code change, each `high` pass over the document found one to four new accuracy findings, each real and minor, so no pass came back empty and the runs ended `unconverged` on `max-passes`, the last passes fixing only wording while every code finding was a repeated deferral or rejection. The fix round's change kinds gain `docs-only`: every changed file is prose for people that no build, test or runtime reads, a file the host loads as instructions, such as a `CLAUDE.md`, a rule, a skill, step or prompt file, not counting, since in a plugin repository those files are the product, while a design document an instruction file only points to counts. A rule `docs-only`, after `after-medium` and before `wide-fix`, gives `medium` to a pass with a fix finding whose round was `docs-only`, so a documentation fix outside the original diff no longer goes to `xhigh`, and the next pass stops under `after-medium` (requirements 301 and 311). Decision 130's other rules and their order stand. Not taken: a stop after two documentation-only passes, which leaves the last fixes unverified at any level. Reopens: a defect in documentation a reader acts on that a `medium` pass misses and a `high` pass finds.
188. **`next.mjs` caps a pass that the run budget cannot hold.** Step 3 capped the next pass only when the run budget was already spent, so a pass that started at 113 of 120 minutes ran a full review, about $24, and the run ended at 128 minutes `unconverged` with that review's 8 findings open and unfixed. `next.mjs` now takes two optional numbers, the minutes left in the run budget and the minutes a pass needs, which the step gives as the review budget for each six groups or part of six that the pass just made reviewed plus the subagent budget in force, 50 minutes at the defaults for a pass of one review, and marks a level `capped` with `budget` when the time left is less; `max-passes` wins when both apply, and a stop is never capped (requirements 301 and 303). The script still reads no clock: the orchestrator computes the time left from its `date` and the record. Not taken: the duration of the run's longest pass as the estimate, which a first pass of a run does not have; the check budget in the sum, since a fix round's checks rarely reach it. Reopens: a pass capped by this rule that would have fit, seen twice.
189. **A moved branch that another worktree has checked out is judged, not blocked.** Decision 146 made a new branch from another worktree a judged `local-branch` line and left a moved branch `blocked`. Refs are shared by every worktree of a repository, so a commit or push on a branch checked out in a sibling worktree moved a ref the audit does not read and blocked the run, once at stage 1 before any agent launched and twice in one run at stages 4 and 5 after a resume had retaken the baseline. `readonly.sh check` now reads the worktrees at check time, and a moved `refs/heads/<name>` checked out in a worktree other than the audited checkout prints as a `local-branch` pair, `-` and `+`, with its config lines, such as those a `push -u` writes or rewrites, as `local-branch` lines too, with exit 3; the orchestrator asks once per branch and new commit, records the yes as a `local-branch` approval, and the pair passes with it, and keeps the line `blocked` when the branch is a bundle's branch or base (requirements 178 and 179). A deleted branch, a moved branch no worktree holds, and the index and the tree stay `blocked`. Not taken: comparing refs only for the bundles' branches and bases, which the script cannot do, since it never reads the manifest. Reopens: a sibling worktree's branch whose move changed what an agent read, which would block the moved branch again.
190. **Guard runs the npm shim of `claude` on Windows.** An npm install on Windows puts only a `claude.cmd` shim on the PATH, and `execFileSync('claude', ...)` cannot start a `.cmd` file, so every `claude auto-mode` call failed with `ENOENT` and guard reported `unavailable` on every run; the native executable, which the suite and the acceptance runs use, was not affected. On Windows a call that fails with `ENOENT` while a `claude.cmd` is on the PATH is made again through `cmd.exe`, the arguments being the script's own fixed words, checked before they are joined; `cmd.exe` is given the full path of the first `claude.cmd` on the PATH, since it looks for a bare command in the current folder first, and the startup hook runs in the folder the session opened; any other failure keeps its own error (requirement 341). Not taken: `shell: true` with an argument array, which prints a deprecation warning; running the shim's target with `node`, which depends on the npm layout. Reopens: a Windows install whose shim `cmd.exe` cannot run.
191. **Guard records the classifier's denials through a `PermissionDenied` hook.** Guard's three allow entries were chosen from the denials of one set of sessions, and nothing recorded a denial afterwards, so no one could tell whether the entries clear the denials they were written for. Decision 181 named a denial logger as guard's later work and requirement 344 left it out of 0.1.0. The host's `PermissionDenied` event fires when auto mode denies a tool call and gives `tool_name`, `tool_input`, `tool_use_id`, `reason`, which names the matched rule in square brackets, and the common fields. Guard now ships `hooks/hooks.json` with one such hook that appends one line per denial to `denials.jsonl` in its data directory: the time, the reason, the tool, the command cut at 300 characters for a shell call or the file path for another tool, and the working directory; never `tool_input` whole, which can hold a file's contents, and never output. It prints nothing and exits 0 whatever happens. `/guard denials` prints the file through a verb the skill pre-approves beside `status`, `list` and `plan`, since it only reads. This reverses requirement 8's three hooks and requirement 334's "no hook", and lint pins guard's `hooks.json` as it pins the other two (requirements 8, 219, 334, 335, 343 and 344). Not taken: a hook in `rules`, for decision 181's reasons; recording denials by the user or a permission rule, which the event does not cover. Reopens: a host release that stops firing the event for a classifier denial, or a recorded line that holds content the rule forbids.
192. **Guard says at startup when its entries are stale.** `status` already rendered the entries against the host's current defaults, so a host release that added or reworded a default line made the state `stale`, but nothing told the user, and the copied lines stayed behind the host until the user happened to run `/guard`. Guard's `hooks.json` gains a `SessionStart` hook, matcher `startup`, that runs `guard.mjs session-start`: with no listed repository it reads nothing further and starts no `claude`, so a user who lists none pays one file read; otherwise it computes the state with the list's recorded remotes and no `claude auto-mode config` check, and prints one `systemMessage` line only for `stale`, nothing for any other state or on any error, and exits 0, as the rules notice does (requirement 41). Requirement 8's hooks outside a request become five (requirements 8, 219, 334 and 341). Reopens: a startup slowed by the defaults call, which would move the check to a recorded host version.
193. **The drift hook lets a shell call go ahead when it cannot run.** The hooks reference says a shell-form `PreToolUse` command hook on Windows without Git Bash runs in PowerShell, where `sh` is missing, and that such a hook failing with `onFailure` unset can block the tool call; the drift hook's matcher is `Bash|PowerShell`, so it could block every shell call for a user who skipped requirement 5's prerequisite. The hook now sets `"onFailure": "continue"`, which the reference names as the fix for a hook whose call should go ahead when it fails, so on such a host the check is skipped and the call runs (requirement 259). The failure was not reproduced: a machine with Git for Windows removed from the PATH still found Git Bash at its install location. The field needs Claude Code 2.1.295 or later; what an older host does with it is not documented, and CI's pinned host is later. The notice hook is unchanged, since a `SessionStart` failure blocks nothing. Reopens: a host without Git Bash where the call is still blocked, or an older host that refuses to load the hook.
194. **A split merges small sibling groups and runs at most six reviews at once.** Decisions 145 and 150 settled the split without a floor, so a wide diff of many small directories gave one child per leaf: about 6,200 lines over 97 files, most of them tests across many folders, gave 29 groups, many of one or two files of 4 to 300 lines, each a full `xhigh` child session; pass 1 took 28 minutes, cost about $160 and returned 245 findings, most on test placement, naming and duplication, so cost and finding count followed the number of groups and not the size of the change. After the grouping, sibling groups under a floor of one fifth of `split`, 300 lines at the default, are merged in byte order of key until each merged group reaches the floor, a merged group holding two groups or more; a merged group names its files one by one, as the `.` key does, and its slug is its parent's with `-small`. At most six children run at once and the rest start as one ends, the pass's reviews having the review budget for each six (requirements 297, 303 and 305). Decision 145's reopen clause, a run that groups differently from the rule, would move the split into a script; the floor and the cap are two short rules a worked example makes exact, so the split stays prose. Not taken: a cap on findings per group through `--max-findings`, which decision 125 keeps off. Reopens: a defect across two merged small groups that the merged review misses, or a run that groups differently from the rule, which moves the split into a script.
195. **The gate gains a reader that returns evidence per finding.** Step 1.6 told the orchestrator to read each finding against the code it cites, with no role to share the reading; a pass of 245 findings, all new, would have filled its context, so the run used read-only readers outside the skill's text. A reader role now runs when a pass has more than 30 findings to sort: read-only subagents at `models.reader`, `sonnet` by default, one per batch of at most 40, in parallel, each returning per finding whether it holds, its kind, whether it is pre-existing at the merge-base, a decision or rule it conflicts with, and one line of evidence, never a bucket. The buckets stay the orchestrator's, who reads the cited code itself for every finding it puts in `fix` and every one a reader marked unsure (requirements 299, 300 and 313). Not taken: a cap on findings per group, which decision 125 keeps off. Reopens: a fix the orchestrator took from a reader's evidence without reading the code, or a reader's `holds: no` on a finding a later pass shows real.
196. **The fix round compares the changed files with its groups again before it stages.** Reading the fix restored any file outside the groups' files, but the checks ran after that reading and the commit staged the paths the round changed, so a build check that regenerated a tracked file left it modified and the fix commit took it; a follow-up commit restored it. Before staging, the changed paths are compared with the groups' files again, and a path outside them is restored as a snapshot restores one and named in the report (requirement 300). Reopens: a check whose rewritten file the fix needs, which would make it part of a group.
197. **A publish step writes its body file first and runs the post alone.** The loop's and the gate's publish steps said to write a body file and post it with `--body-file`, without saying the two are separate calls, and runs combined them into one compound command; a classifier that sees commands and not their output could not tell the text was the body the run had already written and shown, and six of seven denials in one set of runs had that shape. Decision 181 named this a matter for those steps. Each publish step now says the body is written with the Write tool in its own call and the publishing command runs alone in its own Bash call, naming only the absolute path (requirements 116, 118 and 314). Whether the post alone is allowed in auto mode was not checked. Reopens: a post alone, after a separate write, still denied.
198. **The Coverage line of stage 8 is one fixed sentence.** The report's Coverage section asked for each stage 1 to 8 and the checks it accepted, but stage 8's read-only check runs after the report is hashed, so its result cannot be in the report; in one run the orchestrator invented a wording for it in a hashed file. Coverage now lists stages 1 to 7, and stage 8's line is a fixed sentence naming `baseline/8-check.md`, where the result lives (requirement 160). Reopens: none expected; a later stage after the hash would join the sentence.
199. **Section 3 names its cut by tier.** The report's section 3 asked for every Verified OK item of pass one with no cut; a medium run over nine scopes had 115, and the orchestrator cut each to its first 150 characters with a pointer, a form the template did not allow, in a hashed file. At `low` every item stays in full; at `medium` and `high` a challenged item is in full and an item not challenged is its first line, cut at 150 characters, with its pass-one file, grouped by scope with the scope's count (requirement 160). Reopens: a challenged set too long for the section, which would cut challenged items as well.
200. **The run id's slug is the repository name with the pull request number or the branch.** The slug rule, "the first bundle's name and its pull request number, or else its branch", read two ways for a branch bundle, and runs on one machine used both, so a lookup in `runs.json` by a guessed id missed. The slug is the first bundle's repository name, then the pull request number or the branch, slugged and cut at 40 characters, with a worked example of each in stage 1 and the skill (requirement 176). Reopens: none expected.
201. **A `file:` ticket's slug is its export's `id`.** `<ticket>` in file names was the normalized id slugged, which for a `file:` ticket is its absolute path, so a group id ran to the length of the path; one run used the export's `id` instead and said so in its brief. The slug of a `file:` ticket is now the export's `id`, else the file's base name without its extension, while the normalized id stays its identity (requirement 138). Reopens: two exports of one bundle with one `id`.
202. **A finished run takes live results after a plugin update.** Resume compared stage 1's recorded `plugin_version` with the current one and any other value invalidated stage 1, and `--live` refuses a rerun that starts at stage 5 or before; an update removes the old version's cache, so a run finished on one version could not take live results on the next, and the only path was a full rerun, about 4 million tokens on the run on record, while live results usually come back hours or days later, when updates land. With `--live`, a recorded version other than the current one no longer invalidates stage 1 when every other input of stage 1 is unchanged; the final entry of the first rerun stage records both versions and the report's Coverage names them, since stages 6 to 8 then run on the new version's text over the old version's outputs, and `ledger.sh check` still refuses an output it cannot read. Without `--live` the rule stands. The `plugin_version` literal stays where lint checks it and the release script moves it, so both stay as they are (requirements 161, 189, 219 and 221). Not taken: a separate output format version, which no check could keep in step with the text. Reopens: an update between a run and its live results that changes a format stages 6 to 8 read.
203. **The risk floor asks whether a read-only companion of a data change reads the rows the write touches.** A data repair across every tenant kept a read-only listing as its rollback record, and the plan and its review accepted a listing whose header set one tenant under row-level security, so it saw one tenant while the repair touched all; only the final review caught it, and no run reported a wrong result. The risk floor now asks, for a schema, migration or data access change, whether every read-only companion reads the same rows the write touches, and the plan reviewer's request asks the same; the floor's six triggers stay six (requirements 66 and 69). Reopens: a companion that reads different rows and that the plan review still accepts.
204. **The refactor skill names the Bash tool for its `git` reads.** The `/rules` skill pre-approves its script as a Bash call and says to run it with the Bash tool (decision 177), since a Windows session ran it through PowerShell first, which the grant does not cover. The refactor skill grants eight `git` reads as Bash forms and named no tool, so the same session could prompt on each read. It now says to run each `git` read with the Bash tool (requirement 330). Not taken: a `PowerShell(...)` entry, which the host documents for settings but not for a skill's `allowed-tools`. That a Windows run makes no PowerShell call for these reads was not checked. Reopens: a Windows run that still makes one.
205. **The gate's child review runs at `models.reviewer`, `opus` by default, passed as `--model` on every run.** Two runs of four passes each over one pull request, from a session on `opus` with a user settings file that set `fable`, ran every child review on `fable`, two of them with a small `haiku` share, about $68 for the eight reviews, and nothing the gate printed named the model before or after either run; `reviews[].models` (requirement 311) names it only once a review has run. Decision 182 read the child as taking "the model the session or its settings set"; the runs show it takes the settings model and never the session's, so leaving out `--model` honored a choice made for interactive sessions, not one made for the gate. `gate.models.reviewer`, default `opus`, is now passed to every child session as `--model`, and the reading block's Engine line names it and the stand-in's model, each with its source when it is not the default, before the first review starts (requirements 291, 305 and 313). This reverses, on its own reopen clause, decision 182's "Not changed" for the gate's reviewer, and decision 184's "Not taken" of the same key. The default is `opus` and not the settings model, since the block must name the model from the gate's own inputs: without `--model` the child's model comes from the user settings file, the repository's two settings files, managed settings or `ANTHROPIC_MODEL`, by an order the host may change, and a name inferred from them could be wrong as silently as these runs were. `opus` also keeps the top tier an override by name, as decision 182 does for every other default, and matches the stand-in's default. The one stand-in review of a group that met a usage limit runs at `models.fallback-reviewer` when it differs from `models.reviewer`, else at `sonnet`, or at `opus` when the reviewer is on `sonnet`, since with both keys at `opus` the retry would meet the limit it retries, as the loop's swap rule of decision 182 already says, and its `reviews[]` entry names the model it was called at, since a stand-in's text carries no `modelUsage` (requirements 306 and 311). The Engine line names the model each child starts on; in one of the two reviews of this change at `--model opus`, `modelUsage` also named `fable` for about two fifths of the cost, by a part of the review the flag did not set, its source not traced, so `reviews[].models` stays the record of what a review spent. The cost: a user whose settings choose `sonnet` gets `opus` reviews until `models.reviewer` says `sonnet`, and an account that cannot use `opus` fails every review until the key names one it can. Not taken: `--model` only when `models.reviewer` is set, which leaves a run with no key on whatever the settings set, the case of these runs, and makes the Engine line a reading of files the gate does not own. The key accepts the four values the other `models` keys accept; the host's `--model` help names `fable`, `opus` and `sonnet` as aliases, and `haiku` as a child's `--model` was not run. Reopens: users who want the reviews to follow the settings model and set the key to match it, which would add a value that sends no `--model`; a host release whose `claude -p` does not apply `--model` to a skill run.
206. **A cross-file candidate's identifier must look like code.** On a copy of one pull request's ledger of 59 settled entries, a new finding on an unrelated file, "parseRemote returns `null` for a GitHub URL with a trailing slash", drew 5 cross-file candidates; none was the same defect, and each shared only `null` or `GitHub`. The rule of decision 185 took the content of every backticked span and every word with a lowercase letter or digit before a capital, so a plain word in backticks and a product name counted. A camelCase word now starts with a lowercase letter, and a backticked span counts only when it is a camelCase or snake_case word or holds a character other than a letter, a digit or a space, so `null`, `true`, `main`, `JSON`, GitHub and PowerShell drop out while `candidatesFor`, `load_config`, `findings.mjs` and `max-passes` stay (requirement 306). The cost: a PascalCase name, a class or a type, no longer makes a candidate unless its span holds another character, as `new Fail(` does; `macOS` and `iPhone` still count, each starting lowercase before a capital, and so does a backticked span with punctuation, such as `Node.js` or `===`. Not taken: a list of product names, which needs upkeep and misses the next one; a span rule that keeps only `.`, `/`, `(` and `_`, which drops kebab-case keys and flags such as `max-passes` and `--check`, the gate's own configuration words. Decision 185's reopen clause is what happened: a list long enough that reading it costs more than the matches it offers. Reopens: a missed match on a PascalCase name, or a cross-file list still long enough that the triage stops reading it.
207. **Guard runs no startup check in a print-mode session.** With a repository listed, every session start launched `claude auto-mode defaults`, about 0.3 s a call with the native install and two launches through the npm shim on Windows, including each `claude -p` child that the gate and the loop start, six for a split pass of six. Checked first, on 2.1.318: a `SessionStart` hook in a scratch configuration directory's `settings.json` did not fire under `claude auto-mode defaults` in three runs, and fired under `claude -p` in the same directory with `source` `startup`, so the startup hook does not call itself. The hook's environment held `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ATTENDED` `0` in a `claude -p` session and `1` in an interactive one, and `0` in a `claude -p` session that inherited the entry point `claude-desktop` or `claude-vscode`; `CLAUDE_CODE_ENTRYPOINT` is no signal, since the host rewrites an inherited entry point to `sdk-cli` only when it is `cli`, so a child of a desktop or IDE session keeps its parent's. `session-start` now prints nothing and starts no `claude` when the attended flag is `0`, before it reads the list (requirement 341). The hooks reference does not name the flag, so a host release that drops or renames it brings the launch back, never a notice missed in an interactive session. The cost: a headless run that reads `--output-format stream-json`, where a startup notice can show, as acceptance item 4 records for the rules notice, no longer gets guard's; an interactive session still does. Not taken: a recorded host version compared at startup, which decision 192's reopen clause named, since the host exposes no version that can be read without a launch, and a version read from the install's layout is the dependence decision 190 refused; a recorded identity of the `claude` executable, which on an npm install on Windows is a shim whose bytes do not change between versions, so a stale state would go unannounced. Reopens: a host that exposes its version without a launch, or interactive startups slowed by the defaults call.
208. **Concise Plain 4.13: a revised draft comes back whole, even after feedback on one paragraph.** Field feedback of 2026-11-06 on two chat status messages drafted under 4.12, read in a two-pass review the same day, the second pass challenging the first. In one session the two messages were revised over about a dozen turns; after feedback on one paragraph the revision came back as that paragraph four times, and the owner asked for the whole draft each time. Decision 156's reopen clause, "a revision that still comes back as a delta", fires, and this time its named remedy is taken: the opening paragraph's "fewest lines" sentence now says a revised draft comes back whole, beside the exemption for a document. Decision 183's third change, which allowed the one paragraph asked for, is narrowed: the Drafts bullet returns the whole draft even when the feedback was on one paragraph, and one paragraph alone only when the owner asks to see just that paragraph. The costs are lopsided: a whole draft when a paragraph was wanted costs reading, a paragraph when the whole was wanted costs a turn. Decision 183's record of two accepted paragraph returns under 4.10 and 4.11 shows the pull toward a delta predates the carve-out, so the carve-out did not cause the failure; it made the failure accepted behavior. Three other reopen clauses fired with no change, each answered. Decision 183's "an ask still put to the reader for a decision they do not own" fired in both messages' first drafts; the transcript shows every such ask was copied from an earlier session's handoff, which had written unsettled items as asks to the readers, and two of them had been put to the owner as recommendations and approved before the draft. The settle bullet and the house rules' decision item already state the fix, so no line changes; the mechanism is the one decision 183 named, a draft written down from what the session holds, one session further up. Decision 156's first clause, "an id or count its reader will not use", probably fired on a test pass count that reached the last draft, one sighting under a reader bullet that already excludes it, so no change; decision 158's "pass count" is a review-pass count under decision 141, so decision 158 does not fire. Decision 180's register clause fired on its face for two paragraphs judged hard to follow, with the thread's earlier messages in context; that is one sighting on a judgment of tone, so no change.
