Not taken: a table rule for a two-key lookup, since the existing exception for rows the reader must compare already allowed it; an id beside its short name, which the line on calling a thing what its reader calls it already states, and the Links line does not compete with it, since the id was linked as told; widening the status-note clause to readers who open the code, on one sighting; a per-paragraph reader level, which decision 183 declined; a drafting subagent fed the readers and the facts instead of the run notes, since the asks came from the handoff that such a subagent would also read, and the style, which the chat block mirrors, cannot carry a procedure; and a list of draft kinds, since the reader bullet already names a status note and decision 183 applied the section to an email. The chat block carries both changes word for word, so its list of deliberate differences does not change. This change leaves the house-rule count at 36; decision 209 moves it. Settled 2026-11-06 by the owner. Reopens: a paragraph-only revision under 4.13 after feedback on one paragraph, which would point at the "fewest lines" opening and a drafting skill outside the style; a status note to readers who open the code that again carries how the result was reached, which would widen the status-note clause to every status note; a first draft that again puts an ask the sender could settle, with no handoff upstream of it.
209. **House-rule edit twenty: a credential never sits in a file.** Field feedback of 2026-11-06, read in the same two-pass review as decision 208. A read-only reviewer subagent, briefed from a handoff that did not repeat the scratch rules, wrote a bearer token to a temporary file and reported it. The house rules reach a subagent through the instruction file, as the host documents and one subagent's own context showed, checked for one agent type only; the output style, which holds "Redact credentials", does not, and that line is about quoted output. Nothing in the house rules said what may go into a scratch file, and the scratch item keeps such a file until the owner says yes, so the token would have stayed on disk. A new Working item, directly after the scratch item, says a credential never sits in a file, a scratch file included, and reaches a command through the environment or a tool's own store (requirement 345). The item names the credential at rest rather than banning credentials in commands, since a flat ban pushes a token onto a command line, where it lands in the transcript and the process list. The chat block carries a `core.md` item only where it shapes a reply, as with requirement 269's item and the decision rule, so it does not carry this one. The house-rule count moves from 36 to 37. Not taken: a delegation principle for a session on a scarce tier, on one instance with no usage numbers, while the plugins' defaults already keep the top tier an override by name (decisions 182 and 205); a once-per-session grant form, with no instance recorded; and a handoff-writing skill, on one instance, which overlaps the audit's handoff skill. Settled 2026-11-06 by the owner. Reopens: a credential that reaches a file because a tool needs a token file, which would name the environment route in the item; a second session on a scarce tier with usage numbers showing that delegation saved it at equal quality, which would raise the delegation principle again.
210. **Lint compares the chat block's version line with the style's.** Lint pinned the style's version by a literal and checked only the chat block's fence count, so a style bump that forgot the block's "Kept in step with" line would pass every check, and the pasted block would name a version it does not match; the review of the 4.13 change raised this on every pass, though no bump has drifted yet. Lint now reads the version the style's description ends in and fails when the chat block's "Kept in step with" line names another as "(Concise Plain v<x>)", or names none, which the two fence-count cases now also show (requirement 44). The check reads the version from the style rather than from a second literal in lint, so a bump moves no new literal there; the new test cases pin the current version as the R43 case does, and a bump moves them with it. The architecture tree drops its copy of the version, which had stayed at 4.5 since no bump moved it. Not taken: a word-for-word comparison of the chat block's bullets with the style's, since the block keeps deliberate differences (decision 111) that such a check would have to list. Reopens: a chat bullet that drifts from the style while its version line matches.
211. **House-rule edit twenty-one and a handoff prompt skill: a handoff prompt has one definition in the suite.** The same request, a prompt that carries work from one session to a later one, from a summary of a discussion or from a single idea not yet worked out, has been restated across many sessions, each time describing the same prompt. Nothing in the engine defines it, so each session wrote its own reading of it, and the failure of decision 208 is what an undefined handoff produces: a handoff that wrote unsettled items as asks to its readers, which later sessions copied. A new Code item, directly after the item on prose written for an agent, defines a handoff prompt as carrying the context and background, the user's settled decisions and open questions as left, and nothing more specific than the user was, and says it suggests nothing, asks nothing of anyone else and leaves the thinking to the receiving session (requirement 346). The chat block carries the item as the last line of its Content and names it among its deliberate differences, since a handoff often starts in chat, so the style stays at 4.13 and the "Kept in step with" line does not change. A new skill, `/rules:handoff-prompt`, writes the prompt: it reads the item from `core.md` at each run, takes from the request whether a summary or an idea travels, gives an idea as the idea and marked as not worked out, gives a summary what was settled, the facts it rests on with when and how each was checked, and the open questions for the user, names for a receiver that can open files the repository, branch, commit and files, gives a receiver that cannot open files no paths, defines or replaces a term the session coined, and ends in one fenced block with a line on what was left out or is unverified (requirement 347). This reopens decision 209's "Not taken: ... a handoff-writing skill, on one instance, which overlaps the audit's handoff skill". The instance count was the reason it was left, and it has changed: the request recurs across many sessions. The overlap was real in the word and not in the thing: the audit's handoff is a typed file of claims that an audit reads and checks, written from a build session, and a handoff prompt is prose for a session to act on, so the two share a word and no output. The name is `handoff-prompt` and not `handoff`, so the typed command is not `/rules:handoff` beside `/audit:handoff` with a different meaning, and the skill's description says it is not the typed handoff file an audit reads. The skill is model-invocable, with no `disable-model-invocation`, against decision 167's pattern for the refactor skill, which is user-only so that its description costs no startup context: a plain request for a handoff prompt can reach only a skill the model can invoke, and a skill that needs the typed command would not answer the request that prompted it. The cost is its description in every session's startup context, against requirement 274's cap, which records and does not yet enforce; lint's user-only check reads the `/rules` and `/guard` skills alone, so nothing blocks it. It sets no `allowed-tools`, since it runs no command and reads one file, and no `argument-hint`, since the request carries what travels; whether its read of `core.md` from the plugin directory prompts is unverified and is a line of the new acceptance item. The chat block carries the item by decision 209's rule, that it carries a `core.md` item where the item shapes a reply: a handoff prompt is a reply. The loop's typed `handoff.md` (requirements 120 and 137), the audit and `.claude/skills/rules-feedback/` do not change. The loop's file is the loop's own record and is not a prompt; a loop change is reopened by a loop run that needs a prompt where it writes a file. The audit's `/audit:handoff` stays as it is; an eval case loads the rules and audit plugins, asks for a handoff prompt for a later session, and asserts that `rules:handoff-prompt` fired and `audit:handoff` did not (requirement 273), and a misroute in that case reopens the audit command's description, an audit release outside this change. The `rules-feedback` skill's scope already covers `core.md` and the chat block, so the new item and its chat line fold through it as any other. Whether the eval's trace pattern scores 0 without the plugins, and whether a case may load two plugins, is unverified until acceptance item 38 runs it. The house-rule count moves from 37 to 38. Not taken: the name `handoff`; a user-only skill, which no plain request reaches; a handoff prompt that proposes a next step or an approach for the receiver, which the item forbids and decision 208 names as the failure; writing the prompt to a file, which the receiver would have to be pointed at and which leaves a scratch file to clear. Settled 2026-11-07 by the owner. Reopens: an eval or a run in which a plain request for a handoff prompt reaches the audit's handoff and not this skill, which reopens the audit command's description; a startup-cost reading that puts this description over the cap of requirement 274, which reopens model invocation; a handoff prompt that still carries an ask or a suggestion with this item in force, which points at the item's wording and not at the skill.
212. **The handoff prompt skill carries its definition: a skill read of the rule file asks for permission.** Decision 211 had the skill read the handoff prompt item from the plugin's `core.md` at each run and left whether that read prompts unverified. Acceptance item 81 settled it. In a permission mode that asks before such a read, the read asked once per session; where no one can answer, as in a non-interactive run, it was refused, and the prompt was written without the definition, which is the undefined handoff decision 211 was made to end. Listing the read under `allowed-tools` did not fix it: the model-invoked call of the skill was itself refused there, and each session wrote its own prompt without the skill, one of them with a plan ending in a recommendation, which the item forbids. The skill now carries the handoff prompt item and the item on prose written for an agent word for word, each on one line, and asks for no read of the plugin's files (requirement 347); the second is carried since a handoff prompt is prose written for an agent and the house rules block may be absent or stale in the user's instruction file. A test pins each copy to `core.md` as `units()` normalizes it, so a change to either item that leaves the skill behind fails (requirements 324 and 346). The `rules-feedback` skill names the skill's copy beside the chat block's when it proposes a change to either item. This reverses decision 211's "reads the item from `core.md` at each run". It narrows decision 211's "one definition in the suite" to one definition with two pinned copies, the chat block's and the skill's. It reverses its "`.claude/skills/rules-feedback/` do not change" and its "The `rules-feedback` skill's scope already covers `core.md` and the chat block", since the skill's copy is now a third place a change folds into, and its "whether its read of `core.md` from the plugin directory prompts is unverified", now settled. The cost is two more copies of house-rule text, held to `core.md` by the test and not by a single source. Reads the session makes of the repository to name the branch, commit and files are the session's and not the skill's, and acceptance item 81 says so. Not taken: `allowed-tools`, for the failure above; keeping the read and its prompt, since a non-interactive run loses the definition. Settled 2026-11-07 by the owner. Reopens: a host that lets a plugin skill read its own directory without a prompt, which would allow one source again.
214. **Primer's notice finds a memory directory that `autoMemoryDirectory` or `CLAUDE_CODE_PROJECT_DIR_NAME` moved.** Decision 83 fixed the directory at `<config>/projects/<key>/memory/` with the key from the main worktree. The host moves it two ways: `autoMemoryDirectory`, an absolute or `~/` path read from any settings scope, and `CLAUDE_CODE_PROJECT_DIR_NAME` beside `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, which names the project directory of the transcripts and the memory alike (architecture, host facts). With either set the notice looked in the derived directory, found no `MEMORY.md` and printed nothing, so the notes it exists to count were never counted. Two runs showed the session's instructions name the moved directory, the `autoMemoryDirectory` one when both are set, so promotion, which reads the directory the instructions name first, already read the right one; its fallback, used when they name none, and the hook did not. The hook now takes `autoMemoryDirectory` from `.claude/settings.local.json`, then `.claude/settings.json`, then `<config>/settings.json`, the first file that sets the key deciding, as the host takes the value of the highest scope that sets it and then validates it, so a value that is not an absolute or `~/` path counts as unset and the lower files are not read, with `~/` read against the host's home folder, `USERPROFILE` on Windows and `HOME` elsewhere; else the transcript's own directory when its name is `$CLAUDE_CODE_PROJECT_DIR_NAME`, since the host stores the transcript under a name it took and under the derived key otherwise, which covers an unset `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and an invalid name with no list of device names; else the derived directory (requirements 256 and 258). Promotion's fallback takes the same order and judges the name by the host's rule. The review stamp keeps the derived key. The costs: a key set in managed settings or by `--settings` is out of the hook's reach, so there the notice still reads the derived directory; a project or local value the host refuses under workspace trust or `permissions.blockReadsOutsideWorkingDirectories` is still read; and the key is found in the settings text by a pattern, not a JSON parser, since requirement 265 keeps the hook to sh, sed and awk. Not taken: reading the managed settings path of each system, for a key an organization rarely moves. Reopens: a host release that gives the memory directory in the hook input, which would replace the lookup; or a notice missed under managed settings.
215. **A skill's `allowed-tools` grant lasts the turn that invokes it.** The skills page says the listed tools are usable without asking during the turn that invokes the skill and that the grant clears at the user's next message; acceptance item 73 showed it, where the yes turn asked to approve a `git check-ignore` the refactor skill listed. Requirement 59 said the loop's list "pre-approves", decision 166 that fold's version read "prompts once", and `shapes.md` with requirement 254 told primer to add `allowed-tools` for "read-only commands every run needs", each reading the grant as lasting the run. The architecture records the host fact; requirement 59 says the grant lasts the invoking turn; decision 166 says fold's version read prompts because no grant covers it; `shapes.md` and requirement 254 keep `allowed-tools` for the reads of the invoking turn and say a grant for the session is a permission allow rule in the user's settings, which primer never writes; placement item 2 keeps an allow entry out of its proposals, since it enforces nothing. The loop's, the gate's and the audit's lists do not change: their reads in the invoking turn still go unprompted, and the grant does not cover a later read. Unverified: whether a gate run in the default mode prompts for a `git` read after the user's first reply; the check is `/gate` in the default mode, one question answered, and the next `git status` watched. Reopens: that check showing a prompt the gate's permission statement does not predict, which would move its reads to allow rules the README proposes.
216. **The gate asks before a push that triggers a deploy.** The loop judges before it pushes whether the push or its pull request triggers a deploy, from the workflow files of the base branch and of the branch being pushed, and asks first when one does (requirement 56). The gate pushes fix commits to a target's branch, once a pass, and its always-ask list (requirement 294) named force push, `--no-verify`, merge, rebase, amend, reset, repository settings and a new issue, and no deploy; the word appeared nowhere in the gate's files or in these decisions, so no decision left it out. The gate records the ask-first rules of the instruction files, so a rule that asks before a deploy was recorded, but nothing had the gate judge whether a push deploys, so the rule had nothing to apply to. The gap was found by reading the two skills; no run showed it, and none was made, since the text alone shows the judgment is missing. The gate now makes the loop's judgment before each push, in the target's repository, since a manifest's bundles may sit in different repositories, each with its own workflows, and "repo <path>" names a repository other than the session's; it judges `pull_request` triggers as well when the branch has an open pull request, since a push to its head branch starts them, and counts one on another base than the run's as unsettled, since the run never read that base's workflows. It judges before every push and not once at preflight, since a fix round may change a workflow file. Each push that triggers a deploy asks, and a yes covers that push only, so a run of four passes may ask four times: what a push deploys can change with any file a fix round touches, a workflow, a local action, a script or a path a filter reads, and nothing short of judging the push again shows it did not. A workflow whose deploy the files cannot settle counts as one that deploys, as the permission statement counts an outcome it cannot determine as a prompt; it is the one rule the gate adds to the loop's judgment, whose filters it reads as GitHub applies them to the push. Outside "no fixes" mode the permission statement says the deploy is judged before each push. The reads take only the `.yml` and `.yaml` files directly in `.github/workflows`, which are the ones GitHub runs, and list with `--full-tree`, since `git ls-tree` otherwise reads its path from the current folder and lists nothing from a subfolder, and a path it prints in quotes is unquoted before `git show` reads it. `git ls-tree` joins the allowed-tools reads beside `git show`, so the plain forms of the judgment's reads do not prompt in the invoking turn (decision 215). The always-ask list is a class apart from the loop's risk floor and the gate's review level, which raise the scrutiny of the work on a branch: an always-ask action prompts whatever a score or a level says, so the deploy ask is never gated on either. The cost: a refused deploy is now an ordinary end, and it leaves the pass's commit local, which the next run's preflight blocks on as a branch ahead of its remote and which Resume does not push, so that commit is pushed or dropped by hand. Not taken: one judgment at preflight, which misses a workflow a fix round changes; and a yes carried to later pushes, by the names of their workflows or while the workflow files are unchanged, since a fix round can change what a push deploys through a workflow's content, a filter's paths, a local action or a script. Reopens: a gate run that pushes into a deploy without asking, or that asks on a push whose workflows deploy nothing. Amended 2026-11-09: the next run pushes the kept commit when its parent is still the remote head (decision 219), so a refused deploy no longer leaves that commit to push or drop by hand. Decision 325 adds a question before the first push to a host whose files cannot show a deploy.
217. **The loop judges a deploy from the workflows GitHub runs, listed from the root, before each push.** Step 7.2 listed the workflow files with `git ls-tree -r` and no `--full-tree`, which reads its path from the current folder, so from a subfolder it listed nothing, as a run from `docs/` showed, and the loop judged no deploy and pushed without asking. The loop runs its git commands in the session's directory unless that is another checkout, and its preflight does not require the session's repository to be read from its root, so a session started in a subfolder lists from there; that the loop takes such a folder was found by reading, and no loop run showed it. The loop now lists with `--full-tree`, reads only the `.yml` and `.yaml` files directly in `.github/workflows`, which are the ones GitHub runs, unquotes a path `git ls-tree` prints in quotes before `git show` reads it, reads trigger filters as GitHub applies them, and counts a workflow whose deploy the files cannot settle as one that deploys, as the gate does (decision 216), so the gate no longer adds that rule to the loop's judgment. A CI repair push is judged as the first push is, since a repair can change a workflow, and `git ls-tree` joins the allowed-tools reads beside `git show`, as decision 215 gave the gate. The cost: a refused deploy on a CI repair push ends the run `blocked` with the repair commit local, which the next loop run blocks on as a branch ahead of its remote, so that commit is pushed or dropped by hand, as decision 216 records for the gate; asking still beats a repair that deploys unasked. Two of the gate's rules are not taken: the loop judges `pull_request` triggers on every push, since each push is followed by the pull request it opens or one already open, and an open pull request on another base already ends the run `blocked` before the first push. Reopens: a loop run that pushes into a deploy without asking, or that asks on a push whose workflows deploy nothing.
218. **The loop resolves the session's repository to its toplevel.** Step 0 checked `<checkout>` with `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` only for "primary <path>"; otherwise `<checkout>` was the session's repository, and nothing made it the toplevel, so a session started in a subfolder could take that folder. That the loop takes it was found by reading, and no loop run showed it. A run of the preflight's reads from `docs/` showed what follows: `git check-ignore -q .reimagine/` exited 0, so the record would be made at `docs/.reimagine/loop/<branch-slug>/`, where a run from the root finds no record and does not resume; `git ls-files -v` listed only the files under `docs/`, so a skip-worktree file at the root was never compared and the worktree run of requirement 80 could not start; and `git rev-parse --git-path info/exclude` printed `../.git/info/exclude`, relative to that folder. Decision 217 fixed one such read, the deploy listing. Step 0 now takes `<checkout>` from `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` in every run and ends `blocked` when it fails, as it does from inside `.git`, where the command exited 128. A non-empty `git rev-parse --show-prefix` means the session started below the toplevel; the primary's commands then run as `git -C <checkout>` and `cd <checkout> && <command>`, the forms of a named checkout, since the host returns the shell to the session's start folder after a call that ends outside it (decision 153), so a `cd` to the root does not carry, and the permission statement counts them as prompts. The prefix decides, not a comparison of paths, since decision 153 found that a string comparison fails a valid toplevel typed in another case or as `/c/...`. The record root stays the session's toplevel, even in a linked worktree, where only "primary <path>" moves it to the main checkout, so a run from a subfolder and one from the root of the same worktree share a record. Step 0.1 reads `CLAUDE.md` and `.claude/CLAUDE.md` at the toplevel and in each folder from the session's folder up to it, since the host loads the instruction files of the start folder and of every folder above it at launch, as its memory page states, so a rule in the start folder still binds the run. Step 5 keeps `code-review`, since the primary is still the session's checkout: run from a subfolder over a change to a file outside it, `code-review` reported the change's defect at its root-relative path and line, as a run from the root did. The exclude path that `git rev-parse --git-path info/exclude` prints is read against the folder `git -C` names when it is not absolute, as the gate reads it (decision 153), since it is relative to that folder, not to the session's. The handoff and the audit manifest name the primary's repository as `<checkout>`, since they gave a rule only for a worktree run, and a run from a subfolder could hand the gate and the audit that folder. Not taken: ending the run `blocked` from a subfolder and naming "primary <toplevel>", which works with the same forms but makes the user rerun for no gain, and in a linked worktree would move the record to the main checkout. Reopens: a loop run from a subfolder whose record or commands still read that folder.
219. **The next gate run pushes the commit a refused push kept.** A gate push refused by the permission mode, the user or the deploy question ends the run `blocked` with the pass `failed` and its commit local (decision 216). No later run pushed it: preflight blocked on a local branch ahead of its remote, Resume would have continued a pass whose commit was the head at its next-level decision, which skips the push, and the record did not name the commit, since only the write after the push held `commit`. So the commit was pushed or dropped by hand. Found by reading; no run showed it. A `blocked` ending after the commit now writes its sha as the failed pass's `commit`. Preflight lets through, in every mode, a local branch one commit ahead whose parent is the remote head and whose tip is that sha, once it has checked that the record is the branch's own, since it reads the record before the slug check. A commit that a run entry names as `kept` or a complete pass names as `head` is never pushed again: either shows a run pushed it, and one removed from the remote after that was removed on purpose. Resume, outside "no fixes", pushes that commit first, with the deploy judged and asked as before any push. A branch with no remote head has no kept commit, and its next run reviews it as a new head, as before, since the record cannot tell a branch never pushed from one deleted after a merge. Resume writes the sha as `kept` in its own run entry, so that the report and the comment list the commit and the findings it fixed from the record. Then it starts a new pass sequence at `xhigh`, or restarts on a changed merge-base, since no pass reviewed the branch with the commit on it. Resume reads a failed pass that names a commit as absent and finds it by its sha, so it never resumes, closes or rewrites that pass, nor takes its commit as an expected head, and a record with no other pass starts a new pass sequence at `xhigh`. A "no fixes" run pushes nothing, so Resume ends it `blocked` on the commit, naming it, once the run entry exists; preflight cannot, since a run it ends has no run entry yet. The cost: one more review pass, at `xhigh`, of a commit a pass already made from reviewed findings; and a session that ends between the commit and the push, with no terminal state, still leaves the commit unrecorded, to be pushed or dropped by hand; a commit dropped by hand leaves its findings `fixed` in the ledger, so the next pass finds them again as regressions. Not taken: recording the commit in its pass and reopening or closing that pass on the next run, since the reopened pass has to meet every Resume item, a changed merge-base and a "no fixes" run, and finding it by its place in the record names another pass once an earlier pass is left not complete; a per-pass flag marking the commit pushed, which keeps the old pass's result and saves the extra pass but adds a state every Resume item reads, where the run's `kept` is not that flag, since it records only what the run pushed, for its report, and leaves the pass and its result alone; and writing `commit` as soon as the commit exists, which covers the ended session but leaves a `running` pass whose commit is the head, which Resume continues at its decision without the push. Reopens: a kept commit that a later run with fixes leaves unpushed, or a run that resumes, closes or rewrites the pass that kept it.
220. **House-rule edit twenty-two and Concise Plain 4.14: what leaves a session is written for its reader.** Three failures had one cause: output a session wrote for itself, or for the work it had just finished, reached readers it was not written for. A gate comment opened with a verdict word the team does not use and carried review levels and two commit ids; one more push made the ids stale, so a reviewer reading it later would have read a failed-sounding verdict on a head that had passed, and it was deleted by hand. A short status note to people outside the session came back one level too detailed on its first draft twice, carrying the coordination detail the session had just handled, though the Drafts section already said to write for the least technical reader; the accepted second draft was broader and lost nothing its readers act on. And across several loop runs, unfinished items sat as prose bullets in pull request descriptions: environment rechecks with a safety question marked unverified, a scheduled data read, design questions and a likely bug with no ticket, a change another pull request had already made, a check waiting on a fixture, a review with no owner. Nothing asked the owner to decide any of them and nothing filed one, so the ticket board never showed them, and the bug and the design questions would have been lost on merge. The owner settled a split by reader: run output for the agent or a later session stays in the run folder or a record attached to a ticket, which outside the session is the one place a plugin's or a session's own term may appear; a pull request carries what its reviewer needs about its code, in the team's words, true of its head; a ticket says what changed for its readers, what happens next and who decides, with technical detail in an attached record; output stays local unless publishing is the stated purpose or asked for, and before a post two checks hold, that its reader will act on it and that it holds nothing the reader would not say. The house rules block owns the text, as two Working items after the issue item (requirement 348), since a session meets them wherever it posts. Concise Plain 4.14 adds one Drafts bullet, that the detail level comes from the reader and not from what the session just did, and names the house rules for where a draft goes rather than restating them (requirement 43). The chat block takes the bullet's first two sentences and not the items, since a chat product posts nothing and has no house rules block, and its list of deliberate differences says so. Settled 2026-11-09 by the owner. The cost: two more house-rule items, about 190 words, in every session's context, and three copies of them (decision 221). Not taken: the rule in Concise Plain alone, which a session without the style never reads; restating the items there, which gives two texts to keep in step. Reopens: a post that still carries a term its reader does not use, or a draft that still comes back one level too detailed.
221. **The loop, the gate and the audit carry the two items word for word.** Each plugin works alone (requirement 9), and none read the rules plugin; each restated only its own ban on naming a model, agent, tool, pass or level, which is narrower than the split of decision 220. A skill that read `core.md` from the rules plugin's directory would need that plugin installed and, as decision 212 found for a skill's read of its own plugin's files, asks for permission and is refused where no one can answer. So each of the three `SKILL.md` files carries both items, each on one line, under `## What leaves the run`, and a test in the plugin's own test area pins each copy to `core.md` as `units()` normalizes it, so a change to either side that leaves the other fails that area's script (requirement 349). The `rules-feedback` skill names the three copies. Primer and guard post nothing and carry none. This extends decision 212's pattern from one skill to three plugins. The cost: three more copies of house-rule text, held by tests and not by one source, and about 190 more words in each skill's context. Not taken: one pin test under `tests/rules`, which a commit touching only a plugin's copy would not run. Settled 2026-11-09 by the owner for the copies; the tests' place is this decision's. Reopens: a host that lets a plugin read another plugin's file without a prompt, which would allow one source again.
222. **The gate posts nothing.** The gate posted one comment per run on each target pull request (requirement 314): a verdict, the findings fixed, deferred and rejected, the checks and the commits. That is run output for the user and a later session: its verdict word is the gate's own, it names commits by id, and it describes a head that the next push replaces while the comment stays. The owner settled that the gate never posts to a pull request. Requirement 314 is retired and requirement 350 added: the record and the report stay in the run directory and the report is the run's last message; the approval scope drops the comment (requirement 293), and the report loses its Comment section. This reverses decision 127's "publishes the deferred list in the comment and the report": the deferred list is in the report only, and still never in the body. Decision 219's listing of a kept commit and its findings now applies to the report alone. A test scans every file under `plugins/gate` for a posting command. The cost: a reviewer no longer sees on the pull request that a gate ran or what it found, beyond its fix commits, whose messages say why; the user relays what a reviewer needs. Acceptance item 50 is retired, and items 47 to 49, 53, 54 and 82 look for the report in place of the comment. Settled 2026-11-09 by the owner. Reopens: the owner asking for a gate result on the pull request, which would be reviewer text in the team's words that a later push replaces.
223. **The loop settles, puts to the owner, or files every open item.** Requirement 119 listed each deferred item in the pull request body and the report and let none become an issue, and Step 7.4 opened none, so an item nobody settled stayed as prose where the ticket board never shows it (decision 220). The owner settled the outcome: before a run ends, each open item is settled by the run with its evidence, when the same stretch of work can settle it; put to the owner as a decision in full; or filed to a ticket, existing or new, with the handoff context a later session needs. The body carries at most a one-line pointer to a ticket per item, an item resolved elsewhere is not listed, invoking the loop covers every ticket the run files with no ask per ticket, and a ticket the run creates follows the split, its body for its readers and its handoff context in an attached record. The rest was left open and is settled here (requirement 351). What counts as an open item: the house rules' judgment item decides which deferred findings are worth a ticket, those that affect correctness or risk or cost more to leave than to fix, and the rest are notes in the report only, so a run does not file a ticket for every reuse finding; a remainder of a partial input, a check someone must still run, an open plan objection and a question the run raised are open items, and a check deferred to CI is not, since the CI watch settles it. When: once per run, before any body or comment is written, after the first push, so a ticket is filed only on a run that publishes and each pointer has its reference; on a run that ends `prepared`, before its body file; and again for an item a CI repair pass adds, whose ticket one more body edit lists on a pull request the loop opened, since otherwise that item would reach no ticket. Settle or file: a run settles an item only inside the plan's files and criteria, with no input it lacks, within its budget and with no edit in Step 7, since a change after the review and the checks would go out unreviewed and anything wider is work the plan did not cover; an edit inside the plan belongs to Steps 4 and 5, whose fix bucket already takes it. New or existing: the tracker is searched first and an open ticket that covers the same symptom takes the item, as the house rules' issue item says, while a closed one is linked from a new ticket, since a comment on a closed ticket keeps the item off the board; an item a pull request resolves, or a ticket that already holds all it would add, is settled by naming where; a source ticket's own remainder stays on that ticket, which the body then only references, so the same work is not tracked twice. Decisions do not hold back publishing: invoking already approved the pull request, an unanswered question would leave a checked branch unpublished for as long as the owner takes, and the next run on the branch takes the answer up as another push; the cost is that a reviewer may read code a pending decision then changes. A run that ends `prepared` files nothing and writes drafts, so "do not publish", a denial before the push and a host the loop does not publish on (decision 7, requirement 226) still publish nothing; the cost is that on an Azure DevOps remote every ticket is a draft the owner files. An ask-first rule that covers a new issue or a comment asks once for all the run's tickets, since rule 1 keeps every ask-first rule and the owner settled that no ticket is asked for alone; an answer that is not a clear yes leaves each a draft and the run goes on, and so does a filing the permission mode refuses or that fails, never retried, since a run ending `blocked` publishes nothing and one follow-up ticket would otherwise hold back the pull request and every source-ticket comment. Every draft, on any run, is logged and listed in the report with the commands that file it and attach its record, and a run that ends `blocked` or `stopped` before the items are settled lists each as open, so no item is left with no ticket, no decision, no draft and no line in the report. This retires requirement 119, narrows requirement 56's new issue to one that is not an open item's ticket, adds the tickets and records to requirement 54, and reverses decision 141's "the ask-first rule on a new issue already makes each ticket the owner's call". The report gains Open items, in place of Deferred items, and Decisions for you (requirement 135), and the handoff's Raised tickets name the filed tickets (requirement 137). Settled 2026-11-09 by the owner for the outcomes and the approval. Reopens: an item a run filed that it could have settled, one item filed twice, or a reviewer held up by code a pending decision changed.
224. **A record attached to a ticket: one operation, one form per tracker.** The owner settled that technical detail travels with a ticket as an attached record, an issue artifact on GitHub and a work item attachment on Azure DevOps, behind one operation, with the run's record as the source of truth and the upload a copy, so a change to the preview loses nothing. GitHub's issue artifacts are in public preview and subject to change; they are created on the web, through the GitHub MCP server behind a feature flag, through `gh issue artifact`, which needs GitHub CLI 2.103.0 or later, or through the REST API. The loop uses the REST API through `gh api`, `POST repos/<owner>/<repo>/issues/<n>/artifacts` with a name and the record as its body, since that needs no particular gh version: a read of the same endpoint on an existing issue answered with an empty list through gh 2.91.0, older than the subcommand needs. One artifact per ticket and branch, updated on a later run with `PATCH .../artifacts/<number>`, which GitHub keeps as a new version, so a ticket's record stays one item. On Azure DevOps the REST API takes two calls, an upload to `_apis/wit/attachments` and a work item update adding an `AttachedFile` relation; `az boards` has no attachment command, and the Azure DevOps MCP server lists upload and link tools, so the loop attaches through those tools when listed and otherwise keeps the record local. Not taken: `az devops invoke` on the attachments route, since its route parameters and its handling of an uploaded file are not documented, and the loop ships no command it cannot name exactly. A failed, refused or impossible attach is not retried or done another way, and the report names the local record, which a later session can still read. Unverified: no artifact was created and no attachment uploaded, since both reach a forge; acceptance items 84 and 85 cover them, and the tests pin the commands as text, with no fixture or mock. Settled 2026-11-09 by the owner for the trackers and the source of truth. Reopens: a change to the preview API, which item 84 would show, or a documented `az` path for the attachment.
225. **The loop's pull request and ticket texts follow their readers.** The owner left open what the loop posts on a pull request, beyond the gate posting nothing. The inventory: of the six plugins only the loop and the gate wrote to a forge; the audit reaches one only through act's post of an item the owner names (decision 227); primer and guard post nothing. The loop's body, rewritten each run on a pull request it opened and once more after a CI repair push that makes it untrue, describes the current head, and its one comment per run on a continued pull request describes the commits that run pushed, which stay true of those commits; both are about the code and stay. Three texts were run output on a forge and change. Under `commit: true` the run posted the report's external form as its pull request comment (requirement 96); it now posts what it posts without the key, since the committed snapshot already carries the report. The ticket comment opened with "Status from the loop run on <branch>" and listed each criterion with a test name or a commit as evidence, the run's vocabulary and a reviewer's detail on a ticket whose readers act on what changed; it is now written for those readers, and the criteria with their evidence go in the ticket's record (requirements 118 and 352). And the publication texts' ban names terms the suite or the run coined beside models, agents, tools, passes and levels (requirement 11), in the loop's and the gate's commit messages as well; the body's Drift corrections section, named for the loop's own step, becomes Where the ticket and the code differ, and the line on commits from before the run no longer names the run (requirement 117). Under `commit: true` the snapshot still commits the plan and the report into the repository, cut to their external form; that is run output in the repository, kept because committing it is that key's stated purpose, and its external form drops Open items, since the snapshot is committed before any item is settled. Not taken: dropping the continued comment on a pull request the loop opened, whose body already covers every run, since the comment tells the reviewer what the new push changed and stays true of it. Reopens: a reviewer who reads a continued comment as stale, or a ticket reader who needs the evidence in the comment.
226. **The checks before a post are prose, held by tests.** The owner left open whether the two checks of decision 220, that a reader will act on a post and that it holds nothing they would not say, are enforced mechanically. Neither can be checked by a script: whether a reader acts on a text is a judgment about that reader, and a list of forbidden terms run by a hook on every `gh` or `az` call would block ordinary words such as pass, level or run that a team uses in its own sense, while missing a term a new run coins. So the checks are prose in the house rules items and their three copies, and the tests hold what text can hold: each copy matches `core.md`, the gate holds no posting command (requirement 350), and the loop's and the audit's posting texts hold their rules. Not taken: a guard hook, since guard decides what auto mode may run, not what a post says. Reopens: a post that leaks a coined term despite the copies, which a hook scoped to that term could then catch. Decision 316 adds a hook that checks a list the user writes, not the suite's coined terms, before a commit, a push and a post. Decision 321 adds a guard hook of the other kind, one that decides whether a call may run unasked and reads no post's wording.
227. **The audit's post and drafts follow the items; the audit attaches nothing.** The audit writes nothing to a forge except act's post of one item when the owner names it (requirement 194), and drafts ticket text in its report and in `work-items.jsonl` for a person to post. Both were bound only by the ban on model, agent and tool names, so a post or a draft could carry an item id such as `C3`, a claim number or a stage. They now follow the copied items (requirement 349): on a ticket, what changed for its readers, what happens next and who decides; on a pull request, what its reviewer needs about the code; no audit id, stage, verdict or other term the audit coined. The evidence and the ids stay in the run directory, which is the record a later session reads, so the audit needs no attach operation and requirement 227 keeps it without a forge adapter. `work-items.jsonl` keeps its `items` and `reason` fields, which are the audit's record and not posted text. Reopens: the owner asking act to post a record with an item.
228. **The record's artifact name is the branch slug, and a question for a ticket comment names the record.** Acceptance item 84 ran at loop 0.1.20, and every record attach on GitHub failed: `POST repos/<owner>/<repo>/issues/<n>/artifacts` with the name `Record for <branch>` answered HTTP 422, "name must start and end with a letter or number and contain only letters, numbers, spaces, periods, hyphens, and underscores", since the loop's branches hold a `/` (issue 159). The same call with only the `/` turned into `-` created the artifact, and a `PATCH` of its body kept a second version, which settles decision 224's unverified create and update. The name is now `Record for <branch-slug>`, the slug the record directory already uses (requirement 82), which holds only lowercase letters, digits and `-` and ends in a letter or digit, so it stays one artifact per ticket and branch; a later run finds the artifact by the number its record keeps, not by its name. In the same item the run's one question listed the commit, the push, the pull request and a comment on the source ticket, but not the record attached with that comment, and after a yes limited to the actions listed the run kept the record local as outside the approval; a later run's question listed a follow-up issue and no record (issue 160). The question now lists each ticket with its title, its tracker and the record attached to it, and any question an ask-first rule raises for a ticket comment, a source ticket's included, names the record attached with it (requirement 351), so a yes covers what the run does. The rerun of item 84 at the fixed name asked once and named no record for either ticket, though `SKILL.md`'s rule 1, always loaded, already named the record of an open item's ticket. Rule 1 now also names the record for any ticket comment, a source ticket's included. Why the run left the records out is not known, and a rerun at the branch head shows whether the rule is followed. Item 84 is rewritten: as written, its planted check was a criterion of the source ticket, which the loop keeps on that ticket and never files, and its second run, after a criterion the first run already met, took the path for a branch with no new work, which posts nothing. Its plant is now a check the repository asks for and the issue does not name, and its second run adds a second issue the same check covers. Not taken: dropping the attach on GitHub while issue artifacts are in public preview, since the local record stays the source of truth and a failed attach already leaves it named in the report. Settled 2026-11-09 by the owner. Reopens: a change to the artifact name rules, a run whose question names the record and is still read as not covering it, or a run whose question names no record.

229. **Guard's `apply` takes the id of the plan it writes, the denial record is bounded and redacted, and the settings reads entry is opt-in.** One `guard-plan.json` serves every session on a profile, and `apply` checked only that the settings file and the list were unchanged since the saved plan: a plan shown without a merge entry, then another session's `plan --add <path> --merge`, and the first session's `apply` wrote the merge entry its user never saw. `plan` now prints `plan: <id>`, twelve hex characters of the saved plan's SHA-256, and `apply <id>` refuses any other plan. The denial record took a reason, a path and a cwd of any length, never shrank, and kept `Authorization: Bearer <token>` from a denied command, the content decision 191 reopens on; each part is cut, the common credential forms are written as `[redacted]`, and the file keeps its newest 100 lines once it passes 256 KiB. The settings reads entry told the classifier, in every add, that reading the user settings file is not credential exploration, though `env` tokens sit in that file and a read lands in the transcript; it is written only while a repository is listed with `--reads`, which narrows decision 181, and a list from before the flag loses the entry at its next plan. Not taken: binding `apply` to a session id, which the script cannot read; dropping the command from a denial line, since the record exists to show what was denied; a full secret scanner, which the script's line budget does not hold. Reopens: a credential form recorded unredacted in a field run; a host that gives each session its own plugin data directory.

230. **The release script makes the two edits its skill left to a hand.** `node tools/release.mjs rules <version>` exited 0 with `lint: ok` while four tests of `tests/rules/rules.test.mjs` then failed on the shipped version typed as a literal, and the catalog's `metadata.version`, which decision 32 raises with every entry change, stayed where it was until someone moved it. The script now raises the last number of `metadata.version` in the same write and prints the step, and the rules tests read the shipped version from the manifest, so a release moves no line of a test; `pluginVersion` keeps its own test on a manifest with a literal version. Not taken: the script rewriting literals in a test file, which would leave them asserting nothing. Reopens: a release where the catalog version must move by more than one.

231. **The gate stops on a pass with nothing to fix, or on one that only corrects the last fixes.** Decision 130's sequence stopped clean only on a pass with no fix finding and no new rejection, and led a run down through `high` to a last `medium` pass. On the five runs recorded for this repository no pass met that rule: every run ended at a cap, about two thirds of the fix findings of later passes sat in files that the fix round before them had changed, and the rule that sent a run back to `xhigh` when a round touched over a quarter of the diff's files spent a second pass at the costliest level on small changes. The owner settled the stop as verify, then stop. A run ends when a pass has no fix finding, whatever it rejected, or when a pass after a fix round has at most 3 fix findings, all in files that round changed, and its own fixes are committed and pass every check, with at least one check run; a check run again after a check fix counts by its last result. A fix round records the files it changed for that test. The change kind `over-quarter` and the rules `after-medium` and `rejects-at-high` are gone, and a record that holds `over-quarter` is still read. A `pass` can now follow fixes that no review saw, so the report marks them `fixed, no review after` with the checks that passed. The second rule would have fired in three of the five runs; they ran under the old sequence, so the count is indicative. Not taken: stopping after one verifying pass whatever it finds, which gives `pass` on none of the five records and is the owner's to reopen. Reopens: a `fix-of-fix` stop that ships a defect its checks missed, or runs on larger repositories that converge under decision 130's rules.

232. **`split.mjs` groups the reviews of a pass, and `gate.parallel` sets how many run at once.** The grouping of decisions 145, 150 and 194 was a rule the orchestrator carried out by hand on every pass over `split`, about 740 words with worked examples, restated in four more places; a wrong grouping changes the names of the review files and what a resumed run reads, and nothing checked the result. `split.mjs` reads `git diff --numstat -z --no-renames` and prints the changed lines and each group with its slug and paths, and the worked examples are its test cases. A change over `split` that gives a single group is the one review of the whole range. Decision 194's six reviews at once was a fixed number, and six model sessions ran a machine out of memory: the pass was killed, and the next run reviewed every group again and was killed again. `gate.parallel`, 3 by default, sets the number, the review budget and the time a pass needs count their waves from it, and a resumed pass keeps each group's saved output that `findings.mjs` reads. Reopens: a grouping the script cannot express, or large changes that run past the review budget at 3.

233. **Every gate target runs against the root of its repository.** A session started in a subfolder ran the gate's git commands there. Git prints the paths of a status from the root and reads the paths of `git add` and `git checkout` from the current folder, so the first fix commit failed with "pathspec did not match any files" after the review and the fixes had run, and the record, `.reimagine.json` and the exclude path were looked up in the subfolder. Decision 218 settled the same for the loop. Every target now has a root, the toplevel of its repository, and a target whose root is not the session's folder runs in the forms decision 153 gave "repo <path>" and a manifest bundle. Reopens: a host that keeps a `cd` across calls.

234. **A fix commit is recorded before its push.** The pass entry got its `commit` after the push. A run that stopped between the two left a local branch one commit ahead with a record that did not name the commit, so preflight blocked every later run on the branch until the commit was pushed by hand, and a run that stopped after the push left the pass `running` for good. The commit is now written to the pass entry as soon as it exists. The next run lets through a tip that is the commit of a pass still `running`, as decision 219 lets through a failed one, pushes it under the same deploy and remote-head checks as any push, marks that pass's fixes `fixed`, and goes on to the decision; a "no fixes" run ends `blocked` on it. Reopens: a host that makes the commit and the record one step.

235. **The record and the report name the calls a review was refused.** A child review reaches for the checks itself, often in a form its allow list does not match, and on a run with no checks it can run none; four of the five recorded runs had none. The record kept a count per review, and the report printed the count in its log, so nothing said which commands the reviews could not run. `findings.mjs` now lists each refused call by tool and the first line of its command, cut at 120 characters, each once; the review's entry keeps the list, and the report's Checks section gives the total and each distinct call. Open: telling the child session the run's checks in its prompt, which needs a run to show that it helps. Reopens: a host that reports refused calls another way.

236. **The first pass of a run says how many passes its budget holds.** The run budget was first compared with the size of a pass at the decision after pass 1. On a large change one pass can take most of the budget, so a run fixed and pushed what pass 1 found and ended with no review of those fixes, having said nothing beforehand. Once the first pass is sized, the run prints the changed lines, the groups, the minutes a pass needs and the passes the budget holds, and when it holds one or none, that the fixes will be pushed with no review after them and how to raise the budget. Not taken: asking, which would stop a run nobody is watching. Reopens: field runs where the line is missed and a question is wanted.

237. **The report says when a fix push leaves a pull request's description behind.** The gate pushes fix commits to a pull request and edits nothing on it (decision 222), so the description describes the head before those commits, while the skill carries the rule that a description is replaced when a push makes it stale, and no part of the run said so. The report's Commits and pushes section now says that the description was written before these commits and what to bring up to date in it. Not taken: the gate editing the description, which decision 222 rules out. Reopens: the owner letting the gate edit a description.

238. **A reader names the earlier finding that a new one restates.** On a large change most findings of a later pass restate an earlier deferral or rejection in other words. The script carries a finding only on an exact identity, so each restatement arrived as new and the orchestrator matched every one by hand against its candidates, a second triage on each pass, while the readers of decision 195 saw neither the ledger nor the candidates. A reader now gets each new finding's candidates and returns the one it restates, or none. The orchestrator takes the match as given when the candidate was rejected, carried or deferred, and checks it against the code when it would put the finding in the fix bucket. Reopens: reader matches that prove wrong in the field.

239. **The gate's tests check structure and contracts, and its requirements state behavior.** Each past failure of the gate had become a sentence in the skill, a test that asserted the sentence, a requirement that restated it and an acceptance entry. The skill's suite held 81 cases that asserted 102 exact sentences, so a cut or a reworded line meant moving a pin, a pin could not tell a rule that held from one that had only kept its wording, and the skill grew to about 15,500 words with each rule stated in several files. The suite now checks what a run needs from the files: the frontmatter, the defaults, the reading block, the files and scripts named, the report template, the child command, every script command shown, each of which it runs, the change kinds and the rules against `next.mjs`, the record's sample against `next.mjs`, the git commands shown, and a word budget. The skill states each rule once, and requirements 287 to 323 state behavior without the wording. What only a model run can show is an eval case or an acceptance item. Reopens: a regression that a sentence pin would have caught and that no contract check, eval case or acceptance item does.

240. **`findings.mjs` reads a finding without a line, a fence however it is written, and one path per file.** The host's finding shape makes the line optional, and a fence can arrive indented or with its info string in capitals; any of the three failed the whole review, so a finished review ended the run `unconverged`. A path written with backslashes or a leading `./` gave a second identity for the same file, so such a finding never carried against an earlier pass. A finding without a line has a `null` line, both fences are read, and the identity and the ledger hold the path with forward slashes and no leading `./`. Reopens: a host finding shape without `file` or `summary`.

241. **A usage limit fails the pass, and the next run resumes it.** Decision 184 gave a group whose child met a usage limit one review by the stand-in, and decision 205 moved that retry to another model than the reviewer's. Decision 184 was made when a failed pass lost its finished reviews. Since decision 232 a resumed pass keeps every saved review that `findings.mjs` reads and reviews only the groups whose output is missing or bad. The retry cost about 240 words in the step, a second engine inside one pass, and in the default configuration a model the user had not set reviewing part of the change. A usage limit is now a failed review: `findings.mjs` still names each limited output, the pass is recorded `failed` with `usage limit` as the reason in that review's entry, the run ends `unconverged`, and the next run on the same head repeats the pass for the groups with no finished review. This reverses decision 184's retry and the retry model of decision 205; `findings.mjs` is unchanged. The cost: a run that meets a limit ends and waits for a second invocation, where the retry could have finished it. Reopens: limits met so often that runs rarely finish in one invocation.

242. **`resume.mjs` decides where a later run on a branch starts.** The Resume section of the skill was about 560 words of cases that the orchestrator worked through by hand from `passes.json`, the head, the remote head and the merge-base, and preflight restated the test for a commit left unpushed. Three of the eight gaps found by reading the skill's text were in that logic. `resume.mjs` reads the record and prints one action: a new sequence, a continued level, a repeated pass, the decision of a pass whose commit is the head, an ending on the last stop, or `blocked`. With a tip alone it says whether an earlier run made that commit and did not push it, which preflight asks before it lets through a branch one commit ahead of its remote. Writing the cases as tests and reading the script against the prose showed five states the prose got wrong, and the script settles them. A commit left unpushed is pushed first whenever it is the head: with a changed merge-base the prose started a new sequence without the push, which could end `pass` with the commit still local. A branch that is not on the remote counts as not pushed, as the push of a fix round already takes it. The pass resumed is the last one, never an earlier one a run left unfinished, since rewriting an earlier entry with a later run number gives a record that `next.mjs` refuses. A pass entry now records the head it reviews as `reviewed`, and a pass that has no commit is resumed only on that head: the prose compared the head of the last complete pass, so the first pass of a new sequence was never resumed and its finished reviews were lost, and with no complete pass it compared only the merge-base, so a run on a new head reused reviews of the old one. And the first pass of a sequence writes the merge-base with its entry, since a run stopped between the two writes left a pass to be reviewed or resumed on a merge-base that had moved. Every push that Resume makes is recorded as `kept`, so the report names it. What the record cannot show, a complete pass with no head or no usable `next`, starts a new sequence and does not end the run, since one more review is always correct. Not taken: one call for preflight and Resume both, since preflight asks before the merge-base and the run entry exist. Reopens: a record state the script reads wrong, or a host that keeps the run's state itself.

243. **`core.md` and Concise Plain 4.15 state each rule once, and the digests are the one test of the wording.** Most recorded failures had become a sentence in `core.md` or the style, a test of that sentence, a requirement that quoted it and an edit to the chat block, and a cut cost as many edits as an addition, so the texts grew far more than they were cut: `core.md` held 1,383 words in 40 rules and the style 1,003 words. A review of both found the decision rule in four items and again in the style, the clause "Ask-first and stop rules still hold" repeated inside other items, an item on what leaves the session that spoke in the suite's own terms and contradicted the style on what a ticket may hold, a commit form that overrode a repository's own convention, and link forms for two vendors' hosts in a general style. `core.md` now holds 1,124 words in 33 rules and the style 810 words. In `core.md`, one Working item holds the decision rules, puts open decisions together and sends a question to a tool, integration or file before the owner; the opening paragraph says the ask-first rules hold whatever another item says, in place of that clause in two items, so a stop rule is held by its own item alone, and says that a subagent reads "me" as the session that launched it, which can pass on an approval the user gave and gives none of its own, since a rule that says to ask the owner has no reader in a subagent and the launching session must not become a second source of approval; one item replaces decision 220's two and keeps its split by reader, its limits on a pull request and on publishing, and its ban on saying how the work was reached, with a ticket or comment written at the detail its readers act on, where the earlier text sent every technical detail to an attached record and so kept out of a ticket what its reader needed in order to act; the bug item takes in the item on an unverified cause; the scratch item names the host's scratchpad and asks at the end for what was made, by exact name; the commit item follows the repository's convention first and bars naming the session, model or tools; and the handoff prompt item leaves, since it applies only when a handoff prompt is asked for, which is when the handoff prompt skill loads, so the skill holds the definition and the chat block carries it (decisions 211 and 212). Cut by name, each judged to add no behavior the rest does not give: "someone else's area is no reason by itself to defer"; a second option "only when it wins under a condition I control"; "the assumption every option shares"; open decisions together "unless one depends on another"; "A decision reaches my reply only when the next step turns on the answer", which the list of what to ask for and the report item cover; from the scratch item, the list kept "as you make or first change it", "noting which existed already", "from that list and the disk" and "and why, and nothing more" (decisions 141 and 142); from the item on an unverified cause, "say which check"; from the item on what leaves the session, its lists of run output and that a pull request is "as technical as the code needs, in the team's words" and "replaced when a push makes it stale"; a check that ran "not the end of a pipe"; and "Report anything else you notice, with evidence". In the style, Links is one rule with no vendor's forms and no order among its sources, and decision 68's sentence on one host's remotes leaves the chat block with it; Drafts loses the sentence that a draft commits the sender to nothing undecided and grades no one (decision 180), the sentence that puts a shared decision as the team's and the one on a status note (decision 183), the pointer to the house rules on what leaves the session (decision 220), "never a label this session coined", where a mention's form is settled from and that its raw form goes only in a draft a tool posts, and the place of "the sentence that gives them context" in the order of a cut, each covered by the bullet it sat in. The chat block follows at the same version. Tests of a single sentence go: requirement 18's two digests fail on any change to the wording, one test holds the one opening paragraph and the order of the five sections (requirement 354) and one the eight Ask first items (requirement 21), the tests that use the shipped text as their fixture hold only its count of rules and the line numbers that follow from its length, and requirements 19, 20, 232 to 234, 236, 269, 284, 321, 325 and 345 are retired. The cost: an installed block reads `stale` once, and behavior that rested on a cut clause is no longer asked for by name. Not taken: generating the chat block from the style (requirement 228). Settled 2026-11-10 by the owner. Reopens: a recorded failure that a cut clause would have prevented, which restores that clause alone.

244. **Lint holds the three copies of the house-rules item to `core.md`.** Decision 221 put a test in each plugin's own test area, each importing the rules plugin's script and two of them also holding the two items as literals, so one changed word in an item meant edits to nine files, and the test areas of three plugins depended on a fourth plugin's code. Its reason was that a commit touching only one plugin's copy would not run a single test under `tests/rules`. Lint runs in CI on every push and pull request whatever the commit touched, so one rule there meets that reason: it fails when a skill has no `## What leaves the run` section, when the section holds another number of list items than one, whatever their marker, or when the item is not one `- ` line that equals an item of `core.md` with its lines joined and has nothing under it that a Markdown reader would add to it (requirement 349). The three area tests and their imports of the rules script are gone. The cost: a copy that drifts is caught at the push, not by the area script a commit runs, and lint does not know which item of `core.md` the skills are meant to carry, so a copy replaced by another whole item passes. Reopens: a second item the plugins carry, or a copy found holding the wrong item, either of which makes the rule name the item.

245. **A changed ticket reopens a done input.** An input was done once every slice that serves it had a commit on the branch, and a run with no new work reused Steps 1 to 5 whatever their inputs, so a criterion added to an issue after its work was committed was never read: the next run ended `done` with nothing posted. The version Step 1 records could not carry the judgment, since the loop's own comment on a ticket moves it. An input is now done only while its text is the text it was last planned from: a ticket's title and the fields that say what it asks for, or a file's text, are compared with the copy in `inputs.md` of the latest run whose part of `plan.md` holds the input, so a run cut off before it planned does not hide a change. A changed input is open and goes through every step like a new one, with a plan section that holds no slice for it when the branch already meets the new text, and a comment on its ticket (requirement 90). A shorter path for that case would need an exception in the plan, the ticket comments and the report, so a change of wording alone costs a plan review and a final review. Comments are not compared, so a criterion given in a comment is still missed. The fix is not shown by a run. Reopens: a run that misses a criterion added in a comment, or the cost of a full run for a change of wording.

246. **A post is logged when it returns.** A ticket's log line was written after its last post of the run, the filing and then the attach, so a run cut off between the two left no line, and the next run could comment on the ticket again and attach a second artifact. Nothing said when a new pull request is recorded, and a pull request no run entry names is treated as someone else's, whose body is never edited. A run after one that ended `blocked` in the CI watch would run Step 7 in full and post a second comment on the pull request for an unchanged head. The ticket line is now written when the filing or comment returns and again when the attach does, the pull request entry before any other call, and a run with nothing to push and no ticket newly filed or found repeats no body edit and no comment that `run.md` logs for that head (requirement 92). A record from before this change logs no head, so its first rerun can still repeat a comment. From reading the text; no run has shown any of the three. Reopens: a duplicate comment, artifact or ticket on a rerun.

247. **No ticket of the record is the ticket of an open item.** `tickets.md` kept a ticket out of the open items only when it was an input of the same run, while the handoff's Tickets section holds the inputs of every run of the record. A later run could so file an open item to a ticket an earlier run took up, the handoff named that ticket under both Tickets and Raised tickets, and `handoff.sh check` refused it: with the loop's example changed that way it prints `raised ticket 'github:acme/widget-app#12' is already a ticket id in Tickets` and exits 1. The rule now covers every ticket a run of the record took up: one the tracker search finds gets no comment for the item, and the item's new ticket links it, as it links a closed ticket (requirement 351). Keeping the item on that ticket as a remainder would save a ticket, but the closing line, the status comment and every later run would then have to know of it. `handoff.md` now says that its Tickets section holds the inputs of every run, and a test runs `handoff.sh check` on the loop's example, since the format has its writer in one plugin and its reader in another. Reopens: a handoff the audit refuses, or new tickets that repeat what a ticket of the record tracks.

248. **A draft filed by hand is found by its title.** A run that files nothing writes each ticket as a draft and tells the user to file it, and Follow-ups is built only from the `- ticket` lines of `run.md`, so a draft filed by hand had no line and dropped out of the body at its next rewrite. A later run now searches the tracker for a ticket whose title equals the text of a draft line with no ticket line, which for a new ticket's draft is its title, and logs a match (requirement 351). A draft that is a comment on an existing ticket matches none. Carrying over the lines of the live body would need the body parsed and would keep a line a person had deleted. A record attached by hand is not found, so a later change to the item attaches a second one. From reading the text; no run has shown it. Reopens: a draft filed under another title, or a second record on a ticket filed by hand.

249. **Ticket text follows its reader on file names.** `tickets.md` kept every file and line out of a ticket's body, while the house rules leave them out only for a reader who won't open the code. A ticket that asks someone to run a check by hand is read by a person who opens the files the check uses, and the issues the loop filed for such a check named them, so acceptance item 84 could not pass. A ticket now names a file or a line only where its reader opens it to act; the rest of decision 224's list stands (requirement 352). Reopens: a ticket whose file names its readers did not need.

250. **The manifest names each ticket in a form the audit reads.** The loop wrote every file input into the manifest's `tickets` as `file:<path>`, which the audit reads as an export and refuses without its keys, and an Azure DevOps or Salesforce ticket as `ado:` or `sf:`, a form the audit does not list and cannot read without an MCP tool of its own. One audit of a loop's manifest stopped on `export <path>: missing id`. The loop now leaves a file or text input out of `tickets`, where the handoff still carries it as claims, and writes each Azure DevOps or Salesforce ticket it read as an export in the audit's format, named in the manifest as `file:<path>`, with the ticket's handoff id as its `id` so the audit maps the two (requirement 355). This is the exporter decision 31 left unbuilt, on the loop's side, where the ticket's text already is. The fix is not shown by a run. From reading, the audit has no rule against a bundle with no ticket, and in a bundle with one ticket it gives that ticket every commit, so a document's changes are judged with the one ticket beside it. Reopens: an audit that stops on a loop's export or on a bundle with no ticket, or one that misjudges a document's changes.

251. **The loop refreshes its own pull request's body after a push from outside.** The gate pushes fix commits to a pull request the loop opened and edits no body, and a later loop run with no new work on published work edited none either, so the description stayed on the head before those commits, though the house rules ask that a pull request's text be true of its current head. Who refreshes it was open: the gate, a note in its report, or the next loop run. The loop now writes the body of its own pull request again on that path when the tip is a commit no run made and no logged body describes it, and a body it writes lists such commits under Decisions for the reviewer as not the loop's, and as not reviewed when they were made after the record's last review pass (requirements 90 and 117). The gate still edits no body, so the description is stale from the gate's push until the next loop run. From reading the text; no run has shown it. Reopens: a description left stale because no loop run followed a gate run.

252. **Acceptance item 86 plants an open item, not a planned change.** Item 86 had a second open pull request make one change the issue asked for, and expected the run to leave that change out as resolved elsewhere. `tickets.md` settles an item as resolved elsewhere only among the open items, the work left after the plan, and a change the issue asks for is part of the plan, so the run built it and named the overlap in the body and the comment. The item now plants a defect outside the issue that another open pull request fixes. Having the plan read the open pull requests for work its tickets ask for would add a read on one forge for a need seen only in this item. Reopens: a run that builds work an open pull request already holds, at a cost to someone.

253. **An instruction of the loop has one home only where every run has read it.** The loop's skill files repeated instructions, some in up to nine places, and copies had come to disagree: `risk.md` passed the effort hint to reviewers only, where requirement 51, `SKILL.md` and Step 4.2 pass it to every subagent; `SKILL.md` printed a matched block or a PEM header as `<redacted: key>`, where `record.md` wrote `<redacted: block>` and `<redacted: pem_key>`; and `steps/4-build.md` left out the model the stand-in is retried at. `ci-watch.md` also said that `gh` fills `{owner}` and `{repo}` from the session's directory. It fills them from the directory the call runs in, so with a named checkout of another repository a read made from the session's directory would watch the wrong repository; with a named checkout, Step 0 now has such a call name the checkout's repository and keep its plain form, which the skill pre-approves, where a `cd` into the checkout would prompt at every read. One home for every instruction does not hold as a rule. Only `SKILL.md`, `steps/0-preflight.md` and `record.md` are read on every path of a run: a plan-only run reads no step file after `steps/1-plan.md`, a run with no new work reads `steps/4-build.md` without it, and a host other than GitHub never reads `steps/7-publish.md`. A request, a brief or a template also leaves the skill for a reader who sees no skill file. A copy goes only when both copies sit in one file, when its home is one of those three files, or when it sits in `SKILL.md` and is acted on only in the step whose file holds the home.
