By that rule the roles are stated once, as the table in `SKILL.md` and, for what a role's numbered item alone said, in the step that acts on it; the effort words and the credential shapes are in `SKILL.md` alone, and Step 0.1 takes the shapes by citation; the redaction markers are named where the echo prints them, and a check holds them to that file; and Step 4.3 cites the meaning of blocking. The text went from 29,976 to 29,501 words. Left as they are: a ban repeated beside the action it bans, the openings that repeat each file's read point, and the log line forms in `record.md` and `tickets.md`; a test holds the last two equal. A check holds the effort words to one file. The `gh` fix has no test, since nothing short of a run shows where a call is made. Reopens: a run that misses a rule because its home was not in reach, or two copies found to disagree again.

254. **The loop's tests check structure and contracts.** As decision 239 found for the gate, the loop's suite asserted sentences: 157 cases held about 380 exact phrases, so a reworded line moved a pin, a pin could not tell a rule that held from one that had only kept its wording, and a citation of a heading that no longer existed passed. The suite now has 68 cases that read what a run or another reader needs from the files. Every cited section, step, item and record file exists, each read point equals the opening of the file it names, and a run ends in one of five states. The configuration block equals the README's and holds every key path the prose names. The roles, the Step 5 level and the floor are read as tables and lists, and the reading block, the pull request body, the report and Step 7.2 hold their parts in order. The handoff and the manifest parse and match what the audit and the README hold, the `steps.json` example parses with the keys the prose names, the record sits where the audit looks for it, the report names the gate and the audit by their commands, the log line forms a later run reads back are one literal in the files that give them, and the commands a post or a read depends on keep their shape. Four short literals stay because their loss would let a run do harm: the nine credential key names, the actions that always ask, `--comment` and `--fix` after "never", and that `ultra` is never typed. Each check was seen to fail on a change to the text it reads, and a reworded sentence passes. What only a model run can show is an eval case or an acceptance item. The requirements whose check named a sentence now name what the test reads, or review alone (requirements 89, 99, 104, 112, 267, 281 and 282). Reopens: a regression that a sentence pin would have caught and that no contract check, eval case or acceptance item does.

255. **A question says what its yes covers, where it is asked.** Requirement 351 has any question that an ask-first rule raises for a ticket comment name the record attached with it. The rule stood in `SKILL.md`, Approval scope, rule 1, and in `tickets.md`, which a publishing run reads after its first push, while a run under a rule that covers commits and comments asks before its commit. In runs of loop 0.1.24, thirteen questions asked before a commit listed a ticket comment and four of them left out its record. Three of those runs went on: one asked again after its push and named the record then, one kept the record local, and one attached it although no question had listed it. Every run had read the same files before it asked, and none had read `tickets.md`, so the read point does not explain the misses and their cause is not shown. `steps/7-publish.md` now says, where the question is asked, that it lists every action its yes will cover, each ticket comment with its record, and that a yes covers no more; Step 7.4 says that a record no question of the run named gets its own question before it is attached, neither attached unasked nor held back to avoid the question, and that a reply that is not a clear yes keeps it local while the run goes on; and rule 1 gains that a yes covers what the question listed, and that this non-yes is not a denial, since by rule 3 a non-yes after the first push would otherwise end the run `blocked` (requirement 351). That last point comes from reading the rules together, not from a run. On six small issues run to their question, the publish step's new sentence for the question named the record in six of six where loop 0.1.24 named it in five of six; those runs came before the wording for a non-yes. At the earlier rate chance alone gives six of six about one time in nine, so the first half is not shown, and no run reached the second. Not taken: settling the open items before the first question, so that one question lists every ticket. That reverses the timing of decision 223 for no failure seen, since each run that had a ticket to file listed it in its first question or asked for it after the push. Also not taken: text for a ticket that a CI repair pass adds after the question. Its yes does not cover that ticket, and the worst reading of the rules leaves it a draft that the report names. Reopens: a run that posts what no question listed, or a record left local after a yes.

256. **The loop's text is not edited further for length.** Moving each instruction of the loop to one home cut 1.6% of its words (decision 253). Editing one file line by line, `steps/4-build.md`, cut 5% of that file, 3,786 to 3,582 words, with nothing a run does changed, and a read of the result still found one rule that had lost its statement where a run meets it and one changed referent, both put back. The gate's cut of about a third had come from copies across files that every gate run reads, a script (decision 242) and a dropped retry (decision 241), not from wording. So the loop's text gets shorter only by changing what a run does or what a run reads when, and line editing stops with that one file. The loop keeps every feature and ships no script (decisions 31 and 112). Reopens: a run where bookkeeping done by hand slips, or where a rule is missed.

257. **Acceptance item 86 plants a decision its issue gives to the owner.** Item 86 expected a choice between two behaviors, which its issue allowed and did not name, to reach the report's Decisions for you. Two runs, at loop 0.1.20 and 0.1.24, made the choice themselves; the second named it in the report's Decisions as decided by the run and in the pull request body's Decisions for the reviewer. That is the loop as written: a run settles what its ticket leaves open and says what it chose (`report.md`, Decisions), and `tickets.md` sends to the user only an item whose answer is the user's call. The item's issue now states the choice and says it is the repository owner's and not yet made, so the item tests that path. The loop's text did not change. Not taken: a rule that sends every choice a ticket leaves open to the user, which would hold up tickets a run can finish. Reopens: a run that makes a choice its ticket gives to the owner without putting it in Decisions for you, or a run that makes a choice its ticket leaves open and names it nowhere.

258. **A skill pre-approves no git form that can write a file or run a program.** The loop, the gate, the audit, the primer's two skills and the refactor skill pre-approved `git diff`, `git log` and `git show` with a trailing `*`; the loop and the gate added `git ls-remote`, the others `git grep`, the audit also `git stash list` and nineteen `git -C * <command> *` forms, and the gate passed the first three to its review children. Requirement 59 and decision 204 called these read-only. They are not in every form. On Claude Code 2.1.321, a session under the default permission mode with only such entries as its rules ran each of these without a prompt: `git diff --output=<file>` and `git log -1 --output=<file>` wrote the file, `git grep -O<program>` ran the program, and with `Bash(git -C * rev-parse *)` and `Bash(git -C * status *)`, `git -C <repo> -c alias.x=!<program> x rev-parse y` and `git -C <repo> -c core.fsmonitor=<program> status` ran the program, since the first `*` admits `-c`. Run by hand, `git ls-remote --upload-pack=<program>` runs the program and `git stash list --output=<file>` writes the file. With no rule, the same session asked for each of those forms, and ran the plain `git diff`, `git log`, `git show` and `git grep` of its own repository unasked. So the entries added nothing for the plain reads and took the prompt away from the forms that write or run: text a run reads, a ticket or a file under review, could ask for one and no prompt would come. The entries are gone from the seven lists and from the gate's child list, which keeps `git status` and `git ls-files`; a review child with that list read the change with `git diff` and had no call denied. Lint holds every shell entry a skill or a command pre-approves to a list of known reads and runs of the plugin's own scripts, with a `*` only at the entry's end (requirement 356), and the primer's guidance for a user's own skill says the same (requirement 254). What a run under the default mode sees: `git ls-remote` asks in the loop and the gate, which their permission statements count, and every `git -C <repo>` read of the audit asks, or is refused when no one can answer. An audit read that starts with `export GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1;` was asked for already, since the host asks for the first part of such a command whatever rule matches the second. Of the entries kept, `git hash-object` writes an object with `-w`, `git cat-file` runs a filter the repository's configuration names with `--textconv` or `--filters`, and a `gh` read opens a browser with `--web`; none writes to a path the call names or runs a command the call names, which is what the audit's text now claims. Not taken: a script for the audit's reads of another repository, pre-approved by its quoted path. A skill's grant ends at the user's first reply (decision 215) and the audit asks for its fetch after its first reads, so the script would cover those first reads only, which is all the removed entries covered.

259. **A settled item stays out of Follow-ups, not out of the body.** Acceptance item 86 expected the choice put to the owner to appear nowhere in the pull request body and the defect another pull request fixes not to be in the body, and `tickets.md` said an item resolved elsewhere is not listed in the body. `pr-body.md` asks for less: such an item is not listed in `## Follow-ups`, and `## Decisions for the reviewer` holds the design choices and the behavior a reader could take for a bug. A run at loop 0.1.25 followed `pr-body.md`: Follow-ups held neither, and Decisions for the reviewer named the option built and the unchanged defect with the pull request that fixes it. A run that builds one of two options has to tell its reviewer which, so the item's first clause could not pass. The item and `tickets.md` now say Follow-ups, as `pr-body.md` does. Not taken: keeping both out of the whole body, which would hide from the reviewer what the change does.

260. **No audit command sets a variable before git.** Stage 1 had every Bash command that runs git begin with `export GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1;`, so that a missing object fails instead of being fetched from a promisor remote (requirement 153). Under the default permission mode a command of two parts is asked for whatever rule matches its git part: with `Bash(git -C * rev-parse *)` as a rule, `export GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1; git -C <repo> rev-parse HEAD` was refused with the export named, and `GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1 git -C <repo> rev-parse HEAD` was refused too. That is the refusal a run with no one to answer met at validation. The variable covered stage 1's own commands and no agent's or later stage's, and it came with git 2.45, which an older git does not know. What keeps every stage from a lazy fetch is the refusal of a partial clone. The refusal in step 0 read the repository's own configuration file with its includes, and a run by hand showed it miss a promisor remote set in a worktree's configuration; it now names no file and so reads every source, as `revert-tests.sh` does, and stage 1 sets no variable. It read the top level alone: with the variable gone, a diff under `diff.submodule=diff` entered a checked-out submodule that was a partial clone and fetched its missing blob, where the variable had made that read fail. `readonly.sh` now refuses a partial clone in every repository it walks, the top level, the nested ones and the checked-out submodules, so the baseline stops such a run before its first diff. A submodule that is not checked out leaves no repository in the work tree for a read to enter: the same diff failed on one and fetched nothing. A repository inside an ignored directory is not walked and not refused. The script runs the text shows have their path in double quotes, since an unquoted path with a space splits the command, and `SKILL.md` says so once for every path. The README says that what the skill pre-approves holds until the user's first reply, so a run with no one to answer needs a permission mode that lets its commands run. The user's own rules are not offered as a second way: a rule matches a command issued alone, and in one low-tier run 48 of the 50 shell calls, those of its agents included, joined several commands, and one more was a pipe. Not taken: `git --no-lazy-fetch`, which a git before 2.45 rejects and the host asks for even in the session's own folder; a script that sets the variable and runs git, a new command form on every read for a guard the refusal gives; a published list of allow rules, since a rule for a `git -C` read has to name the repository's path to keep `-c` out (decision 258).

261. **A ticket's id is not how it was read.** Stage 1 listed `mcp:<tool>` and `text` among the forms of a ticket id, beside `github:` and `file:`, while the reading block and the brief record the same two as how a ticket resolved. Read as written, two tickets read through one tool, or two known by their ids alone, share an id, and the file names made from the id collide; and the `ado:` and `sf:` ids the audit's own handoff writes (requirement 138) were in no list, so a handoff ticket with one mapped to a manifest ticket only through an export that carries the same id. This comes from reading the text; no run showed it. The id forms are now `github:`, `ado:`, `sf:` and `file:`, an id in another form stays as the user wrote it, and how the ticket resolved is recorded beside the id. A ticket known by its id alone was audited without the run saying that its body was not read; the reading block and the report's Coverage now name it. A plain document given as a `file:` ticket stopped the run with `export <path>: missing id` and nothing that said what an export is; the loop no longer writes one (decision 250), and the sentence after that stop now says what an export holds and where the README shows it, with an example there. With `ticket_token`, a pattern takes the number of an `ado:` or `sf:` id, since the loop's exports carry those ids (requirement 355) and the whole id in the pattern matches no commit. Not taken: stopping on an id in no listed form, which would end a run that today audits the ticket by its id and the user's words; matching a commit by the number of an `ado:` or `sf:` id in a bundle with no `ticket_token`, since a bare number matches unrelated text and a handoff's commits join their ticket without it; an exporter for Azure DevOps (decision 31).

262. **On Windows a TERM goes before the KILL that ends a test command's group.** `revert-tests.sh` ends a test command's processes with a KILL to its process group, once the command has ended and when the run is interrupted. On Windows a native program's own children are not in that group. With a batch file as the test's program and a `node` under it, the child kept running after a test file that passed and after an interrupted run, and held the work dir, so a run that otherwise succeeded ended by naming the work dir as left (issue 190). By hand, a KILL alone left the child running in each of 9 runs, and a TERM followed at once by the KILL ended it in each of 60; at the timeout, where the group already gets a TERM before its KILL, the child ended. So on Windows the script sends a TERM before each of those two KILLs, and cases 5f to 5h of its suite, which run on Windows only, hold the three paths. Elsewhere the group holds every child and the KILL still goes alone, since a TERM there would only let a process the test left behind start its trap. Why a TERM ends the child and a KILL does not was not looked into. Not taken: ending each native program's tree with `taskkill`, which adds a scan of the process table and a second tool where the TERM was enough. Still out of reach: a native child whose parent has already exited, which by hand outlived both signals. A `node` program's own `node` child ended on a KILL alone, so a test runner built on node did not show the defect.

263. **On Windows no TERM goes before the KILL that ends a test command's group.** Decision 262 put a TERM before the two KILLs of `revert-tests.sh` on Windows, because the TERM ended a native program's children where the KILL alone did not, and did not look into why. The reason is the runtime of Git for Windows: a shell that waits on a native program answers a TERM by ending the program's tree itself. It lists the program, then every process whose recorded parent id is on the list; a shell found that way gets the TERM if it leads its group, and a native program found that way is ended. A recorded parent id outlives the parent, and Windows hands a freed id to a new process, so a process whose parent has exited is taken for a child whenever a listed program holds the id that parent had. That much is from reading the runtime. By running: a job in its own group ran a batch file that started seven programs, the group got a TERM and at once a KILL, job after job, and of twenty shells that stood by, each started by a `cmd` that had exited, 5 got the TERM within 48 jobs, 16 within 342 and 18 within 324, in three rounds; for 10 of those 39 the id of the batch file's `cmd` was the shell's recorded parent id. So the TERM of decision 262 could end a shell the audit never started, and by the reading a native program too, each time a test command left a native program behind or a run was interrupted. One run of the whole suite at 0.1.21 carries the walk's marks on processes that were no kin of the script: the three shell suites that were running and a hook's shell in another plugin's tests each ended on a TERM, and three `node` processes of other tests ended with status 143, which is 128 plus the TERM's 15, the status a `node` reported by hand when a TERM to its group ended it. Whose id had been handed out again was not shown, and fifteen more runs did not repeat it. One earlier run, at 0.1.13, carries the same marks on shells: the same three suites and two hooks' shells in another plugin's tests each ended on a TERM; no native program ended there, and which signal sent it was not shown. So the reach did not begin with 0.1.21, and withdrawing this TERM does not end such a failure of the suite. It takes away what 0.1.21 added: in a run, a TERM for each test command that leaves a native program behind and for each interrupt; in the suite, three cases that send one on every run. The TERM is withdrawn. Cases 5f to 5h go with it, the third because it sends the timeout's TERM to a group that holds a live native program, which is the same walk. Issue 190 is open again: after the KILL alone a native program's children keep running and hold the work dir. Still there: the TERM at the timeout and the TERMs of the `bg` mode go to a group that can hold a native program (issue 193), and whether the KILL alone reaches a shell that is no kin, as the runtime's text says it does, was not run. Not taken: keeping the TERM and its cases until the script can end a tree itself, which leaves every run on Windows that ends a group with a native program in it able to signal a process it does not own; a case that holds the reach, which needs a process to get the id a dead parent had, and a suite can wait for that but not arrange it. Reopens: a way for the script to end a native program's tree that takes a process for a child only when it started after its parent, which issue 190 and issue 193 both need.

264. **The audit's text agrees with itself in eight places.** The audit's files said different things about the same step in eight places, found by reading them against each other, the scripts and the requirements. (1) The skill said stage 1 takes the read-only baseline before anything else, and the README and requirement 178 put the snapshot before stage 1. The stage file takes it in step 1b, after step 0 has made the run directory and after the fetch, and `readonly.sh snapshot` stops without the run directory. All three now give the stage file's order. (2) The skill prints the reading block before any other tool call and the sentence on background fetch after it. Stage 1's step 0h printed both again after seven steps of calls, with an older label for a ticket whose body was not read, step 0a said to report a missing `jq` before anything else, and `/audit:run` printed a fetch sentence of its own before the skill loads. For a run the skill's print is now the only one: step 0h prints the block only under `/audit:resume`, where no earlier step does, and the command's sentence is gone. That reads "the summary" of requirement 147 as the reading block, and the requirement now says so. A resume that does not rerun stage 1 prints no block, as before. (3) The budget rule and requirement 152 record every unstarted stage as `failed` with "not run: budget expired"; stage 7's file said it gets no entry. It now writes the entry. No script reads a stage entry, so what changes is the stage's line in the report's Coverage and the entry a resume supersedes. The skill and requirement 158 said stage 6 always runs, against the same rule, which launches nothing once the budget has expired; both now carry the exception the stage file already had. (4) The stage file makes the sentinel token with `od` piped through `tr -d ' \n'`. The skill and the request template named `od` alone with the spaces removed, which leaves the newline to the reader; both now give the whole command. (5) The report template's Coverage list and stage 8's each lacked lines the other had. Each now holds the other's lines, and none is removed. (6) Hard rule 3 said act's commit messages name the activity and the date, as requirement 11 says of every commit message, while act and requirement 194 write the message in the repository's convention. The date stays for report text that could be posted and for work-item text; a commit message of act carries one only where the repository's convention does, and requirement 11 now makes that exception. (7) A resumed run has no budget (requirement 152). The skill allowed one from the resume's own request, which the command cannot carry, and a stage 1 rerun under resume rebuilt the manifest and the recorded request with the first run's budget in them. The skill now says a resumed run has none, and step 0b that the recorded value stays in `manifest.json` and is not applied. `_test` `expire_budget_after_stage` still expires the budget on a resumed run, since that key is how a test reaches the path, and its text says so. (8) The skill's definition of a failed agent lacked the pass file without its claims heading, which `common.md`, `report.sh` and requirement 322 already fail. Apart from the command's fetch sentence and stage 1's second print under a run, no copy of an instruction is removed here: giving each instruction one home waits for a check that every citation resolves.

265. **A file and the folder of its name are one review group, and the stand-in reads its paths as names.** `split.mjs` gives each group of a split pass its paths, and each review reads the range limited to them. Git reads a path as the file and as everything under it, and as a pattern besides, so two groups could select one file, which was then reviewed twice in the pass. A finding is identified by its file and summary and was kept once, so the cost was review time (issue 180). A file replaced by a folder of the same name was listed among its parent's own files while the folder's files made a group of their own, and both groups selected both. Such a file now takes the folder's group, and that folder is not grouped again, since any group that names the file selects the whole folder (requirement 297). A name that holds `[` selected, read as a pattern, a file of another group as well: with changes to `lib/x.js` and `l[i]b/x.js`, the paths `l[i]b/x.js` and `m.md` selected `lib/x.js` too. The stand-in's diff, which the gate itself runs, now takes `--literal-pathspecs` (requirement 307). Not taken: `GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS` in the child session's environment. It makes a name of every path in every git call of that session, a pattern then selects nothing and git gives no error, and in 5 of 98 saved sessions of the review command the reviewer's own git calls named paths with wildcards, such as `plugins/*/.claude-plugin`, `'plugins/**/*.md'` and `':!*plugin.json'`. So a child review still reads its paths as patterns. What is left is a name that, read as a pattern, matches a changed file of another group: a file named by itself, or a folder beside a changed file, as `app/[id]` beside a file `app/i`. By hand a folder's path selected nothing under another folder, `app/[id]` beside `app/i/page.tsx`. `tests/gate/paths.test.mjs` builds a repository for each of the two cases and compares what git selects with literals. Reopens: a run in which a child review reads a file of another group, which would move an escape of such a name into `split.mjs`.

266. **A child review's allow list names each shell entry for the PowerShell tool as well.** The list held `Bash(git status *)`, `Bash(git ls-files *)` and one `Bash(<check>*)` per check (requirement 305). A session on Windows with the PowerShell tool on may run a command with either shell tool, and a rule for one does not cover the other, which decisions 177 and 204 met in two skills. On Claude Code 2.1.321, two child reviews of the seeded fixture with the check `npm test` each called it through the PowerShell tool, typed alone, and were refused; a third, whose list also held the three PowerShell entries and was otherwise the same, ran it (issue 192). Of 98 saved sessions of the review command, 17 used only the Bash tool, 2 only the PowerShell tool and 79 both, so which tool a review picks is not fixed. Before the entries went in, a session under the default permission mode with only those three PowerShell rules was given thirteen commands that begin with `git status --short` or `npm test` and also write a file: after a `;`, through a pipe to `Out-File` or to a script block, by `>`, after `&&`, in a `$()` and on a second line. Each was refused and no file was written, while the two plain commands ran. A session without the PowerShell tool started and ran with the entries in its list. So the list holds each shell entry once per tool on every system, and lint holds a PowerShell entry to those three forms (requirement 356). Not taken: telling the review its limits and its checks through `--append-system-prompt`, which would leave the prompt of decision 143 alone. In one pair of runs of the three planned, the review that was told had 3 refused calls against 3, opened with the same refused compound read and still tried to run code with `node -e`; the runs stopped there, since the refused check had the other cause (issue 169). A review that is not told may still chain a check first: in a run of the gate with the new list, the review piped `npm test` into another command, was refused, and then ran it alone. That costs one refused call, which the record lists. Reopens: a run in which a review whose list names its tool gets no check to run, because every form it tries is refused.

267. **After a compaction the audit reads `SKILL.md` again, and then the files of the current stage.** The skill's recovery section had the session read the run's own files, `common.md` and the stage file of the current stage again, and not `SKILL.md` (issue 198). The host keeps only the first part of an invoked skill's text across a compaction; its documentation gives 5,000 tokens for each skill within 25,000 for all of them. On Claude Code 2.1.321 a skill of 400 numbered entries, each with a code of its own, was invoked in a session, the session was compacted, and it was then asked for codes by number: it held entries 1 to 249 and none after them, the first 19,813 of the body's 31,697 characters. The same session with no compaction held all 400, and a session resumed in a new process after the compaction held none. `SKILL.md` was about 42,000 characters, so after a compaction a run went on without the read-only check, the state file rules and every section after them, and nothing told it so. The recovery section, which ends before character 12,000, now starts with a read of `SKILL.md` and ends with the files the skill loads for the current stage, which for stage 8 are three (requirement 357). `tests/audit/structure.test.mjs` holds the section inside the first 15,000 characters and to naming that read, and fails on the text before the fix. Not shown: an audit run that is compacted and then reads the file. The probe shows the host's side alone, and each of its counts is from one run. Not taken: a `SKILL.md` short enough to be kept whole, since the kept length is the host's and all the skills a session invoked share it. Reopens: a compacted run that goes on without reading `SKILL.md` again, or a host that keeps less of the file than the section's end.

268. **Each instruction of the audit sits once, in a file its reader holds, and the other files point at it.** The audit's files restated one another: the skill summarized each stage's steps, four agent definitions each carried the command list, stages 2 to 7 each restated the ladder, and some of them the budget or when an output counts as failed as well, and stage 8 and the report template both listed what each report section holds. Copies drift, as the eight corrections of decision 264 show, and a session reads every copy it loads. A copy was cut only where each of its readers also holds the home: the session that runs the stages holds `SKILL.md`, `common.md` and the files the skill loads for the current stage; an agent holds its definition and `common.md`, and an auditor or adversary `audit-evidence.md` as well. A clause that only the copy held moved to the home first, and where copy and home differed one was chosen. The homes: the commands an agent may run, in `common.md` hard rule 2, with each definition keeping its own limits; the leads a finding must clear and the claims list, in `audit-evidence.md`; each stage's steps, in its stage file, with one paragraph for the stage in the skill; what each report section holds, in `report.md`, and how it is built, in `stages/8-report.md`; the rule for text that leaves the run, in `common.md` hard rule 3; the budget, the ladder, the token label, the request's phrases, the read-only snapshot and the turn that must not end, in the skill; a failed output and the claim kinds, in `common.md`. The skill and agent files went from 66,384 words to 62,577, the skill from 6,795 to 5,987, stage 8 from 4,962 to 3,608 and the auditor's definition from 2,096 to 1,664. Differences settled: (1) The skill ran stage 6's ledger check with `--stage6 failed` after a failed batch and the stage file ran the check only when no batch failed; the stage file now holds both, and the script's suite covers the flag. (2) The skill listed the manifest `scratch` key as the first choice when it is inside the repository and ignored, which read as going on to the next choice otherwise; stage 1 stops the run there, and the skill and requirement 175 now say so. (3) `resume.md` opened with a looser list of what makes a stage rerun than its step 6 holds; the opening points at the step. (4) Coverage's stage line and forge line follow `report.md`: the stored status is `complete`, and a forge also goes unqueried for a `text:` or `mcp:` ticket. (5) `report.md` said the template is not used when a read-only check failed; stage 8's own check runs after the report is written and leaves it on disk, so the template says a check before stage 8. (6) The low tier's Coverage line was a rule in stage 8 and an example in the template; the template states the rule. (7) Three stage files cited the budget section for the stage boundary line, which the control flow section holds. (8) The adversary now holds, in `audit-evidence.md`, the brief's line for a base that was not fetched and that a finding on an open pull request states the forge fact first; the auditor's definition held them alone. (9) Hard rule 2 gained two clauses the definitions held: no two-dot diff, and no `git status`. (10) An auditor's line for a claim it could not check follows the claims list, `not verified, not reproduced` for a `verification` claim and `not verified` for any other kind; the cut bullet named no kind. (11) The rule against ending the turn while a background run is live was labeled headless in the skill and stage 6 and had no condition in stage 1; it holds in every session. (12) Resume kept no top-up file under its own wording of a failed output; the shared rule exempts a top-up from the pass-two heading, so resume names the top-up file and still keeps none. Where the skill's summary of a stage differed from the stage file otherwise, on the fields of stage 6's role block and on the tier's counts, the stage file stands. Checks: the lint's 45 phrase checks of the command list over five files became 10 over hard rule 2; its checks of the skill's stage 6 summary, of the run id slug in the skill, of the claims heading in stages 4 and 5 and `resume.md`, and of stage 8's copies of three report sentences went. The check of each home was kept, except for the stage 6 summary: one new check holds `stages/6-second-opinion.md` to moving the mandatory set only on exit 0, and nothing checks its wording of `tmp/mandatory.txt` or of `## Additions`. `tests/audit/readers.test.mjs` looks for the reader rule in `common.md` alone. In their place `tests/audit/structure.test.mjs` holds a citation of a hard rule by number to a rule that exists, each agent with a shell to naming hard rule 2 before its steps, each stage section of the skill to naming its stage file and holding no list or table, `report.md` and `stages/9-act.md` to their pointer at hard rule 3, the skill and `resume.md` to their pointer at the rule for a failed output, and stages 4 and 5 to citing the skill's Roles section, which holds that pointer. The lint's other phrase checks stand; turning them into checks of structure is a later change. Not taken: the skill's own hard rules, which stay as decision 148 keeps each agent's restated ones; the merger's item block in `stages/7-converge.md`, which the lint holds equal to the merger's definition and whose reader does not hold that definition; the tests row of `stages/4-pass-one.md`, which is written into an agent's scope file; stage 5's list of what the adversary writes, which is its completion check. Left as found: stage 6 relaunches a batch in the same scratch folder while launch rule 3 gives each relaunch its own; stage 4 writes `usage.md` on each completion while the skill writes it at each stage boundary; stage 6 checks an answer file's last nonblank line while the output contract checks its last line. Reopens: a run in which a stage or an agent skips a rule that its own file held before and that it now reaches by a pointer.

269. **The audit's tests check structure and contracts.** As decisions 239 and 254 found for the gate and the loop, the audit's lint and one test file asserted sentences: `tests/audit/lint.sh` looked up exact phrases of the text, each in one file or in several, and `tests/audit/readers.test.mjs` held five cases of the same kind, so a reworded line moved a check, and a check could not tell a rule that held from one that had only kept its wording. Each lookup got one fate by the rule of decision 254; 73 of the lint's went, a phrase counted once for each file it was looked up in, which takes a run of the lint from 189 lookups of a fixed string to 116, and so did all five cases. A literal stays only where a script or another file reads it, or where its loss would let a run do harm; a rule that files or scripts must agree on gets a check of that agreement; a rule that only a run can show goes to the acceptance item whose expected result names it; the rest is review. Kept as literals: the ten forms of the command list in hard rule 2, with `mkdir -p` of the scratch folder and the bar on an inline program; the six things each agent's restated rules name, so decision 148 stands as it is; the trailer sentence of hard rule 3 in `common.md`; and the scan that no stage file names the top tier as a default. `tests/audit/structure.test.mjs` gains seven cases. It now holds the launch step of stages 4 and 5 to citing step 7 of `resume.md`, and that step to naming the two outputs it keeps, and their completion step to citing the skill's Roles section at least twice, for the failure and for the ladder; every section an agent definition names to a heading of a file that agent reads, `common.md` for all five and `audit-evidence.md` for the auditor and the adversary, or of its own file, or to an output heading `common.md` names, and the auditor and the adversary to naming the leads a finding must clear where they read `audit-evidence.md`; every failed ending in the three files that write, define and judge the merger's to the one form `status: failed at <file>:<line>`; act and the finding rules to the same five instruction sources read before a change; the rule for a failed output to the headings and the file suffix that `report.sh` tests; the stage 6 file and its request to the `### X<n>:` heading that `ledger.sh` parses, which narrows the gap decision 268 left: the wording on `tmp/mandatory.txt` stays unchecked; and the README's roles table to the default models of the skill's. Each of these cases failed on a copy of the files with its rule broken, 22 breaks in all, and all passed on eight copies with a sentence reworded. To an acceptance item, and so to review until that item runs again: the rules and skills directories in the default rank (item 35, which names no more of that list), `REVIEW.md` read at the base and agents reporting as if its rules were absent (item 37), the cap of 80 claims a group (item 59), the bars on a lead that the ground-truth fixture plants (item 15), and the second opinion's default model (items 14 and 42). To review alone: the merger's paging, anchor and fence rules; act's sources for required checks, its demonstration of a defect and its commit message rule; a claim that names no file spreading across groups; the slug of a `file:` ticket and of the run id; what a resume keeps; a late or unlisted completion notice; the move of the mandatory set only on exit 0, which no script reads; the swap rung; the fixed stage 8 line and section 3's cut in the report template; the `--live` exception for a plugin version; the wording of the reader rule, the pointer in the skill's own hard rule 3 and that act keeps the evidence and the item ids in the run directory; the auditor's sentence on specialist scopes, the adversary's on redoing a finding's three dimensions, the tests row written out in full, the skill's clause on an input copy, the two notes on which Evidence section is meant and the bars on a lead that no fixture plants; the one line `none` under a claims heading with no claim; and the copies of the trailer sentence in the skill and in act. No `Check:` clause of a requirement named a check that went, and requirement 349's sentence on what holds the posting rule now names the audit's pointer checks. Not taken: a copy check between the skill's own hard rules and those of `common.md`, since none of the eight is word for word the other; and a check that the restated section exists in place of its six tokens, which would narrow decision 148. Reopens: a regression that a sentence check would have caught and that no contract check or acceptance item does.

270. **A script writes the registry.** The skill had the session type the lock of `runs.json` itself: two `mkdir` calls, a `find` for an old lock, a temporary file, and one command that replaced the file and released the lock, about 430 words, two of its rules added after a run broke (decision 109, and a glob that stopped the command in one shell). No test covered the steps, and in a low-tier run the two updates took four of the session's 61 turns. `registry.sh` now does both updates, `add` and `set`, each in one run of the script under a lock it takes and releases itself, and its suite runs on each system and under mawk (requirement 177, now checked by test; requirements 6 and 184 name the twelfth script). A repeated command changes nothing, so a session that cannot tell whether an update ran runs it again: `add` takes an id with the same run directory as the same run, which the `mkdir` without `-p` of the run directory makes safe, and after a `set` that finds no entry the session adds the entry and sets it again. The script holds the lock only while it rewrites the file, so among its runs a lock older than a minute is from one that died, and the script removes it in every session; an interactive run used to ask first whether another audit was running. A session of an earlier version can still hold the lock across a prompt; it then loses the lock, as it already did to a run with no one to ask, and starts over, since it checks that it holds the lock before it replaces the file. A first form of the script lost entries when several runs met an old lock at once, 10 or 11 kept of 12: one run removed the lock another had just made anew, and both then held it. A run now holds the lock only when its own directory is the one entry in it, and otherwise steps back; the suite starts twelve runs against an old lock. What is left is the instant between a holder's check and its replacement of the file, in which a run that read the lock as old just before can still remove it. `tests/audit/structure.test.mjs` holds the two commands of the skill's State files section to the words, the argument counts and the states the script takes, and every other mention in the audit's files to a word it takes. The scope follows a measurement: of the 8.5 million tokens of that run, 79 percent were the session reading its own context again, a median of 107,000 tokens a turn, so text moved from the skill to a file read on a condition saves about one percent of a run, and a turn saved is worth more than the words. Not taken for that reason: a file of its own for the judging of a read-only check that exits 3, files for stage 1's conditional steps, run copies of `audit-evidence.md` without the sections a run cannot use, and a later read of `common.md`. The start again after a lock is taken in between has a case of its own: an `awk` first on the path removes the lock while the run rewrites the file, then runs the real one. The case holds only while the script finds `awk` by that name. Reopens: a registry update lost between two runs, a lock removed from a run that was alive, or a registry file the script refuses that a session wrote.

271. **The design documents hold what binds now; the history holds how they got there.** The documents had grown by addition. acceptance.md kept every run of every item and a record of runs that repeated them, 96,000 words of which the items' specs were under a tenth. requirements.md opened with 46 dated notes on what each change had amended, kept 35 more inside entries, several of which held a rule the entry's own text did not, and kept a stub for each of 31 retired entries. decisions.md kept entries whose subject was gone, and build-plan.md ordered milestones that had merged or that a decision had set aside, beside the list of prose findings that decisions 161 and 162 defer. Every reader of a document, and every session that loads one before a change, read past the account to find the rule. Now an acceptance item ends in its last result, one sentence that a new run replaces, and the pull request that carries a run says what else it showed. A requirement states what holds and cites the decision that holds the reason. A decision stays while it binds or holds evidence that a later decision or an open issue rests on, and goes when its subject is gone or another decision replaces it. Numbers are never reused, and each document's header lists the ones not in use. build-plan.md is removed, decisions 161 and 162 name the issues that hold the list they defer, and the unit of work is the pull request: the whole suite and lint run once per pull request (requirement 277), and the reviews of decision 172 recur by merged pull requests. Git history and the release tags keep every removed line. Not taken: cutting each remaining decision to its choice, its evidence and what reopens it, since nearly all bind as written and a cut risks a condition for a small saving. Reopens: a question about a past run or a removed entry that the history cannot answer.

272. **On Windows the script ends a test command's processes by id.** Decision 263 left two things open: the TERMs that still went to a group that can hold a native program, at a command's timeout and at the three stops of the `bg` mode (issue 193), and a native program's children outliving the KILL and holding the work dir (issue 190). Both rest on one fact of the runtime of Git for Windows: a signal it delivers to a native program ends that program's tree by the parent ids Windows records, and a recorded id outlives its parent. So on Windows `revert-tests.sh` sends no group a TERM. At a command's deadline, and before the KILL at a command's end, the run takes the group's processes from the runtime's own table, with every process the table lists under one of them, a parent before its children, and gives their Windows ids to one call of Windows PowerShell. The call lists every process with its parent id and start time, takes a listed process only when it started before the table was read, since Windows can hand the id of one that exited in between to a new process, takes a process for a child only when it started no earlier than its parent, and ends each one by its own id, in the order given, after checking that the id still names the process it listed. Each query and each end is given 30 seconds; a service that stops answering was not run, so whether that limit holds against one is unverified. At a command's deadline the watchdog wakes the run and the run ends the group. The roots come from the runtime's table because Windows keeps no parent link from a shell to what it forked and replaced by `exec`: by running, a walk of Windows ids alone from the `bg` child's group reached the shell that led a command's group and stopped there, and the batch file and the program under it stayed. Where `bg` or the leader of its child's group sends that group the TERM on another system, at the `bg` deadline, on a signal and when `bg` is lost, the run alone gets it, and answers it as it does there: it ends its command's group and removes the work dir. Only the run knows what it has started. Ending the run by id from `bg` was built first and not kept (issue 209): the call takes about a second to start and the run kept working meanwhile, so a test file it started in that second was in no list, outlived the run with no deadline, and ran on in a work dir that `bg` then removed. By running, with a setup that ended as `bg` got a TERM, the test file's shell was left in nine runs of nine, and with the TERM to the run alone the test file did not start in twenty of twenty; case 15n holds that. A step in the run's foreground, a git step among them, holds off the run's trap, so the leader lists what the run has in its own group, sends the TERM, and then ends the listed by id; what the run starts after the listing is in no list and is the run's to end. While the run is itself ending a command's group by id, the leader ends nothing: its list would hold that call's own processes, and the call ended part way would leave the native programs of a command whose shells it had already ended, with no group left for the run's cleanup to find. The run marks the call in the work dir, and the run's trap follows the call. The leader reads the mark after its listing and before the TERM, since the run answers the TERM by ending its command's group and marks that too: by running, with a stand-in for PowerShell that fails and the mark read 50 milliseconds after the TERM, the leader's line was missing from `.err` at the `bg` deadline and with `bg` killed. Leaving the call's PowerShell out of the list by name was built first and not kept: the list could still hold the shell that reads the call's answer and the shell about to become the call. Case 26 holds the mark with a shell that traps TERM and a `sleep` under it, and fails on the `sleep` without the check; case 15o sends the stop while the run ends the program a passed file left. By running, on processes the trial started: a job led by a batch file with three programs under it was ended whole in about a second while three programs whose parent had exited stayed; in the script's own launch shape the shells, the batch file and its program all ended; a program whose parent had exited before the group's end was not reached; given a time from before a process started, the call did not take that process, and given one from after, it ended it. A shell ended by id reports 0 to its parent and a native program the 137 the call passes, so a command that timed out on Windows records, beside the timeout, the status of the process that leads it: 0 for a shell, 137 for a native program. The script's text reaches PowerShell on stdin as one script block, so no execution policy applies to it; given on stdin as commands, a text with a parse error printed nothing and exited 0. Cases 5f to 5h of the suite return, and without the change 5f and 5g fail, at a passing file and at an interrupt; cases 5i and 15h hold that a TERM trap runs at a deadline elsewhere and not on Windows; cases 15j and 15k hold a batch file's program whose parent shell has exited, at the `bg` deadline and when `bg` is killed. This takes up what decision 262 did not, a second tool that ends each tree, since the TERM that made it unnecessary is the defect. Not taken: `taskkill` with its tree flag, whose rule for a child was not checked and is not the script's to set; ending only the native programs by id and leaving the shells to the KILL, which lets a shell start its next program between the two; a job object, which would hold every descendant, a parent's exit included, and needs compiled code inside the call; sending nothing and reporting what is left, which leaves a hung command running after the audit. When the call fails the script says so on stderr, once per process; a run that a signal ends while it waits on a command has no stderr by then and prints none. A command's group then gets the KILL alone, with the reach decision 263 left unverified, and a step in the run's foreground holds off the stop until it returns or the KILL comes 60 seconds later: cases 15l and 15m hold the first with a stand-in for PowerShell that fails, at the `bg` deadline and with `bg` killed. A KILL of the run in place of the TERM was not taken: the run's cleanup never runs, so its command's group outlives it with no deadline. Still there: the KILL that `bg` and its leader send 60 seconds later goes alone; a native child whose parent had already exited; the children of a native program that started while the table was read, which the KILL ends alone; a step the run starts in its foreground between the leader's listing and the TERM, which holds off the stop if it blocks, until that KILL, and the work dir then stays; on every system, a command the run starts as a TERM lands, between the command's start and the run's record of it (issue 210); the start of the call itself, which has no time limit; a signal from outside the script to one of its shells while that shell waits on a native program. Reopens: a run that ends a process it did not start; a host that refuses the call.

273. **The gate's skill states a rule in the step that acts on it, and a file's read point once.** The skill restated what its step files hold where they act: the bound of `max-passes`, the review, subagent and check budgets, that a cap is named in the report, the stop in the description, and the stand-in's mechanics in the Roles table. The report's notes repeated a fixed line of its template, and each supporting file opened by saying when a run reads it, which Supporting files and the last paragraph of the step before it already say. A run read each of these twice, and the copies could drift. The copies are cut, each only where a sentence with the same condition and the same consequence stands in a file a run reads before the action: `steps/3-next.md` for the stop and the caps, `steps/1-pass.md` and `steps/2-fix.md` for the three budgets, `steps/1-pass.md` for the stand-in, and Supporting files with each step's last paragraph for the read points. The sentence on where the gate sits among the plugins stated no rule and went too. Kept in the skill, since no other file holds them: the order of the run budget's sources, what stays after a cap, and when the reading block chooses the stand-in. The permission statement of `steps/0-preflight.md` stays as it is. The gate's files open with no read point, unlike the loop's, whose suite holds Supporting files to each file's opening (decision 254); the gate's suite reads Supporting files and each file's last paragraph. The skill's files went from 10,276 words to 10,028. Reopens: a run that skips a cap, the stand-in or a file that it reached before by a cut sentence.

274. **The primer's skill and its two reference files each state a rule once.** `SKILL.md`, `placement.md` and `shapes.md` restated one another. Init spelled out the hub's parts, cap and checks, which `shapes.md` holds; Adopt restated four placements and two rules of `shapes.md`; Commits and Proposals restated what the steps below them say; each reference file told its reader to read it; and `shapes.md` repeated its own import rules and the skill's rule for the person's own files. A run read a rule two or three times, and the copies could drift. A copy was cut only where the same condition and the same consequence stand in a file a run reads before it writes: `SKILL.md` loads whole in every mode and sends the run to both reference files before any write, move or proposal. Where two copies were each other's homes, the one in `shapes.md` stays, since every mode reads it: the rule for a README, under its own heading in place of the docs pages section, whose other sentences item 8 of `placement.md` and the caps list hold, and the rule for an untracked import. The sentence on naming a `docs/` file in words moved from Rules to the import rule, which covers a skill too, and the reason the hub holds no import moved to the Hub section. Kept, since a request about one item runs no other pass and only the copy covered it: "A `user` note is never proposed" in Proposals and the plan that lists every commit in Commits. Kept, since its conditions for a skill, a nested file and a rule differ from those of Maintain step 4 and `placement.md`: Init step 5. Kept, having no other home: that primer never installs a hook or writes a check, the form of a rule or nested-file proposal, "never route around it" for the drift hook, the definition of personal content, and "Delete no real constraint to shorten a file", which is wider than the cap's "to fit". Moving Init and Adopt into files of their own changes the file list of requirement 237 and when a mode's text loads, and is decision 276. The three files went from 4,872 words to 4,390. Reopens: a run that skips a rule that its mode's text held before and that it now reaches by another file.

275. **The audit's remaining copies go where their reader holds the home.** Decision 268 gave each instruction one home, and a later read found copies it had left. The skill summarized stage 1's steps and listed, under Supporting files, read points that the section of each command and stage already gives. The report template restated stage 8's closing lines and, for its Decisions and Claims sections, the shapes its Parts section holds. `live.md` and `work-items.md` listed each validator's messages and checks after the format rules those checks follow. Stages 2 to 5 restated what an agent's definition has the agent do, stage 1 opened with a list of its own steps and gave the fields of the reading block again, and the second opinion's request template carried the acknowledgment rule of stage 6. A copy was cut only where its reader holds the home before the action, by the reader rule of decision 268. Kept, since only the copy held them: under Supporting files, the read points of `report.md`, `work-items.md`, `live.md` and `audit-evidence.md` and the rule that a pointer is not a read; in the report template, the form of the `next:` block, the Other decisions part and the verdict of a claim with a live result; in `live.md`, the revision check, the equality rules for `query` and `env`, and the way out of a failed result copy. Two rules that only a cut list had held went to their homes first: compaction recovery names the current command's section beside the stage's, since `resume.md` and `handoff.md` are named there alone, and "The --live file" says that the frontmatter opens on line 1 and closes, that no other key or line is accepted and that no value is empty. Not taken: the Live checks block, which `report.md` and `live.md` each hold for a reader that lacks the other; the host and `--name-status` sentences of stage 1, which no other file states; the descriptions of what `revert-tests.sh` and `run-copies.sh` do; the merger's rules and the late adversary's list in stage 7 and the skeleton in stage 5, as decision 268 left them; and the lines that act and stage 8 print to the user. Moving split mode and the `_test` rules into files of their own changes when text loads and is decision 276. The skill and agent files went from 64,155 words to 62,800. In the reruns on this text, five of seven runs wrote stages 2 and 3 as not applicable without reading the files of those stages, against one of five on the text before, and one run read the files of stages 7 and 8 before those stages began. The read points of those files left the Supporting files list with this cut and stand in `SKILL.md` under each stage and at the end of stage 1. Neither stage applied in any of those runs, the entries written were the ones the files give, and every clause the acceptance items check came out as before, so the cut stands. Reopens: a run in which a stage skips a rule that its own file held before and that it now reaches in another file, or a run that starts stage 2 or 3 where it applies without the stage's file.

276. **Text that few runs use loads when a run needs it.** The primer's `SKILL.md` carried the steps of all three modes, stage 7 of the audit carried split mode on every run, and stages 2 to 6 carried parts of the `_test` rules that `fault-injection.md` lacked, though that file says the rules of each field are in it alone. A run read text it did not act on, and the fault rules had two homes. The steps of Init and Adopt went to `init.md` and `adopt.md`, and the mode choice has a full run read its file right after the mode line. A run can then print the mode line before that read, where it had printed it after its reads, at the head of its last message. The eval case's grader read the last message alone and failed 3 of 6 runs; the one failing run whose trace was kept had printed the line before the read. The grader now reads the trace for a message that starts with the mode line. The plan form and the order of the commits, which Adopt and Maintain share, stay in `SKILL.md` under Plans, so a maintain run reads neither new file. Split mode went to `stages/7-converge-split.md`. Step 5 of stage 7 reads it once the ledgers reach the threshold, before it builds a slice, and step 7 reads it again, so a run that lost the file to a compaction holds it before it launches a merger or merges itself. The audit lint checks the merger prompts and runs the slice commands where they now are. The top-up scope ids, what a planted map error records and how dropped acknowledgments are counted went to `fault-injection.md`, and each stage keeps one sentence where the fault acts. Kept in the stage: the role and the scope of each launch, which are the stage's own facts, and that the plant comes before the hash. The primer's `SKILL.md` went from 2,225 words to 1,736, stage 7 from 3,695 to 2,696, and stages 2 to 6 lost 100 words. Reopens: a run that acts on a moved rule before it has read the file that now holds it.

277. **After a compaction the loop reads `SKILL.md` again, and then the files of its current step.** The loop's `SKILL.md` is about 26,000 characters and named no recovery step. The host keeps only the first part of an invoked skill's text across a compaction, about 19,800 characters of a probe skill (decision 267), so after one a run went on without the end of the skill, from about Questions on: Failure rules, Configuration, Record, Final report handling and Supporting files, and nothing told it so (issue 215). A recovery section now sits after Approval scope and ends before character 12,000 of the body. It has the run stop, read `SKILL.md` again, then `record.md`, `steps.json` and its own section of `run.md`, which give the current step, then the files Supporting files names for that step, and before Step 0.5, with no record yet, `steps/0-preflight.md`. `tests/loop/structure.test.mjs` holds the section inside the first 15,000 characters and its read of `SKILL.md` (requirement 359). The skills of the gate and the primer are under 15,000 characters, so the host keeps them whole. Not shown by a run: no loop run was compacted and then checked. Reopens: a run that goes on after a compaction without a rule its skill holds.

278. **A failed attempt's output leaves its path before the relaunch.** Stage 4 said that a relaunch overwrites the output file, while the definitions of the auditor, adversary, digester and mapper make a file whose last line is `status: complete` final and have an agent that wakes after it end without writing. A relaunch takes the same output path. So when the failed attempt had left a complete file, by an injected failure or by an error returned after the file was written, the relaunched agent wrote nothing (issue 214). In the run that showed it the session moved the file aside on its own, counted the relaunch as its own error and not as a step of the ladder, and launched a third auditor, which listed the run directory and read the moved file. `SKILL.md`, Roles, now has the session move the output file a failed attempt left into that attempt's scratch folder as `failed-output.md` before the ladder relaunches the agent. Stage 6 held such a move for its own batches under another file name and now takes the one in Roles, and stage 4's sentence on overwriting is gone. A top-up that appends to a pass-one report keeps the restore from the saved copy that stage 4 gives it, and the merger's ladder relaunches no agent, so neither moves a file. `common.md` hard rule 2 has no agent read anything in another agent's scratch folder, so a relaunch does not start from the failed attempt. `tests/audit/structure.test.mjs` holds the move in Roles, that no other file moves a failed output by a rule of its own or says a relaunch overwrites, and the sentence in `common.md` (requirement 151). A run with the same injected failure on this text moved the file before the relaunch; the relaunch wrote its report, the stage entry held two auditors, one relaunch and no swap, and the relaunch listed the run directory, which shows the file's name, and opened nothing in the first attempt's folder. Shown for the auditor only; the adversary, digester and mapper take the same rule by reading. Reopens: a relaunch that ends without writing, or an agent that reads a file in another agent's scratch folder.

279. **Only an agent's own completion notification drives what follows it.** A run set a background watch on its pass-one auditor's output file. The watch ended when the file's last line was a status line, and the run took the end of the watch for the auditor's completion: it hashed the report, launched pass two and wrote the stage 4 read-only check while the auditor still ran. The auditor then wrote its report again, so stage 5's recorded input hash was the first version's, a pass-two verdict cited wording the final report no longer held, and the stage 4 check covered nothing the auditor did after it (issue 218). Three earlier runs set the same kind of watch and went on only after the auditor's own notification. `SKILL.md`, Queue, already said that each completion notification drives the next launch. It now says that only the agent's own notification counts, and that neither a status line at the end of a running agent's output file nor the end of a watch on that file is one. The sentence covers an agent the session launched that still runs, so a resume still acts on the files it kept. The auditor's second write stays as it is: its definition makes the file final once its last line is `status: complete`, and a write before the notification harms nothing once the session waits for the notification. `tests/audit/structure.test.mjs` holds the sentence (requirement 152). Not shown by a run: the early launch was seen in one of the four runs that set such a watch, so one more run could not show its absence. Reopens: a run that judges, hashes or launches on an agent's output before that agent's notification.

280. **A requirement may describe a state not yet built, and one item lists every such requirement.** An update that changes three plugins and adds a seventh was settled in the documents before any code, so that each change that builds a part has its requirements to read and to be reviewed against. No test and no lint rule reads the four documents, so they can run ahead of the tree; lint's literals, the catalog, the README, the hub and `package.json` cannot, since lint holds them to each other. Requirement 360 therefore names, by milestone, each requirement that describes a state not yet built, and has one commit of a milestone move every count the shipped files hold. A session that reads a requirement before changing behavior reads that item first and knows which sentences are ahead of the tree. The item leaves with the last milestone, and its number goes to the list of numbers not in use. Reopens: a pushed tree where lint and a shipped file disagree, or a change that reads an unbuilt requirement as built.

281. **A credential never lands in tool output.** A session that held the rule against printing a secret needed one value of a secret that keeps several under one name. To learn the key names it printed every value except those whose key name looked sensitive; no key name did, so every value landed in tool output. The same thing happened again later on the same secret, in a subagent whose brief also carried the rule. Both runs knew the rule and read a filtered print as following it. Apart from these, a log query that returned whole events put a bearer token and an API key in tool output. The item of decision 209 named a credential at rest in a file; tool output is kept in the session's transcript on disk, so it is a place of the same kind, and the item now says so, says that key names are what a session prints of a secret, and says that a query selects its fields (requirement 361). A filter is named because the filter was the misreading. No hook takes this on: nothing removes a value from output once it is printed, and a check before the call cannot know what a command will print. The item ships whatever else of the update is cut. Reopens: a value printed with the item loaded, which would move the check from the text to a wrapper the session calls for every secret read.

282. **The check on a finished draft is a skill, not more text in the style.** Across drafts written for other people, most of the owner's corrections matched a sentence that was loaded when the draft broke it. Drafts took between two and seven rounds. In one case the session's own review of its draft asked for more detail and made the draft longer. Where a check ran on the finished text, it caught what another loaded sentence had not. So the fix is a check at the point where the draft is made: the `draft` skill, which strikes and verifies and never adds detail, run by the session itself before the draft is first shown (requirements 363 and 364). The style gains one sentence that names it. The cost is one description in every session's startup context. Not taken: moving the whole Drafts section into the skill, since the short-sentence rule is reported as working and a skill that did not load would leave a draft with none; and an agent as the checker, since it cannot verify what the session did and costs a launch for every draft. Reopens: a draft shown without the skill having loaded, which would move the check's list into the style; or a check that lengthens a draft.

283. **Concise Plain 4.16: the findings form, the thread and the first draft.** The sentence that lists what each finding carries produced long comments tied to single instances, and each rewrite came to about a third of the length; it is replaced by a form with the general point first and its instances after, one recommended change, the change and whether it blocks in the title, and one summary comment for small points. A first draft on a thread copied the earlier message's greeting, lead-ins and sign-off, filled each topic with counts and tracker detail, and avoided the table the thread used; the earlier message now sets the density and the form, measured before any wording, and its form wins over the style's rules on tables and labels. That clause replaces the one in Tone on a thread's length and register. A first draft is the shortest text that carries the ask, with detail added on request, since drafts took up to seven rounds and a first version on a thread missed on density; the bullet on the detail level is rewritten to say so. A draft that will be posted is shown whole in the reply that asks to post it. An id carries a few words of its item's title at first mention. A working link in a copy for pasting is a rule the style already holds, so it goes to the check on the finished draft and gets no second sentence (requirement 364). Where an added sentence replaces none, the change names the cut that pays for it, since the style is loaded in every session (requirement 366). The sentence that names the `draft` skill replaces none, and the cut that pays for it is the Drafts sentence against presenting a settled choice as a question or a proposal as agreed: the skill's check strikes a decision handed to the reader that is the sender's to settle, and writes what another person will do as asked, never as agreed. The chat block loads no skill, so it keeps that sentence, and its list of deliberate differences says so. Reopens: a comment in the new form that an author could not act on without the old list's parts.

284. **An email draft is one HTML file.** Keeping a Markdown copy and an HTML copy of one draft in step failed, and doubled every edit while it lasted. In another case a Markdown draft had to be made again as HTML before it could be sent. An email draft is now one HTML file, the only stored copy, and the reply shows its text; every other draft is Markdown (requirement 365). Reopens: a reader of email drafts who needs Markdown, or a second kind of draft that must be pasted with its formatting.

285. **A session logs what it learned about its rules and never folds it in itself.** When logged proposals were checked at review time against the files as they then were, most changed: one repeated an error, one named a section that did not exist, and two wanted different text on the same lines. So a session records observations with their evidence, and a separate review writes the edit (requirements 362 and 398). The item is a house rule, since it has to hold in every session, with or without primer. The directory is fixed under the configuration directory, so the item needs no setting and primer finds the logs without depending on `rules`. An entry has no form beyond its `Recommended` section, since a form would cost words in every session and the review reads prose. An entry is appended to the day's file, since several sessions share that file and one that wrote it whole would drop another's entry. A rule that visibly worked is logged too: it is the only evidence so far for keeping a rule when room is short. Not settled: nothing yet measures whether a folded rule prevents a repeat. Reopens: logs that no review reads over a release, or a permission prompt on the write that makes sessions skip it. An entry now names its repository, and one sent to a log that loaded context names is not read by primer (decision 328).

286. **`review`: a seventh plugin, for a pull request someone else wrote.** The first of two reviews of other people's pull requests ran the gate in its mode without fixes, then the audit, then went on by hand; the second was done by hand. The gate's run ended `unconverged` because it found something, which is a failure state for an outcome that is the purpose of a review; the audit's act stage and its ticket edits take the branch and the tickets to be the user's. What those reviews needed came after the first reading: the full list of sites, one hop out from the diff, citations read again at the head, a disposition by the team's conventions, two texts, and a posting step. The loop, the gate and the audit each call or build their own review, so there is no shared engine with a read-only end to add a mode to. The plugin therefore takes its first reading from the host's review or from a gate or audit report and owns what follows (requirement 367). It is named `review`; its skill is `run`, by the rule of decision 35, since the bare `/review` is the host's own. Reopens: a finding engine the plugins share with a read-only end state, which would make this a mode of it; or a host review that runs these checks itself. Decision 323 adds two checks the plugin owns and widens what its stand-in reading is asked for.

287. **The review's record lives outside the reviewed repository, and the pull request's head is untrusted.** The loop, the gate and the audit keep their records under an ignored directory of the repository, which is the user's. In a repository that belongs to someone else, the ignore entry is a change to their tree and a record there can be staged by a broad `git add`. An agent briefed as read-only also left copies of files in the system's temporary directory. The record, the drafts and the scratch worktree are therefore under the plugin's data directory, each brief says where an agent may write, the run compares the reviewed work tree's status before and after, and it lists what it created (requirement 370). The head is the author's: a settings file, a hook, an instruction file, a skill or a server definition in it would load into a child session started there, a hook of the repository can run on a checkout or a merge, and its checks run with what the user's machine can reach. So the child session loads no configuration from the head and has no check of the repository on its allow-list, every `git` command in the scratch worktree runs with the hooks path set to an empty directory, and code from the head runs only after the user approved that by name (requirements 369 to 371). Not observed before the first reading was built: a child session in a worktree whose head plants a hook and an instruction line, under each way of starting it, and which way loads neither. Until that is observed the first reading is a stand-in agent of the session, given a patch of the change and the worktree's path, and the skill starts no child session. Observed: on Windows a tree whose relative paths are 245 characters long, in a worktree under a record path of about 205, fails `git worktree add` with `Filename too long`; with `core.longpaths=true` the checkout succeeds, and a later `git status` without it shows the files as deleted and with it shows a clean tree. So every `git` command in the scratch worktree carries that setting beside the hooks path. The cost of the record's place is that it goes with an uninstall. Reopens: a record lost that way, a forge that needs a file in the work tree, or configuration or code from a head that ran unasked.

288. **One complete round is the aim of a first review.** The cost a review can cut is a second batch of findings after the author fixed the first. Three checks did the most: a list of sites built from one spelling of a name held 15 of 20; consumers of the changed output and the documents that state the changed rule sat outside the diff; and reading every cited location again at the head made three corrections. The first fix proposed for a blocking finding was wrong, and a run showed it, so a fix is run before it is recommended or marked `not run`. The worst defect in one change was first written down as an unverified note outside the scope, so such a point gets a short trace (requirements 372 and 373). A follow-up reports each earlier item as landed or open, follows the author's reply to the place it names, and settles the conflict status by a trial merge, since a branch the forge showed as free of conflicts had three conflicting files (requirement 376). Not yet exercised, and left out until it is: a ledger for a follow-up that maps each thread to the check that proved it, runs those checks again on the new head, proposes which threads to resolve, and records a finding new in round two as missed by round one, which would measure the aim. Also not yet exercised: a vote, and a post on GitHub; acceptance item 96 is their first run. Reopens: a round two whose misses come from none of the three checks, or a follow-up that needed the ledger. Decision 323 takes the first: two more checks, from findings none of the three would have reached.

289. **Posts are made from files and compared by a script, and a forge posts only when it offers all four operations.** A posting step stopped after its first comment, and reading the threads again kept that comment from going up twice. Each draft is a file, each post is made from its file and recorded before the next, and `posts.mjs` compares what the forge holds with the drafts, since equality of two texts is not a judgment (requirements 377 and 379). A vote is no thread text, so it is cast only when the user's vote on the forge differs from it. The script opens no connection, so its suite replaces nothing. Posting needs a comment, its anchor to a file and line, a vote and the threads with their status; where a forge's tool gives fewer, the step writes a paste-ready export, which is what both reviews used (requirement 378). A thread's resolution, a ticket edit and a merge are outward actions too: each runs only where the session has a tool for it and the user approved it by name.
