The summary for the person who gates the merge is posted only as a comment approved by name, and is otherwise the user's to send. Requirements 226 and 230 now name the loop and the gate, since their sentences were written before a plugin whose purpose is to read and post on someone else's pull request. Reopens: a forge whose tool gives some of the four and whose users want those posted.

290. **Four report items for the audit and the gate.** Run on other people's pull requests, neither report held the full list of sites to fix: one said "several" and one gave half as all. A child review had its reads refused and its report had no line saying so; the gate has since reported refused calls, and the audit's agents do not. A ticket state was flagged that the team's own table makes correct; the table was loaded in the main session and was in none of the briefs. Evidence comments named a commit older than the head. Requirements 381 to 384 take each one. The gate runs no search of its own for sites, so its report lists every site its reviews named, and the audit lists every site it found with the search that found them. Reopens: a report that still gives a sample for a list, or a brief that lacks a convention the session held.

291. **Primer gets two profiles, and `local` lands in primer.** Some repositories may hold no agent file in their tree or history. Primer wrote every destination into the work tree, committed with a scope that names the plugin, marked the hub with a comment that names it, and stopped when the root instruction file was ignored, so it could not be used there. The `local` profile keeps the same placement judgment and puts the result in a store outside the repository (requirement 385). It is in primer and not a new plugin, since the judgment is primer's; the two conditions for splitting it out are not met, Node being the suite's own prerequisite and the profile's steps sitting in a file read only in a `local` run. The profile is asked once with no default, since a wrong silent choice either writes agent files where none may appear or hides context a team expects in the tree. In a `shared` repository that is not the user's own, the fallback commit scope names no plugin (requirement 396). Not settled: what the marker does there. It stays until that is picked, since the drift hook and the notice key on it and neither can read the map. The other answer drops it and has the map entry stand for it, which costs those two hooks a Node start in every repository without a marker and a second way for a run to find its mode; a tracked comment that names the plugin shows what the scope was changed to hide. Also not settled: what a user with no notes starts from in `local`, and what that first pass costs. Reopens: `SKILL.md` over its cap, a loader that needs a second runtime, or the marker question picked. Decision 319 makes `docs: <subject>` the fallback in every repository. Decision 330 drops the `own` answer and leaves the marker question open.

292. **The store, and how a repository is matched.** A repository is matched by a remote, written as guard writes it, since a remote is the same on every machine and in every worktree; one without a remote is matched by its main work tree's root, found from the git common directory as the notice keys auto memory. A group names sibling repositories that share a layer. There are three layers: what holds in every mapped repository is stated once, siblings share what only they need, and what names a person, a date or a path of one machine stays on it. The store may be a directory the user versions and shares, so the machine's layer and `config.json` sit outside it. The map also lists the directories whose repositories the user expects to be mapped, so the warning for an unmapped repository fires there and not in every repository on the machine (requirements 386 and 389). Reopens: a need for a layer of one repository inside a group, two machines whose remotes for one repository differ, or an unmapped repository outside every listed directory that went unnoticed.

293. **The loader runs one hook entry, which prints no string in a resumed session.** The documentation says a hook's context string is capped at 10,000 characters, measured per hook and per field even when several hooks answer one event; that a longer string reaches the session as a file path and a preview it is not asked to open; that a session gets the context of every hook that answers; and that injected context is to be factual statements. So each layer was to get its own entry and its own cap (requirement 388). Context once sat over the cap for days with nothing reporting it, so the loader measures what it emits and warns at every start. A check that timed out was once injected as a failure and stalled a run no one attended, so the loader's `git` calls have a time limit inside the entry's timeout, a load that ran out is reported as `unknown` apart from failed, and the checks and warnings are skipped in a subagent and in such a session. Sessions asked whether their context had loaded, so each string closes with a canary that `status` prints, at the end since a preview keeps only the start (requirement 389). An entry starts Node at every session start in every repository on a machine with primer, whatever its profile, and the first spike records what that adds; if it matters, the entry gets the test for primer's own directory that the drift hook has. Five things were to be observed before the loader merged: that three entries of about 9,000 characters each arrive whole; that one entry's `systemMessage` and `additionalContext` both arrive; which session sources keep an earlier string; whether a subagent's context holds the string, per agent type; and which signal marks a session no one attends or a child session, the variable of decision 207 being undocumented. If the first fails, the loader emits one string and the tightest repository's total sets the budget. None of the five was observed before the loader was built or merged, since each needs a session to watch, so the loader took the case in which the first fails: one entry emits the three layers as one string under one cap, on every session source, with no entry on `SubagentStart`. The five were observed after, in sessions no one attended and with a test plugin in the loader's place, whose hooks printed the strings; no attended session was watched. Three entries of 9,000 characters each arrived whole, on two runs, in an order the host chose. An entry of 10,000 characters arrived whole and one of 10,001 as a preview and a file path; the text was ASCII, so characters and bytes were not told apart. The `additionalContext` reached the session and the `systemMessage` did not; whether a person is shown it was not observed. A resumed session still held the string of its start. After `/compact` and after `/clear` the session held none of it, and held a string printed on that source. The general-purpose, Explore and Plan agents each held none of the session's string and held one that a `SubagentStart` entry printed, and `SessionStart` did not run for them. The variable of requirement 341 was `0` in those sessions and in their subagents, and a subagent's start names its agent type; the variable's value in an attended session was not observed. So the entry prints no string on the source `resume`, where it added a second copy at every resume, and still prints its warnings there; on every other source, one not yet seen among them, it prints the string. A layer changed since a session's start therefore reaches a resumed session only after `/clear` or `/compact`. The loader stays at one entry: three would start Node three times at every session start and arrive in no fixed order, and no repository's string is known to be over the cap, so an entry for each layer waits for the size warning to report one, and the script keeps the per-layer form of its verb for that day. No entry runs on `SubagentStart` yet: requirement 388 has one run for the agent types that load instruction files in a `shared` repository, which types those are was not observed, and a brief names the layers' files meanwhile (requirement 383). The warnings are still skipped where the variable is `0`. Measured for the first spike, as medians of 20 runs: the entry adds about 73 ms to a session start where primer's directory is absent or the map has no entry for the repository, and about 214 ms where the map lists a directory above the repository and `git` runs. That is paid once per session, and the entry returns before `git` where the map is empty, so it gets no further test. Not settled: whether the loader can compare what it emitted with what the session received, beyond the size check and the canary. Reopens: a host change to the cap, the events or the signal; an attended resumed session that cannot give a layer's canary; a repository whose string is over the cap; which agent types load instruction files, once observed.

294. **The staging check denies, and runs inside the drift hook's entry.** In a `local` repository an agent file that reaches a commit cannot be taken back from a pushed history, so the check denies a `git add` or a commit that would stage one the upstream does not track, and denies when it cannot read the upstream's tree. A path the upstream tracks is the repository's own and passes. A line added to a `.gitignore` that names an agent file shows what the file would, so it is denied too; the lines go in `info/exclude`, which git never shares. The drift hook is POSIX sh and cannot read the map, so it hands a `git add` or a `git commit` to the script, which answers only for a `local` repository. It first handed one over only in a repository whose hub has no marker. A `cd` into a `local` repository from a marked one or from outside any repository, and a `local` repository whose hub carries the marker, then went unchecked, so the hub's first line no longer decides. It starts Node only for such a command and only where primer's own directory exists, so every other shell call still starts one process and a machine without the profile pays nothing. A command the script cannot resolve, such as an add and a commit in one command or a change of directory it cannot follow, is taken to stage every file git does not ignore, so it is denied while an agent file is unignored and passes once the lines are in `info/exclude`. A forced add in such a command passed with those lines in place, so what each add or commit of the command that the script can read would stage is counted too. A change of directory the script could not follow was checked against the working directory alone, which let an add through in a `local` repository reached from another one, so every `local` repository of the map is checked there. A command with no add or commit in it was denied in a `local` repository for a here-document or a variable the script could not read, so only a text that may run one out of sight still counts, and since where it runs is unknown, it is checked in every `local` repository of the map as well. The check reads the shell text of a session's ordinary commands and is no boundary: an `eval`, a subcommand or a program name held in a variable, a git alias, a script and another program that runs git are not read. A hook of git's own under the git directory would see every form; it is not taken here, since whether the host lets a session write there was not observed. An error inside the script ends it with a status other than 0, and the hook denies on that. A missing `sh` or `node` lets the call through, as the drift hook's entry already does, since a hook that cannot start cannot deny; the README says so. Which forms of `git add` resolve was observed: `git add --dry-run` resolves `.`, `-A`, `-u`, a file, a directory, a glob, `-f`, `-N`, `--renormalize`, `--chmod`, paths after `--` and a `-C` before the subcommand, and stages nothing; `-p` and `-i` end with status 128 without a terminal, as does a pathspec that matches nothing; `-e` ignores `--dry-run` and stages; and `--pathspec-from-file` reads input the hook does not hold. So the check takes `-p`, `-i`, `-e` and `--pathspec-from-file` as calls it cannot resolve. `git commit --dry-run --porcelain` with `-a`, `-i`, `-o` or paths shows what the commit would hold, but with none of them it rewrites the index, optional locks off or not, so for a commit of the index alone the check reads the staged names with `git diff --cached`. Whether the host lets a session write under the git directory was not observed, so the skill shows the lines for the user to add where the host refuses the write (requirements 390 and 391). Not taken: a deny on a commit message that carries a session attribution line. The host adds that line by a setting that decisions 136 and 170 leave to the user, and no such commit has been seen in a `local` repository; the profile's first run proposes the setting instead. Reopens: an agent file, or an attribution line, that reached a commit in a `local` repository.

295. **Context size is measured, and an addition names its cut.** Estimates of size were wrong in ways a script is not: the wrapper lines count, line endings differ between files, a shared layer costs in every repository, and the tightest repository sets the budget. The cuts that paid best were, in order, text about how a file is loaded, text a pointed-to document also holds, text the loader or the host already supplies, and expired facts. A copied release date later produced a wrong date in a message, so a pointer to a source the user keeps current is preferred, and a copied fact carries its date and an expiry. A hand edit inside the managed house rules block stopped later updates being offered, so a proposal aimed there carries a warning with where the line belongs. No file states how much room is left, since the number is wrong after the next edit (requirements 392 and 393). Reopens: a layer over its cap after a proposal that showed the measure.

296. **Fold-back runs two passes and keeps a ledger.** Checking logged proposals at review time changed most of them, so the edit is written then, each claim is marked verified or not with the kind of check, and a first pass on evidence runs before a second on worth, placement and wording. The review ends in a pick, since a ranked list left the choosing undone. The logs belong to no one repository, so one stamp marks them reviewed for all (requirement 397). The ledger gives each item an id and a status, joins items across logs, brings a held item back with its count when a session trips on the same gap again, and records a later item that matches a folded rule as that rule's failure, whose fix is to reword it, move it or propose a hook. A rule that was loaded and missed is a miss, fixed by delivery or a pointer and never by new text (requirements 398 and 399). Not settled: how to tell that a rule worked beyond the entries that say so, and how a rule that has not failed is retired other than by a cut for room. Reopens: an item folded twice, or a held item that never returns though its gap recurs.

297. **Copies are checked against a snapshot, and mining waits for a pilot.** A port of context text to a second place had to be asked for repeatedly, and twice the earlier version of the source was gone, so a copy is declared with its source and a snapshot taken at each port, and its status is reported by bytes (requirement 400). For mining, the strongest signals so far were found by hand, by reading transcripts for what the user repeated, and each review rebuilt its own reader. One mining pass over reviewers' comments cost about 975,000 agent tokens for 5 changes, most candidates being dropped as already written somewhere or as generic advice. So the reader is built once, with no model call, and skips what it cannot read, since the host documents the transcript format as internal and changing between versions. The detection on top of it is untested, and is built only after a pilot shows it surfaces what the hand review found for fewer model tokens (requirement 401). Reopens: the pilot's result, either way. Decision 331 lets a copy have more than one source.

298. **Not taken in this update.** For drafts: relaxing "one idea each" in the short-sentence rule, where clipped sentences were seen in one message and the rule is reported as working elsewhere; settling the venue and the sender before drafting, on one case, which the review covers for reviews; looking for earlier messages of the same kind where there is no thread, on one case; a short label on a paragraph where no thread shows one, which was a reading of hand edits to one message; showing only the changed paragraph of a long revised draft, which the owner did not raise and whose opposite is reported as working; linking every occurrence of an id, asked for in one draft with many ids; taking the cutoff of "since the last report" from the sent time of the message it follows, on one case caught before the draft; building a status update from reconciled sources and treating another tool's summary as unverified, both of which worked under the existing item on claims; and an HTML copy for chat messages. For primer: a transform that ports rules to another runtime, which is heavy and serves a runtime outside this update; searching before an item is logged, since the search at review time is settled and a rule that exists and was missed is worth logging; comparing a written convention with observable practice, on one case; saying what a tool sees in a structured source that a person does not, on one case; start-up checks the user defines, which are the user's own hook; and an owner field on each fact. Each reopens on a second case of its failure.

299. **Another repository's instructions reach a session that edits there.** A session that edited files in a repository other than the one it started in missed a convention on 16 files. The documentation describes instruction files loading for subdirectories of the working directory and for a directory the session moves to or adds, and says nothing of a second repository edited without either. The reach check therefore answers the first edit in another repository with its layers or the paths of its instruction files, and keeps one small file per session so that it answers only the first; nothing removes those files yet (requirement 395). Not observed before the check was built, since it needs a session to watch: a session edits a file in a second repository whose hub states a fact found nowhere else, with and without that repository added as a directory, and is asked for the fact. It was observed after, in sessions no one attended: once the session had written a new file there, and once it had read and edited a file already there, it did not know the fact, with the repository added as a directory and without. The host covers neither case, so the hook ships for every repository other than the session's own, and comes out for each case a later host is seen to cover. Measured, as medians of 20 runs of the script: an edit in the session's own repository takes about 54 ms, 8 ms more than the start of Node alone; the first edit in another repository about 229 ms; and a later edit there about 55 ms. Two neighbors are settled with it: a brief names every instruction source the session loaded (requirement 383), and text written for an agent is checked against the house rules' item on such prose before it is saved (requirement 393). Reopens: a host release that loads a second repository's instruction files on an edit.

300. **An acceptance item expects only what its sources and its Setup allow.** Items 3, 29, 35, 48, 49, 67 and 69 each expected a result that a requirement, a decision or the text under test ruled out on the day the item was written, or one its Setup could not produce, so a correct run read "passed in part"; item 86 was written from a design and recorded as failed for the same reason (decisions 252 and 259). No line said what an item may expect, and the hub sent a session to `acceptance.md` before running or recording an item, while these were written with a design, which is neither. The lead of `acceptance.md` now says that an item expects nothing its requirements, decisions or the text under test rule out, and nothing its Setup cannot produce, and the hub names writing an item beside running and recording it. Read against that line, 12 of the 21 items written or changed since those seven were corrected failed it, and are reworded. Item 21 expected the pull request checks from a push to `main`. Item 28 expected nothing else written, where the profile answer writes the map. Item 92 barred a statement that the draft skill lets stand with a note. Item 94's pull request could be the user's own, which the review refuses. Item 95 expected a reply on a thread that item 94 never posts, and the mode in the reading block, which prints before the record is read. Item 97 expected an export from a run that does not post, in a form without the head and base the review needs. Item 99 expected a commit from `init`, which makes none, and an unchanged status after a question whose yes changes it. Item 100 could be read as expecting whole layers from a string over the cap. Item 101 let one call stage and commit `AGENTS.md`, which the check denies, and took for granted the ref the upstream's tree is read from. Item 102 expected the over-cap warning in a repository not mapped `local`, and a canary from a string over the cap. Item 104 counted a rule as worked that the ledger did not hold as folded, and expected a held item back from a log and a run its Command did not have. Item 106 ran over two layers where its Setup had one. No result changes but item 21's, which takes the checks of the last merged pull request and the run on the push of its merge: each of the others is of a run on the earlier text. Item 108 stays as it is: its Setup names a fixture not yet built, which is what the item says is still to do. No check holds the line, since reading an item against its sources is a review. Reopens: an item written after this entry that a run shows to contradict its sources or its Setup.

301. **The rule for tests of prose is a requirement for every plugin.** Decisions 239, 254 and 269 each took the sentence pins out of one plugin's suite and recorded the rule for that plugin alone; requirement 353 stated it for the gate and requirement 18 for the house rules. The decisions are read before a design choice is reversed, not before a test is written, so the suites those decisions did not reach kept their pins and gained more. The suite of the rules plugin's skills held 51 cases that looked up about 40 phrases of the skills, and the primer skill's 32 cases about 100; the cases written after decision 269 for the draft skill, the local profile, the budget and the fold-back review were again one lookup of a sentence each. Requirement 402 states decision 269's rule for every plugin, and requirement 353 keeps the gate's list and points at it. By that rule the two suites now hold 21 and 17 cases. Kept, as what the host reads: the frontmatter, the description phrases, the tool lists, the caps and the files of each skill directory. Kept or added, as agreement: the handoff prompt skill's copy of a `core.md` item and the chat block's copy of the skill's definition, found by their place and no longer by their opening words; the one line of the style that names the draft skill; the mode line against the pattern of its eval grader; every script command the primer's files show against the verbs and options its script reads; the stamps against the notice hook; the caps and the marker of `shapes.md` against the drift hook; and the `info/exclude` lines against the agent files the staging check knows. Four literals stay for the harm their loss allows: `--no-verify` is named only to bar it, a forced `init` never writes over an existing `CLAUDE.md` or `AGENTS.md`, the list of what is never edited keeps its eight entries, and a `local` run writes nothing in the work tree and makes no commit. The rest went to the acceptance items the requirements of those skills already name, or to review, and requirement 255 now names review alone. Outside the two suites: two cases of `rules.test.mjs` that requirement 18 already ruled out, a phrase of the Windows paragraph and one of `core.md`, read the shipped text instead; the release test holds the `plugin_version` literal and not the audit's line around it; the audit's test of the refused line, written after decision 269 as one sentence looked up in five files, holds the two forms that stage 8 searches for; and the review's suite finds the carried item and the settings of `git <c>` without the words beside them. Requirement 21's eight items stay as literals whose loss removes a stop, and requirement 354's test reads headings. A reading of the loop's and the audit's suites found 14 cases that held a sentence or found their target by one, the refused line's among them, and 13 are cut. Six went to review with no check left: the list of Step 0's headings, the Mode words of the reading block, the description cap in `pr-body.md`, the three outcomes of an open item, the order in which Step 6 reads two files, and the slug examples. The two tests that decisions 278 and 279 wrote to hold a sentence went too: the first keeps only its scan that no file but the skill holds the move of a failed output, the second is gone, and requirements 151 and 152 name what is left. The others keep their check and lose the sentence around it: Step 5's items are held by their ids, the credential keys and the configuration keys are found by the keys themselves, the opening action by the labels of the reading block, the sections the committed report keeps and those it leaves out by the template's headings, each list in its own sentence, the record path, the `steps.json` key and the keys of an export by their place in the files of both plugins, the audit's table by its indent, and two paragraphs by the literals in them. The fourteenth stays as it was: the audit's lint looks for the words "line count" in the three steps that write a merger's prompt, because the merger's own file tells it to stop at the count its prompt gives, which is how a file read short is caught (decision 70), so the words are what two files with two readers must agree on and not a sentence of one. Requirements 215 and 396 name no test. Across the suites, 41 breaks of a rule in a shipped file each failed the check that holds it, and seven rewordings of a sentence each passed, the two of `rules.test.mjs` among them; the release test's case and the cases whose way of finding a line alone changed were not broken. Not settled: the same reading could not place about 19 lists of headings, labels or table rows that no script reads, in the loop's, the audit's and the gate's suites, most of which decisions 239 and 254 keep as structure, and the lint's rule on a step file's hand-off holds five verb forms (requirement 219); they stand as structure until one is shown to hold a sentence. Reopens: as decisions 239, 254 and 269, a regression that a sentence pin would have caught and that no contract check, eval case or acceptance item does.

302. **One home is a requirement for every plugin, for text written from now on.** Decisions 253, 268, 273 and 274 each cut restated instructions from one plugin after copies had come to disagree, and each recorded its rule for that plugin alone. Requirement 403 states the rule of decisions 253 and 268 for every plugin: an instruction has one home, and another file repeats it only for a reader that does not load the home. It binds text written from now on, and the copies those decisions kept stay. The case against it is not answered. The first commits of the loop's skill and of the audit's state the intent, step text in the files read at their step and `common.md` as the one home of the hard rules, and the restatements arose anyway; and no check can stand in for the rule, since the copies were restatements, which no comparison of text finds. It is taken because four decisions had to cut this class one plugin at a time and its rule sat in no requirement, which is where the hub sends a session before it changes a plugin. The text written in the two days after those decisions shows the gap: a reading of it found copies whose reader holds the home in the audit, the gate, the loop, the primer and the review, and some already differ from their home. The audit's five agent definitions give the refused line without the exception for a retried read that `common.md` holds; the review's posting file has a changed draft shown whole again where the skill has it shown and recorded again; and stage 7's copy of the merger's rule on sites lacks its last clause. None is cut here: each cut changes a plugin, which then needs its version (requirement 14), so they are left to a change of each plugin. Reopens: two copies found to disagree in text written after this entry, which shows a requirement does not hold the rule either.

303. **The house rules say what a test of prose checks.** Two items ask for tests wherever the rules are installed: expected values are literals, and a behavior change gets a test. A change to a skill's text is a behavior change, and the test that meets both items is a literal sentence of the skill. Suites written under them pinned sentences in the gate, the loop, the audit, the skills of the rules plugin and the primer (decisions 239, 254, 269 and 301): a cut cost as many edits as an addition, and a pin could not tell a rule that held from one that had only kept its wording. Requirement 402 reaches only a session on this repository, while the two items load everywhere, so the Tests section gains a fourth item: a test of prose written for an agent checks only what the host, a script or another file reads, and a run or a review checks the rest (requirement 404). The cost: 27 words with no cut named, in every install, and an installed block reads `stale` once; `core.md` holds 35 rules. The chat block does not carry the item, since it covers replies and not files, and no plugin carries a copy. Not taken: one home as a house rule, for which no case outside a plugin's prose is recorded. Reopens: a regression in prose written for an agent that a test of its sentence would have caught and that nothing read by the host, a script or another file, no run and no review does.

304. **One version for a run of related work on a plugin.** From 2026-11-10 to 2026-11-13 each pull request carried its own release: 32 release commits and 31 tags in three days. Requirement 14 asks only that a changed plugin carry a version above its last tag, so nothing asked for a version per pull request, and decision 173 has a refactor bundle name the release it needs without saying whether two may share one. In the day after, nine pull requests ended in nine tags, and the gate, the primer and the review each took a second version within a day of the first, for fixes that runs found after the merge. The release skill now says that one pull request per plugin carries a run of related work, its fixes as separate commits, with one version and one tag; that a fix users need at once ships alone, and a pull request too large to review is split; and that a change that needs several plugins at once is one pull request with one version and one tag for each, as the report items of the audit, the gate and the loop were. The cost: one failing part holds the rest of its pull request. Reopens: a fix that users needed and that waited on unrelated work in its pull request.

305. **The copies found after decisions 253 to 274 are cut, each to its home.** Decision 302 named copies whose reader holds the home and left them to a change of each plugin. Each is cut only where every reader of the copy loads the home, and where copy and home differed one text was chosen. The audit: the five agent definitions no longer restate the refused line, which the output contract of `common.md` holds with its exception for a retried read. Every agent closes its file as that contract says and the report names an output with no refused line, so the line is not one of the rules decision 148 keeps in each definition, whose breach a run cannot see. Stage 7's copy of the merger's rule on sites, which decision 268 keeps, gains the clause it lacked; step 6 of stage 8 names hard rule 3 and takes the `heads:` values from `report.md` in place of restating both; and the report's Refused calls line no longer repeats how stage 8 gathers it. The gate: a run with no record is described once, in the step that ends a run, and a brief names the instruction files by rule 1 of Approval scope. The loop: the build brief names the instruction sources by Roles and models, and that the `code-review` skill takes no request text stands in the skill alone. The primer: what a layer's text is stands in `local.md`, and what goes in `machine.md` stands in `placement.md`, which gains the path of this machine. The review: a changed draft is shown and recorded again by the skill's one sentence, where the posting file said shown and left out recorded; Out of scope lists only what the skill says nowhere else; the order of the drafts stands where their names are given; and the rule that the comments on both pull requests of a pair, and the summary, state the dependency and the merge order moves from the follow-up file to Drafts, so a first review of a pair follows it too (requirement 375). The audit's test of the refused line holds the two forms in the contract and in stage 8's search, the agreement that is left. Reopens: an agent output with no refused line in a run, which would put the line back in the definitions; or a first review of a pair whose comments should not state the dependency.

306. **A blocked run's report gives both ways out of a fix round left in the tree.** A check that ran past its budget ended a run `blocked` with the fix round's edits in the work tree, uncommitted, since nothing had shown that the fixes pass. The next run ends `blocked` at preflight while the tree is dirty, and the report's commands said neither what to do with the edits nor what the next run would then do. The check was only slow: stopped at the default of 15 minutes, it passed in 18 when run alone. The report's Blocked or stopped section now gives both ways. Keeping the edits is the check run by hand, a commit and a push, after which the next run starts a new sequence, since the head is no longer the one the pass reviewed. Dropping them leaves the head as reviewed, so `resume.mjs` returns `repeat` and the pass is made again from its saved reviews, under the same budget. A budget raised in `.reimagine.json` has to be committed and pushed, since the next run needs a clean tree and a branch that is not ahead of its remote, and that moves the head, so the run after it starts a new sequence whether the edits were kept or dropped; the report says so. The README says to set `gate.timeouts.check` to hold the slowest check with room and lists the ending under `blocked`. Not taken: a later run that takes up the fix round from the tree, as the `post-<k>` files describe it, and runs the checks again. It would commit edits the run did not just make, on a comparison of hashes, and the budget that stopped the check is still too small unless `.reimagine.json` changed, which has to be committed and so moves the head; one run is the evidence. Reopens: a second fix round lost to a check budget that was set as the README says.

307. **The audit makes the branch slug as the loop and the gate do.** The audit's handoff built `<branch-slug>` without removing a trailing `-`, which the loop and the gate remove. For a branch whose slug ends in `-`, such as a long name cut at a separator, it looked for the loop's record under another name, found none, and wrote the handoff without the plan, the review log and the commit lines. This was read from the files and not seen in a run. The handoff's rule gains the clause. No script makes the slug, so a test holds the three files to one rule: lowercase, the 60 characters and the trailing `-` (requirement 402). Reopens: a fourth file that makes the slug, which would move the rule into a script the others call.

308. **Item 44's targets for the audit and the primer suites are their last recorded times with a tenth added.** Item 44 held `test:audit` to the baseline of `revert-tests.sh` divided by the tick ratio, 31 seconds, and every other area script to 30. Its last run took 628.8 s for `test:audit` and 229.6 s for `test:primer`. Timed alone, `readonly.sh` takes 521.5 s, about 220 s of it a fixture built for each case and most of the rest process starts, `revert-tests.sh` 439.2 s and `hook.test.mjs` 293.0 s, and no saving that was measured brings either suite near its target. The two targets become 692 and 253 seconds, those times with a tenth added, and stay there, so a run fails when a suite grows; `npm test` keeps half the baseline and the other area scripts keep 30 seconds. Not taken: no target for the two, which would let them grow unseen. The savings found, a built fixture copied for each case of `readonly.sh`, the hook tests run concurrently and a shorter lock wait in the guard test, are left to a change of their own. Reopens: those savings, which set the targets from the new times.

309. **An instruction file's model for reviews is the reviewing agent's.** The first reading passed the session's own model to its agent, and the skill also says the user's instruction files win. A session whose instruction files send a review to a lower tier started the agent at that tier, then again at the session's model, and kept the second reading: two readings where the skill asks for one. `first.md` now has the agent called once, at the model an instruction file sets for reviews, else the session's own (requirement 371), and the leak check before a post takes that model's names beside the session's. The gate and the loop take their review models from the configuration, and a user who wrote a tier for reviews chose it for this one too. Reopens: a first reading shown to miss at the lower tier what the session's model finds.

310. **Case 15l expects the first line only once its test file has started.** On Windows, with a PowerShell that fails, the case expected `cannot end a process tree by id` in `.err` at every deadline, and one run lacked it. The group's leader prints the line only when its listing finds a step of the run and the call that ends it fails. A deadline that lands in the setup, between two of the run's children, finds none, makes no call and prints nothing, as the script's header says. With the script held in setup past the deadline with no child the case failed in 5 runs of 5, and held there by a `sleep` child it passed in 5 of 5. The case now expects both lines whenever its test file had started, and takes the deadline line alone only when it had not, at no cost in time. Not taken: a deadline of 15 seconds with a test file that outlasts it, about 11 seconds more for each run, which still missed when the setup was slower than that. Reopens: a miss with the test file started.

311. **The commands of a `prepared` run name the base and both branches.** The `gh pr create` such a run prints had no `--base`, which Step 7.2's has, so a pull request opened by hand after a run on another base would go to the repository's default branch. The list for a host without `gh` gave the title and the files to paste and neither branch, so a reader handed that list alone had no target. Both were read from the files and not seen in a run. The command and the list now name them, in the skill and in the report, and the test that held Step 7.2's command to `--base <base>` holds every `gh pr create` the skill files show (requirements 74, 116 and 215).

312. **The review reads the session user on GitHub only.** The read that tells the user's own pull request stood outside the sentence scoped to GitHub, so a run on an export would call `gh` for a host that is not GitHub, and acceptance item 97 expects no forge call. This was read from the files; item 97 is not yet run. The sentence is scoped, and no such read is made for an export, which names no author (requirement 380). Not taken: comparing `exported_by`, which names who exported the pull request and not who wrote it.

313. **The gate's README says what the review budget holds.** A child review has the run's checks on its allow list (decisions 144 and 266), so it may run the whole suite inside its own budget, 30 minutes by default, and a review past its budget ends the run `unconverged`. No review was seen to run out. The README now says `timeouts.review` holds a reading and one run of each check. Not taken: adding the check budget to the wait for a review, which changes requirement 303 for a failure not seen, and taking the checks off the list, which reverses those two decisions. Reopens: a review that ends past its budget while it runs a check.

314. **Three savings in the suites, with no case dropped.** Decision 308 left them to a change of their own. `readonly.sh` built a fixture for each of its cases, about 4.9 s each; it now builds one and gives each case a copy. The built fixture names its own directory in the app's filter configuration and in two claims files, so the copy has those rewritten to name itself; it holds one repository and no linked worktree, submodule or symbolic link, and nothing the cases run reads the index's stat data, which a copy leaves stale. The tree a case starts from was compared with a fresh build for five cases: the same paths, modes, contents and `git status`. The hook tests that write no shared repository run concurrently, as the file's other groups already did, and the groups that stage in a shared repository stay one after another. The guard's test of a held lock waited the whole 200 tries of 50 ms; `main` now takes the wait as it takes the clock and the `claude` call, a run passes none and waits as before, and the test counts the 200 waits in place of sleeping through them. Not taken: `readonly.sh` in shards, which saves wall time and no work; a setting the guard reads from the environment, a knob in a shipped script for a test alone. Reopens: a case of `readonly.sh` that needs the index's stat data, which would build its own fixture.

315. **A PreToolUse hook sees the text of a commit, a push and a post, and its deny holds under auto mode.** A spike with a hook that logged its input and denied on a marker, in a scratch repository with a local bare remote, with stand-ins for `gh`, `az` and an MCP tool, in two child sessions under `--permission-mode auto`. Every input carries `cwd`, `tool_name` and `tool_input`, and an MCP call also `mcp_server`. A commit typed with a Bash heredoc or a PowerShell here-string has its whole message in `tool_input.command` as typed, newlines included; `git commit -F <file>` and `gh pr comment --body-file <file>` carry only the command, and the hook read each file through `cwd`; `gh issue comment --body` and `az repos pr create --description` carry their text in the command; an MCP tool's arguments arrive as an object. At `git push` the hook listed the commits about to leave with `git log @{upstream}..HEAD` in `cwd` in 73 ms, a range that fails for a branch with no upstream. A deny held for every shape, a Bash call made by an agent included: no commit reached the log and no line the stand-ins' logs, and the session and the agent each saw `PreToolUse:<tool> hook error: <reason>`. `cwd` follows a directory change the session keeps, but not one made inside a command (`cd x && git push`, `git -C x push`), which the hook has to find in the text. No hook sees more than a script's name when the session runs a script that commits, pushes or posts, nor the host's own calls: a stand-in logged a `gh pr view` that no tool call made. The hook's own work took 0 to 2 ms a call; starting node costs more. Reopens: a host release that moves the command out of `tool_input` or lets a call past a deny under auto mode, which acceptance item 110 would show.

316. **One hook in `rules` checks what leaves for a repository's history or a forge against a list the user writes.** The owner's constraint: nothing a session sends into a repository's history or to a forge may show assistant use, and one leak costs more than the check. Decision 226 turned down a term list run by a hook on every `gh` or `az` call, since a list of the suite's coined terms blocks words a team uses in its own sense; this list holds what the user writes, and `/rules` offers as its first lines the host's and its maker's names, the model family names and `reimagine`, and not the seven plugin names, everyday words that whole-word matching would deny in ordinary messages. One hook in `rules`, and not review's leak check copied into four plugins as a step before each commit and post: it costs one paragraph in the `/rules` skill, for the offer of the list, and no words in any other, covers a plain session as well as a plugin run, and keeps each plugin working alone (requirement 9). It searches the whole command and splits it into no words, which is where primer's commit check fails; a word joined to a short option, as in `-mtext`, is found by a second search with the option split off. It reads each file the command names as a message or a body, the forms `$(cat <file>)`, a pipe from `cat` or `Get-Content` and a `<` redirect included, since a message written to a file and read back is how a session often commits. A path it resolved is left out of the search: it stays on the machine, and a scratch folder named for the host would otherwise deny every commit made from a message file in it. Left out everywhere, it hid the same path where a message named it, so a path is left out only where no quote and no line break comes before it in the command. A count of the quotes before it was tried in place of that and misjudged an apostrophe inside double quotes and a backslash before a closing quote, in both directions: it hid a path a message held and skipped a file a command read. So a real path after a quoted argument is searched, which costs a needless stop and no leak; a user whose scratch folder holds a listed name puts the file option first or allows the folder's name. At a push it reads the messages of the commits that `HEAD` or any local branch reaches and no remote-tracking ref holds, in `cwd` and in each directory the command names, since `@{upstream}` fails for a new branch and `cwd` misses a directory changed inside the command (decision 315). It reads no refspec: matching branch names in the text missed `refs/heads/<branch>`, and a commit on a branch the push leaves behind is denied too, which costs a needless stop and no leak. A commit only a tag reaches is read when the command sends tags. `git stash push` is not read as a push; a push that only deletes is, since a `--delete` elsewhere in the command would otherwise switch the read off. A list saved as UTF-16, which a redirect in Windows PowerShell writes, is read as such; read as UTF-8 it matched nothing and the hook was off without a word. Of an MCP call it collects the string values, since in JSON text the `\n` before a word breaks whole-word matching. The added lines of a pushed diff are read only with `check: diff`, since that reads every pushed diff. The hook is off until the list exists; a file it cannot read is a warning, and its own failure lets the call go ahead, so a broken install blocks nothing. A commit whose attribution trailer names a listed string is denied: the host's setting still decides whether a trailer is typed, and the hook removes nothing (requirement 11, decision 136), so a user who lists the host's name and wants the trailer allows it or turns the setting off. Not covered: a commit, push or post made by a script the session runs, though a commit made that way is read at a typed push; a message read from a file by a command other than `cat`, `Get-Content`, `gc`, `type` or a redirect; the host's own calls; a commit and a push typed in one call, where the push is read before the commit exists, so only the command's text and the files it names are checked; a tag's message at a push, though one typed with `-m` is read at the call; a commit pushed by its id that no branch reaches; a commit another remote already holds. A read through `gh`, `az`, `glab` or an MCP tool whose text holds a listed string is denied too, since the hook does not tell a read from a post. Review keeps `posts.mjs leak` for its drafts (requirement 377), since it looks for what the list cannot hold: the paths, user name and host name of the review's own run, in each spelling of a path. Reopens: a leak the hook missed, a listed string that blocks ordinary work often enough to drop it, or a host release that gives the hook less than decision 315 shows.

317. **The staging check reads a PowerShell here-string and a pathspec git does not know, denies only an add or a commit when the map cannot be read, and counts an agent file's name in any letter case on purpose.** The parser read a single quote up to the next one and had no rule for a PowerShell here-string, so a message with one apostrophe between `@'` and `'@` left a quote open; the command got no parse, counted as one that may stage out of sight, and was checked in every `local` repository of the map: a commit in one repository was denied for an agent file in another, and a `Set-Content` of a note that held the word `commit` was denied though it ran no git. Through the PowerShell tool a here-string, from `@'` or `@"` that ends its line to the same quote and `@` at the start of a line, as PowerShell reads one, is now one word, with `$` and `$(` in `@"` marked as in double quotes; under the Bash tool the quote rules are as before. Reading it as one word took away a stop that had held by accident: an apostrophe in a here-string had left the command with no parse, and with the here-string read, commands passed that a run showed to stage or commit an agent file. Probes of the change found them: a here-string piped to a shell (`@'`, the commands, `'@ | sh`), which is a here-document by another name, and one handed to `Write-Output` or kept in a variable and then piped; `$(& "git" add -A)` inside `@"`, where the quotes around `git` hid it from the pattern for a substitution; text joined to the closer (`'@CLAUDE.md`), which PowerShell splits off as an argument of its own; and `$m=@'` after a command's name, which PowerShell reads as a here-string only where it starts a statement. Following PowerShell's grammar case by case did not converge, so the rule is the narrow one: a here-string is one word only where `@` starts a word and whitespace, a separator or the end follows its closer, and any other placement leaves the command with no parse, the result it had before. Unless a `git` call takes it as an argument, a here-string whose text names a git add, stage or commit on one line counts as text that may run one, as the text after a here-document does, and a line continued with a backslash, a backtick or a caret counts as one line there, in a here-document and in a command handed to another shell. The pattern for a substitution passes over a quote before `git`. The change was then held to one test: every here-string and shell command the probes had tried, 150 of them, was run through the script as released and as changed, and each that went from a deny to a pass had to be a fault this entry names; the three that did are a commit whose own message is a here-string. So `$m=@'` with no space and a closer followed by `.Trim()` get no parse and are checked in every `local` repository, where `$m = @'` is read; a commit message that names `git add` is read as a message; a note or a post body in a here-string that names one is stopped where an agent file is in reach. The hook's own word splitter in `drift.sh`, which decides whether the drift check runs, had the same fault: after a here-string with one apostrophe it missed a commit in the same call, and it took a line `git commit` of a here-string's text for a commit and denied a `Set-Content` on a hub's finding. It reads a here-string as one word where it starts a word, and also after the `=` of a variable that starts a command, since a missed drift check costs less there than in the staging check; it reads inside `@"` only for `$(`. A command handed to `pwsh`, `powershell` or `cmd` was not read at all, so `pwsh -c "git add -A"` passed; one that names an add, stage or commit, or holds an expansion, now counts as running one out of sight, which also stops `pwsh -c "git log --grep commit"` where an agent file is in reach. A backtick before `$` in `@"` still marks the word, a needless stop. Still not read, and passing as before: a command a shell reads from its standard input (`'git add -A' | sh`), an encoded command, `git` spelled with an escape inside the word, a here-string inside a block comment, and the forms requirement 391 already lists. With two apostrophes the quotes paired, the message split into words, and the words left over became pathspecs: the dry run exited 1 for paths git did not know, which the script read as nothing to commit, so a commit with `AGENTS.md` staged passed. Git also exits 1 when there is nothing to commit, so after a status of 1 with path arguments the script asks `git ls-files --error-unmatch`, over the index with `HEAD` and over the index alone, whether git knows every path, as the commit's own matching does; where it does not, the commit is unresolved and judged as staging every file git does not ignore. The answer is read from exit statuses alone, since git's messages change with its language. The hook handed a call over when its whole input held `commit`, a description included, and the script judged it; the hook's match stays a cheap first filter, and the script now goes on only when the command itself holds the words. The script lowercases a path before comparing it with the agent file names, while git matches the `info/exclude` lines by case where `core.ignorecase` is false, so there an untracked `claude.md` counts as an agent file and a `git add -A && git commit` is denied. Comparing as git does, in any case only where `core.ignorecase` is true, was built and taken out: it turned that deny into a pass, and on a file system that ignores case with `core.ignorecase` false the host loads `claude.md`, which could then enter a `local` history; on any system the name in a history shows what the profile hides. The compare stays as it was, requirement 391 now says it is meant, and a test holds it; it costs a needless stop for a file of that name that is no agent file, and no agent file in a history. A map that could not be read denied every call that reached the script, a `git log --grep commit` included. It now denies only a command in which the script sees an add, stage or commit, or whose text may run one out of sight, with a reason that names the map file and how to restore it, and passes every other command: without the map the check cannot tell a `local` repository from another, and an agent file staged in a repository that must not hold one costs more than a needless stop. A map that is missing is no map, and the check does nothing, as before. A `config.json` that cannot be read is still an error of the script's own, and it too is now met only by a command that holds an add, stage or commit, since the script reads the file after the command. Reopens: a command that went from a deny to a pass with this change and stages an agent file, a here-string form sessions write that gets no parse often enough to matter, a path that `git ls-files --error-unmatch` accepts and a commit refuses, an unreadable map that stops ordinary work often enough to let calls through, or a file named `claude.md` in another case that a team keeps and the check stops often enough to matter.

318. **Only `/primer` starts primer's skill, and a reply that stops still opens with the mode line.** The description named four phrases so that prose could invoke the skill (requirement 245, decision 84), two of them everyday words, "add this to the project instructions" and "we keep doing this". A session in which one was said in passing could start a run, whose first question records a profile for the repository in the map, and the description cost startup context in every session. The skill now sets `disable-model-invocation: true`, as `rules`, `refactor`, `fold` and `guard` do. The proposal for one item, which a phrase used to start, is `/primer` followed by the item, and the session source of requirement 253 is handed over that way. Lint holds the key in place of the phrases. The `mode-line` eval case types the command; a command the user types loads the skill without the Skill tool, so the case's `tool_used` grader read 0 calls in every run and is removed, and the mode line, which no run without the plugin printed, is what shows the skill ran. That case had failed 1 run of 30 taken alone and 1 of the 2 in each of two release runs; in the one failing run whose trace was kept, the session chose `init`, found an empty work tree and no shell, and answered with a stop report that did not open with the mode line. The skill now says a reply that reports a stop in place of a pass still opens with the mode line and the profile line. On the new text the case, run alone 4 times, printed the line in all 4, above a stop report in the runs whose traces were kept, and in none of the 4 runs without the plugin. Reopens: starting primer by prose missed often enough to bring a phrase back, or a run of the case that prints no mode line.

319. **The fallback commit subject names no plugin in any repository.** Where a repository's instruction files name no commit convention, primer committed as `docs(primer): <subject>`, a scope that names the plugin in the history, and as `docs: <subject>` only where the map said the repository is not the user's own (requirement 396, decision 291). A repository the user owns has other readers of its history too, and the skill held two rules where one serves: the fallback is `docs: <subject>` everywhere, and the `own false` case leaves the skill and requirement 247. The map keeps `own`, which the open marker question of decision 291 turns on, and no commit reads it. Reopens: a team that wants the scope to find primer's commits by. Decision 330 drops `own` from the map.

320. **In `local`, what the team tracks is read, and an addition to it goes in the store.** `local.md` and requirement 385 said instruction files the upstream tracks are read and never rewritten, and nothing of a nested `CLAUDE.md` or `AGENTS.md`, a rule, a skill or a `.claude/settings.json` that it tracks, nor of where the user's own addition to one goes; a run could propose a line a tracked nested file already states, or a path-scoped rule in the work tree, which the profile bars. The text now names those files, sends an addition to the layer or to a document under the store with the directory or the files it covers named in its line or pointer, since nothing in the store loads by path, and has a setting proposed for `.claude/settings.local.json`, a file the staging check keeps out of a commit (requirement 391). No script changes. Acceptance item 111 runs it. Reopens: an addition that is of use only when it loads by path, which would have the loader read what the session touches.

321. **Guard asks before a push or a post to a flagged repository, through a hook.** Guard's publishing entry lets a push and a new pull request in a listed repository run with no question, and its entries are not per repository, so a repository whose every push, pull request and comment must be seen first had no setting. Three ways to make auto mode ask were tried in child sessions under `--permission-mode auto`, in a scratch repository with two local remotes, with a stand-in MCP tool named as the permission prompt tool, so that a raised prompt, a classifier pass, a classifier block and a hook's deny each left a different record, after a control in which an ask rule reached that tool. A `PreToolUse` hook that answers `ask` raised a prompt for a Bash push, a PowerShell push and an MCP call aimed at the flagged remote and none for the same calls to the other remote; it did so with a `permissions.allow` rule for the push and guard's own environment and allow entries in place, an approved call then ran, and a subagent's push prompted too. With no one to answer the call was denied: under `--permission-prompts none` the session read the host's own denial, in a plain `-p` session the hook's reason, and in neither did a `PermissionDenied` hook run, so guard's denial record does not hold it. A `soft_deny` line naming the remote blocked the push with no prompt, and beside guard's allow entry the push went through. A `permissions.ask` rule prompted, but a rule sees the command as typed and a push does not name its remote. Each result is one run. So the flag is `ask` on a list entry, written by `--add <path> --ask`, and the check is a hook, `ask.mjs`, that reads the list beside the settings file and writes nothing to `autoMode` (requirement 406). It judges the text with patterns and no shell grammar, and fails toward asking: every directory a text changes to is one the call may run in, a remote counts by the path after its host, and what it cannot resolve asks. In two rounds 1,119 command and tool inputs were run through the script; the first found 65 writes that reached a flagged repository unasked and 23 reads that asked, the second 21 more writes, and each fix began as a failing test with the input that showed it. The inputs a fix released were read one by one: raw reads, `curl -G`, a literal GraphQL query, the read tools of an MCP git server, and raw and MCP calls while the list cannot be read. What the hook does not see is listed in the README: a script or alias that pushes, a program started by another, a raw call that names the repository by an id or builds its address from variables, an MCP tool told neither name nor path. Not taken: `soft_deny` and a `permissions.ask` rule, for the results above, and because requirement 336 keeps guard's writes to two `autoMode` lists; a deny in place of an ask, which would stop the user's own push; a hook that fails closed, since a missing `node` would then stop every shell call of a user who flagged nothing. This amends requirements 334 and 344, which held guard to two hooks, and stands beside decision 226, whose refusal was of a hook that reads what a post says. Only that a prompt was raised was seen, not what an interactive session shows (acceptance item 112). Reopens: a host release in which a hook's `ask` no longer prompts under auto mode or an allow rule outranks it; a push or a post from a command form a session uses that leaves unasked.

322. **Guard does not support two configuration folders that share a settings file, and says so.** The list file sits beside `settings.json` and does not travel with a settings file that is synced or linked into a second configuration folder, so that folder held guard's entries with no list: the startup hook printed nothing there (requirement 341), and a flag on a list entry would be lost without a word. Carrying the list and the state inside the settings file would make both travel, a larger change to what requirement 336 lets guard write, for a layout with one known user. So the layout stays unsupported and is announced: at startup guard names a list that is missing beside marked entries, and one that cannot be read or is not a guard list, neither of which printed anything before; in both states the ask hook asks before every push and every `gh` and `az repos` write, since it cannot tell a flagged repository from another, and lets raw calls and MCP calls pass, since a question before each of those left a session that drives a browser or another MCP server unusable; and the README says a copy of the list file beside the second `settings.json` ends the questions. Reopens: a second user of the layout, or a flagged repository reached unasked from a folder in that state.

323. **The review reads the author's tests and the description, and its stand-in reading is asked for more than correctness bugs.** Of 121 findings that reviewers raised on a set of pull requests, 17 were weak tests, each visible in the diff alone: an assertion that is always true, a case that accepts either outcome, a new branch with no test. 19 were gaps between the pull request's description and its change, a stale claim among them, and 12 were breaks of a team's conventions. None of the three checks of decision 288 reaches any of them: the brief of the stand-in reading asked for correctness bugs only, the proof that a test fails with the change undone covered only a test the reviewer hands over (requirement 375), and no step read the description, which on GitHub was never fetched. So the brief also asks for weak tests and for breaks of the conventions it already carried, and two checks join the three before any draft: each test the change adds or alters is read for whether it would fail with the change it guards undone, and each claim of the title and body is held against the change, at a follow-up against the new head and once more before a post. A claim the change contradicts, or one true of an earlier head only, is a finding; one the change cannot show, such as a test run elsewhere, is a note, since the other reading would make a finding of nearly every description. Both run in the session and not only in the brief, since a gate record, an audit report and the host's own review take no brief. The title and body are the author's text, so they are data as the head is, and they are kept in the record. A finding on the description has no line of the diff to anchor it and goes in the author's summary. What evidence a description must carry differs by team, and the suite holds no team's list, so such evidence is asked for only where the team's conventions require it, the repository's own files at the base commit or a file the request names. Requirement 367 still holds: the plugin finds little by itself and takes its reading from elsewhere, and these are checks on that reading and on the change, as the three are. Not taken: a house rule on test strength, which would move the digests of every install for a rule with one source; the same clause in the gate's stand-in brief, since the gate's review is the host's, which takes no request, and its stand-in asks what the host's does; and a list of other open pull requests that touch the same files, which two findings support (decision 252). Reopens: weak tests or stale claims that still reach an author's second round; or description findings that are noise where no conventions file is named.

324. **The loop's implementer brief asks for a test that fails with its change undone.** Step 6.4 fails a behavior change that has no test, and nothing asked what the test proves, so a test that asserts only a type, or accepts either outcome, met the rule. Such tests were a group of findings a reader could see in the diff alone (decision 323). The brief now says that a test the slice adds must fail with the change it guards undone, and the reply says for each such test whether a run showed that, since a slice that runs in parallel runs no checks and can only say so. Not taken: a house rule, for the reason decision 323 gives; a step of the loop that undoes each change and runs the test, which would run every slice's checks twice. Reopens: weak tests that still leave a run whose replies claimed the failure was shown.

325. **The gate asks once before its first push to a host it cannot tell is GitHub.** The deploy judgment reads the workflow files under `.github/workflows` and nothing else (decision 216), so on any other host no push counted as a deploy and none asked, unless an instruction file asked before every push. A pipeline on such a host can deploy on a push all the same. Where the target's remote matched no repository that `gh repo view` names, the run now asks once before each target's first push and says that the host's pipelines are in no file it reads; a yes covers that target's pushes, since nothing between two pushes of one target can change what the gate knows there, and the deploy judgment still runs at every push where workflow files exist; and any other answer is a denial of the push. With nobody to answer the run stops at the question, its fix committed and not pushed and the commit named in the record, as a run stopped before any push leaves it. `ask-push` set to `false` in the `gate` key drops the question, for a repository whose owner knows its pushes deploy nothing; a bad value asks. Not taken: reading another host's pipeline files, a forge adapter the suite does not have (requirement 215); saying in the README that the judgment is for GitHub and leaving the push unasked. Reopens: a host whose pipeline files the gate can read as it reads workflow files, or runs that set the key in every repository.

326. **An untracked `.reimagine.json` is excluded, not a reason to stop.** Both preflights end `blocked` on an untracked file that is not ignored, and both checked that before they wrote the exclude file, so in a repository that must not show the file a run could not start until its owner excluded it by hand. Before its first read of the whole tree, which in the loop comes ahead of the clean-tree step, each preflight now asks git whether `.reimagine.json` is untracked, with `git status --porcelain -- .reimagine.json`, and adds it to the exclude file where it is. The test is on the untracked state and not on `git check-ignore`, which also fails for a tracked file and would add the line in every repository that commits its configuration. A tracked file is untouched and read as it is, and a team that wants to commit an excluded one adds it with `git add -f`, which both READMEs say. Reopens: a run that reads no configuration because the file was excluded and then left behind, as in a new worktree; or an excluded file lost to a checkout of a branch that tracks one, which git overwrites without a word and which both READMEs warn of.

327. **Concise Plain 4.17: a reply that leaves something open ends with numbered closing lines.** Of about 120 answers to replies that left decisions open, 37 pasted the reply's own lines back to say which decision they answered, and 86 accepted every pick. 23 turns asked what came next after a work report; in 14 of them the next step was in the reply but not at its end, and 10 of those replies ended on a list of files. Some of the 18 turns that asked for a missing pick followed a list of open items. The style said a decision is spelled out in full and gave it no place, no number and no way to accept every pick in a word. A Closing section now ends a reply that reports work or leaves something open: the next step in one line, what waits on the user's answer numbered with one line on what a bare "yes" accepts, and any other open item with a pick or with whose it is to decide; after everything else in the reply, with no heading, and with nothing in it but the next step said earlier, so the lead still names what the reader must act on. An approval the reply asks for is numbered with the decisions, since a bare "yes" to a reply that numbered its decisions and listed a deletion beside them did not say whether the deletion was approved. Numbering reverses part of decision 158, which cut "one numbered message" from the house rules' decision item because no incident stood behind it; the pasted answers are the incident, and the number lands in the style, not in that item. Trials of one small fix with two choices left open, run headless: under 4.16, two runs of two wrote no number, no next-step line and no line on a bare "yes". A first wording that said the decisions are "written only here" had both of its runs write their picks in the body and again, shorter, in the lines, and so did one run of two after a sentence said that what the lines hold is said nowhere earlier; moving that rule to the opening paragraph had three runs of three repeat them. The next wording, that nothing in the lines appears earlier and that a pick the user asked for is given there once, had five runs of six write each decision once; the last three of those came after "nothing waits on me", which runs copied in their own voice, became "even when that is no one", and one of the three wrote no next-step line. A read of that wording found that "a reply" also names a draft to someone else, that "a pick I asked for" let a decision go without one, that an open item could be called the user's with no pick, and that the order of the lines and the next step in the lead were left unsaid; with those five settled, three runs of three wrote the next-step line, the numbers and the line on a bare "yes", and two of the three wrote each decision once. On that text, which is the one kept, a plain question got no lines, a fix that left nothing open got the next-step line alone, a draft to someone else held none of the lines, and a run whose check was refused numbered the approval by what it covers and wrote its picks twice. Not taken: the lines on every reply, since a plain answer needs none and some requests were about replies already too long; a heading over them; a second line with one overall recommendation, which the line on a bare "yes" already is; an eval case, since the documented fields of a case hold none that selects an output style, so the check is acceptance item 115; `force-for-plugin: true` in the style's frontmatter, which the host documents as applying a style wherever its plugin is enabled, and which would change every install to serve a case; the style's text in a case's `append_system_prompt`, which is not the style loading and would probably reach the arm without the plugin too. Two added sentences replace none (requirement 366). The one on when and where the lines appear is paid for by the Drafts sentence on a source the draft names, since the `draft` skill's check strikes a sentence that restates one, and the one on a pick given once by "Background after." in the lead bullet, which "Lead with" already says. Reopens: replies that keep writing a decision in the body and again in the lines, as three of the last ten trial runs with decisions did; a work report whose lead leaves out what is blocked, which would restore the clause the opening paragraph lost; a draft that restates its source after the skill's check, which would restore that sentence; an eval field that selects an output style, which would move item 115 to a case.

328. **The logging item names its repository, no host, and a log that loaded context names.** Entries from every session shared one daily file and none said which repository it came from. A session whose loaded context named a feedback log of its own held two rules for one finding. The item named one host's variable and folder, so it could not be copied into another tool's instructions. And primer has a proposal say how a change is proposed to a plugin, which nothing defined. The item now has an entry name the repository the session worked in, puts the file in `feedback/` in "your host's user config folder", and sends the entry to a log that loaded context names for these entries where it names one. The three edits ship as one change, since any change to `core.md` moves its digests and makes every installed block read `stale` once. A session outside a repository has none to name, and the item says nothing for it. This reverses part of decision 285: the directory under the config folder is no longer the only place an entry can go. An entry sent to another log is not read by primer, which reads the config folder alone (requirement 397), and the sentence of primer's README on where the logs are written is owed a change with primer's next release. Requirement 276's "no second host" stands: the suite supports one host, and the wording only stops blocking a hand port; the plugin's README names the folder for Claude Code. The root README defines the channel: an issue on the repository that carries what an entry's `Recommended` section recommends for the house rules, with any repository name or path taken out first, since the item bars a person, an id and a path only from a recommendation for the house rules; nothing is sent for the user, as requirement 276 rules out telemetry. Trials, run headless without the installed rules and with the new text supplied. On a first wording, "your host's config folder" and "the feedback log that loaded context names", two runs of two wrote to the folder the config variable names, which the item no longer spells out, and named the repository, and one run whose context named a log in the repository wrote there and not to the config folder. A read of that wording found that the folder could be read as a repository's own and the log as the notes of auto memory, so the folder became the user config folder and the log one that loaded context names for these entries. On that text one run whose context named a log wrote there. Two runs of two without one, told an entry was owed and asked where it goes with no write possible, gave the user config folder, which each took from the path of its memory directory, and named the repository in the entry. No run has written there on that text, and acceptance item 93 covers it. Not taken: "repository" in the list of what a recommendation never names, which primer's fold-back and requirement 398 repeat and would have to follow; a file or a tag for each repository, which primer's count of logs by file would have to follow. Reopens: a log written where primer does not look; a recommendation posted with a repository's name in it.

329. **Primer's staging check is a script of its own.** `primer.mjs` stood at its 700 lines, so a change to any verb had no room, and requirement 387 has a verb that does not fit go in a second script, never in a longer file. The staging check, about a third of the file, is now `scripts/staged.mjs`, which the drift hook starts in place of `primer.mjs staged`. It imports the map's reader and the git helpers from `primer.mjs`, and nothing imports it, so the map keeps one reader and the two scripts form no cycle. The `staged` verb left `primer.mjs`, since the hook alone called it. Its three tests moved to `tests/primer/staged.test.mjs`, which names no other script, as the ledger's and the transcript reader's tests do, so the rule of requirement 6 can be shown for one script at a time. The move changed no behavior: the hook's suite passed before and after with no assertion changed, and the script's changed one, the list of verbs in the refusal of an unknown command. The third of the moved tests writes its map entry where it ran `map --add`. Not taken: a higher budget, which requirement 387 rules out; a third file for what the two scripts share, which nothing else needs. Reopens: `staged.mjs` near its own budget.

330. **Primer no longer asks whether a `shared` repository is the user's own.** The first run in a `shared` repository asked it and the map held the answer as `own`. Since decision 319 nothing read it, the fallback commit subject naming no plugin anywhere, and the field was kept for the marker question of decision 291, which is still not picked. So the user answered a question whose answer changed nothing. The question, the `--own` option and the field are gone, and requirement 396 is retired.
