A map written before holds `own` in each entry: the loader reads past it and the next write leaves it out, so no map is edited by hand. This reverses decision 319's sentence that the map keeps `own`. It does not pick the marker question of decision 291, which stays open: if its pick turns on whose repository it is, the question returns then, asked where its answer is used. Reopens: the marker question picked in a way that needs the answer.

331. **A copy can have more than one source.** `copies --add` refused a second source for a declared copy, so a copy ported from two files recorded one and a change to the other went unreported. A copy now has one record for each source: `--add <copy> <source>` adds a record where the copy is declared with another source and refuses only the same pair, and `--ported <copy>` takes the snapshot of every source of the copy and the copy's digest again. The record's shape is unchanged, so a `copies.json` written before needs no change. `--ported` cannot record one source alone, so after a port that took up one source's change and not another's, both read `current`. Not taken: a `--source` option on `--ported`, with one port as the evidence. Reopens: a port from one source of several.

332. **The fold-back stamp sits beside the feedback logs.** The logs are under one config folder and the stamp of their review was `reviewed/feedback` in the plugin data directory. Two config folders that share one plugins folder share that directory, so a review under one marked the logs of the other reviewed, and the notice there never counted them. The skill now writes `<config>/feedback/.reviewed`, and the hook and the loader take it before the old file, which they still read where the new one is absent, so a review made before does not come up again. Neither reader counts a name that starts with a dot, so the stamp is no log. A log an earlier review under another config folder hid stays hidden until the old file is removed. Not taken: reading the old file never, which would have every log reviewed again in every config folder; and moving `reviewed/<key>`, the stamp of the auto memory notes, which can be shared the same way and has no report against it. Reopens: a report of notes hidden by a review made under another config folder.

333. **The loop's plan states each input's effect, and the plan review checks it.** Field feedback of 2026-11-16, a review of sessions whose findings reviewers had been told to refute. In it a section of rules loaded into a project's sessions gained a sentence asking each change to state its effect on stored records and on scheduled work. Of about 31 such effects before the sentence was added, about 12 were caught by a reviewer or a review pass and the rest in the change itself or by the user, some of those prompted, so most were not caught late; the review comments that changed the most were still of this kind: a script that widened what a scheduled job selects, a validation change that altered behavior for records already created, a stricter check that stored values would fail. After the sentence was added, the three or four gaps on record were caught at plan review or before the commit. That count has no denominator, the review rates the finding as moderate, and what it supports is a review that checks the statement, not the instruction alone. The loop's plan held scope, acceptance criteria and buildable status for an input and nothing on its effect; its reviewer was asked about the code that reads a contract and the companions of a data change; and the first text on what changed for a ticket's readers was written at publication from a record that held no such fact. The plan now states the effect of each input, and the plan review is asked for what a statement leaves out and for what it claims that the plan would not produce (requirement 408). The step names the kind, whatever already exists and depends on the changed behavior, with stored data and unattended work as examples, since in the same review a rule that named one instance was followed at most for that instance. The plain form, that no one would notice, is allowed and names the input the work serves, so that no benefit is invented for technical work. The other carrier is the one the review has, a sentence among a project's own rules, such as one in its instruction file, which the loop's planner and reviewer also read and which can name that project's own kinds of state. The loop's step is taken as well because the section that held the sentence sat, for its first eight days, past the point where the context was cut off, so no session saw it, and a planner can miss a project's sentence the same way, while a step file is read when its step runs; and because a repository with no such sentence gets nothing. Not taken: an acceptance criterion the run writes for an effect the change must not have, which could turn a closing line into `Refs` and show a ticket's readers a criterion no one asked for; a read of the statement against the diff and rules for where published text repeats it, for which no case is recorded, the body and the comments being written from the record's facts with each claim checked before it is repeated; a sentence on value to a client in a pull request body or a ticket comment, since in the review such openings were cut or reworded and sales wording was turned down; the ask in the stand-in's diff review, whose findings have no bucket for a statement that is wrong where the code is right; the statement as a claim of the loop's handoff, which would change the keys the audit's script accepts, with no case behind it; and a gate, a rank or an ordered list by the statement (decision 334). Not settled: whether the loop's step does more than a sentence among the project's rules. The first run of acceptance item 116 compares them by the bar that item gives. Reopens: a pull request body or a ticket comment that repeats a statement the diff made untrue, which adds the read against the diff; or the first run of item 116 meeting its bar with the project's sentence alone, which takes out requirement 408 and the clauses of requirements 66 and 99, the planner and the reviewer of that run having both read the project's sentence.

334. **No rule ranks work, and what else was not taken in this update.** The update set out to have the suite put work that the people who use a product would notice ahead of other work, as well as to have each bundle of work say what it does for them. In the field review behind decision 333 every statement of priority was typed by the user, from sources no session could read, and one changed within a day; both wrong rankings on record turned on a fact the session had not read; and the cases of technical work chosen while other work waited were three, none clean. Whether supplying a purpose changes what a session proposes next is untested there. A rule to rank would have nothing to rank with, so none is written, the loop ranks nothing by an input's effect and orders no list by it, the report's open items among them (requirement 408), and until a trial or a field record shows that a supplied purpose changes what is proposed, nothing carries one. The loop's request could not carry one as it is: its headings, its phrases and its configuration keys are fixed lists that tests hold (requirements 46, 49, 81 and 282), and the remaining text of a request is the description the run builds (requirement 281). Also not taken. From that review: asking the user for a purpose where none is recorded, one more question in every run, with no case on record that went wrong for want of it; a ticket's labels and milestone read into the plan as who is waiting on it, on one case, with nothing in the loop that would rank by them; finishing a fix in the current work over filing it, on three cases, against the limit of requirement 351 that keeps an unreviewed change out of a run (decision 223); sorting work by whether it changes behavior today, on one case; a rule to save a found fact, on one case; and a sweep of open items in every text that carries one, where the review names the kinds of text its traced asks came from and not which of this suite's texts, so no line can be changed on it, and which reopens on a trace that names the text. From a thread report of working habits, none with a result behind it: a statement of value as a gate on what enters a backlog, which the filing test of requirement 351 already is for what a run files; work bundled by the behavior a client sees and releases paced to clients, which are the user's to decide for the tickets a run is given; every item marked with its origin, and with it a ticket filed for an open item saying that it was found during other work, where the loop's handoff already keeps the tickets a run was given apart from the tickets it raised, the loop also files owed work that "found" would mislabel, and the sentence is close to saying how the item was reached (requirements 348 and 352), and which reopens on a filed ticket that a reader took for requested work; and an open ask for improvements replaced by a question about what a client would notice. Each of the rest reopens on a second case of its failure, or on a first where none is recorded, and the rule to rank on a trial or a field record in which a supplied purpose changed what a session proposed.

335. **House-rule edit twenty-three: routine steps on the owner's own repositories need no ask, only direction is asked for, a side finding is a note, and a change runs the checks it can break.** Measured over eight days of sessions: about two thirds of the items put to the owner were permissions the Ask first section required for a commit, a push, a pull request, a merge, a tag, an issue or a deletion, about one in twenty was a decision on direction, and about one in eight was a technical choice the session could have made; about half of session time had a test run or a wait on CI in flight; and of 72 issues opened, about 45 came from the suite's own gate runs, acceptance runs and CI failures and about 7 from work a user did. Four causes, one edit each. The stop on every commit and push made each routine step a question with a predictable yes, so the item now stops a commit or a push on a repository that is not the owner's, a push to the default branch of one that is and any push that deploys, and a draft pull request on the owner's repository is no longer among what reaches other people; merging a pull request, a tag and marking a pull request ready are named there, where "a release" and "a PR" had left them to be inferred. A repository is the owner's when they own it or their own instruction file names it, so that a repository others work in is not taken for one by the right to push to it. This is the first edit to take a stop out, which requirement 21 ruled out, and the owner decided it. The item on asking named "my preference", which a session read as leave to ask about any choice; it now names a decision on direction, what to build or drop and for whom, or money. A side finding got its own issue, and a finding from the suite's own runs was then fixed and released in the same session; it is now a line of the report or of a backlog note unless a user of the product would meet it in normal work, and the report comes when the outcome the request names is delivered. And the Done item asked for the checks before and after a change and the repository's checks before every report, so a change to seven lines of prose that no test reads ran the whole suite four times; a change now runs once the checks that read what it touched, and the rest is left to CI where there is one. In this repository the line under Commands follows, and a release makes no acceptance item due by itself: the items owed are run in one batch before a version that someone other than the owner relies on. Not changed: how the loop and the gate file tickets for open items and run fix rounds, which is looked at once sessions have run under these rules. Reopens: a commit or a push the owner would have refused; a decision on direction that was not put to the owner because the session judged it a technical choice; a defect a user met that a skipped check would have caught; or issues from the suite's own runs still piling up, which takes the question to the loop and the gate.

336. **Review's leak check looks for the assistant's name and its maker's without being given them.** Decision 316 left those names to the outbound list of `rules`, whose hook reads a post, and kept `leak` for the strings of the review's own run. An export is pasted by hand, so no hook reads it and `leak` is the only check on that path. Run as the posting step has it, with the machine's names, the model's and the plugin's, it printed `clean` for a draft that held a co-author trailer, for one with a "Generated with" line and for one that named the maker, and it found a model's family name only where the session passed it as one word. The script now holds the two names and reports a hit as the kind `assistant`, found where no letter touches either end of the name, so also where it is joined to `_` or a digit, as in `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `claude_code` or `Claude3`, while the other kinds keep their edges of no letter, digit or `_`, and the step passes the family name as one word. A reviewed repository that tracks a file named for the assistant gives a hit in a draft that cites it, which the posting step settles with the user by name; the export step waited for a clean check and could not finish on such a draft, so it now takes the same answer. The check printed only the kinds, so a yes for one string of a kind, a citation of a file the repository tracks, let through another hit of that kind, a co-author trailer. It now prints one line per hit: its kind, its line in the draft and the string as found, every occurrence being a hit; and the posting step and the export go ahead for a string that is the repository's own only once the user, shown every hit with its line, has said of each by name that it is the repository's own, a yes for one hit covering no other. Not taken: reading the outbound list from the script, which would tie review to another plugin's file (requirement 9); a list of attribution phrases, since each one seen holds one of the two names; and finding the two names inside a longer word, which would flag words such as a given name that only starts the same way. The README now says which pushes send the refs a run leaves in the reviewed repository: with two throwaway repositories, a push with `--mirror` sent `refs/reimagine/review/` and a plain push, `--all` and `--tags` did not. Not taken: another name for those refs, with no push on record that sent one. Reopens: a draft that showed assistant use and passed the check, or a run's ref seen on a remote.

337. **A line of the outbound list names a command to read as a forge post.** Decision 316 left a post made by a script the session runs out of the check. Where a forge's posts go through such a script, or through a plain HTTP call, the check then read commits, pushes and few posts: given `node <script> --body-file <file>` with a listed string in the file, it printed nothing and never opened the file, where the same file after `gh issue comment` was denied. A line `post: <phrase>` now has any command that holds the phrase read as a post, its text and the files it names after the options the check already knows. The phrase is found anywhere in the command, in lower case and with a backslash read as a slash, since a script's path is typed in either direction; a search whose text holds the phrase is read too, and denied where it holds a listed string, which costs a needless stop and no leak, as with the three forge programs. Still not read: a text the script builds or reads by itself, and a file after an option the check does not know. Not taken: reading every command that starts `node` or holds a URL, which would search most of a session's commands; the file after any option of such a command, with no script on record whose option the five do not cover; a narrower test for the three forge programs, the word only where a command name can stand, which on a copy of the script no longer read a call behind `env`, `xargs` or a shell given a command, while a quoted pattern with a pipe before the word was still read; and a notice at session start where a config folder has no list and another has one, since the hook knows only the folder it runs under, so the README says the list belongs to one config folder. Reopens: a post through a named command whose text the check did not read, or a script whose body option the check does not know.

338. **A map entry matches a clone under any form of its remote, and two skill steps say what their runs left out.** A repository matched an entry only where a remote was written the same way on both sides (decision 292). With a `local` entry under `dev.azure.com`, the staging check printed nothing for `git add CLAUDE.md` in a clone whose remote named `<org>.visualstudio.com`, with or without `DefaultCollection`, or `vs-ssh.visualstudio.com`, or the same path in another letter case; `status` gave no entry and no layer loaded. The two sides are now compared without letter case and with the older host forms read as `dev.azure.com`. A remote of the older host with no project segment, `<org>.visualstudio.com/_git/<repo>` with or without `DefaultCollection/`, reads as `dev.azure.com/<org>/_git/<repo>`. A clone first takes the entry that holds one of its remotes as written, or the entry at its path where the clone or the entry has no remote, and only a clone with no such entry takes the first entry one of whose remotes has the same form; comparing by form alone gave both clones the first entry of a map that held one entry for each form, which can hand a `local` clone the `shared` profile. The map stores what it stored, so neither an entry nor a clone is rewritten, and a remote is still written by guard's rule (requirement 340). Letter case is dropped for every host: where a host tells two paths apart by case alone, a clone of one matches the other's entry, which checks more and loads a layer meant for the sibling, and no such pair is on record. Two open reports ride with the change. Step 6 of the fold-back review named the ledger's forms and not which outcome takes which, and in two answered runs a held item stayed `open` and the rejected ones got no line; the step now names the three outcomes. The offer of the `info/exclude` lines was made "once per repository" with nowhere to keep the answer, so it came again after a no and while the file held the lines; the run now reads the file and offers only what is missing, and a no is asked again at the next run. Not taken: a field in the map for the no, which changes the map's shape to stop a question whose yes is what keeps an agent file out of a commit the staging check cannot resolve; an entry matched by its path once its remotes no longer match, for a repository whose remote was changed, which the check passes with no word and which has no report; reading a remote with no project as the one whose project has the repository's name, and decoding percent-encoded names, each unverified against a forge; reading the default config folder's map from a session under another, which would apply one folder's profiles in a folder set up apart on purpose, so the README says how the second folder names the first one's store; and moving `reviewed/<key>` (decision 332). Reopens: an agent file committed from a clone the map should have matched, a repository whose remote changed and went unchecked, or a second config folder whose missing map went unnoticed.

339. **`git` and a subcommand make a command a post only on one line, and a denial names what made a command a post.** The check took a command for a commit, a merge or a tag where it held `git` and one of those words anywhere (decision 316). A command that only appended a text to a local file was denied where the text named `git` on one line and held `commit` or `tag` on another, as a log that names its shell and reports a commit does. A report of such a denial could not give its cause, since the reason said only that the listed string was in the command; the text that report quoted passes, so what fired there is not known. The two words now count where one line holds both, in either order, a line continued with a backslash, a backtick or an open parenthesis at its end being one, while a line that ends in a comma, a pipe or a brace is not joined, since a line of plain text ends in those too, and anywhere in a command that holds a `$` or the word `xargs`, since a variable or the input of `xargs` may hold the program or the subcommand. The change was held to the rule of decision 316, that a needless stop is paid before a leak: a table of typed forms is read as before, an option whose quoted value holds a `;` or an `&`, both continuations, the program or the subcommand in a variable set on another line, a shell given the command, `xargs git` after the subcommand, and a here-document or a here-string for the message. One simple command, as the push test has it, would have lost the quoted `;` and the `xargs` form, so the line is the unit. No longer read: a commit whose two words sit on two lines of one call with no `$` and no `xargs` in it, as with an alias defined and used there. Still read where it posts nothing: a text that holds both words on one line, and any text that holds a forge program's name as a word, which decision 337 leaves as it is. So the reason for a hit in the command now names what made the command a post, `git` and the subcommand that comes first after `git`, else the first in the command, the forge program or the `post:` phrase, so a word of the message is not named where the command is another subcommand, and the README says a text that trips the check and posts nothing can be written with a file tool, which the hook does not read. Not taken: reading only the commands that push or post by their target, which the hook cannot tell from the text; leaving a here-document's body out of the test, which would stop reading a shell fed its commands that way; and an exemption for a local file by name, which would also exempt the name in a message. Reopens: a commit the narrower test did not read, or a local write still denied often enough to drop a listed string.

340. **Concise Plain 4.18: a draft is checked before it is published as well as before it is shown.** The style, the `draft` skill's description and its first line timed the check to the first showing of a draft (decision 282). A message the user asks to have published at once, such as a ticket created on request, is never shown first, so read to the letter the check had no moment to run. No check skipped that way is on record: the report is of the wording, and a pull request description published unshown was checked first under the same text. The three places now say shown or published, and requirements 364 and 366 follow. The chat block names no skill and changes only its version line. Not taken, from the same report: a line saying the named reader is each draft's own where one call checks several drafts, and a closing form for a reply that only waits on background work, each seen once with no wrong result on record. Reopens: a draft published without the check, which moves the check's list into the style, as decision 282 has it.

341. **A push is read with one `git` call per folder inside the hook's own time, and a push from a folder the hook cannot find is denied.** The hook's entry gave it 10 seconds and lets the call go ahead when the hook fails (decisions 193 and 316). A field report had a push with a listed string in a commit message run unchecked: with `check: diff`, pushed from a folder other than the call's own, where each `git` call took seconds, the hook passed its limit, and the same push was denied once the entry's limit was 60. Read here: such a push started four `git` calls, two in the call's folder and two in the named one, and two from the call's own folder; and the hooks reference says a hook stopped at its timeout does not block the call unless `onFailure` is `block`, which would block every shell call where the hook cannot run (decision 193). The overrun itself was not reproduced, each call here taking a fraction of a second. The script now keeps its own time. One `git log` per folder gives the messages and, with `check: diff`, the added lines, its output cut at a mark made for the call, so a control character in a commit no longer ends a piece; a folder spelled two ways is read once, where the call's folder with a trailing `.` and the same folder after `-C` were read twice. The calls counted from git's own trace went from two to one, from four to two, and for the folder spelled two ways from four to one. The entry's `timeout` is 60 and the `git` calls of one input share 45 seconds; a push whose commits were not read in that time is denied, with the folder and the limit in the reason, since the script can answer only while the host still waits for it. A `git` call given half a second and stopped at that limit returned at once where `git` is a launcher that starts the real program. The second failure: a push folder written through a variable, as in `git -C "$env:NAME/repo" push`, or misspelled, named no directory for the hook, which skipped it without a word, while the same folder as a literal path was denied. A variable in the path of a folder or a file is now read from the hook's environment in each shell tool's own form, a folder is looked for in the call's folder and in each folder named before it, since `cd a && cd b` pushed from a folder that was never read, and a push in a command that names a folder still not found is denied with the name as typed. A warning would not do: the host adds a hook's `additionalContext` beside the tool's result, after the push has run. The deny holds only where no quote and no line break comes before the name, as for a resolved path (decision 316), since `cd` and `-C` are also words of a message, and a commit whose message says "the CD pipeline", typed with a push, would be denied; a name that starts with `-` is an option or `cd -`; and a `-C` counts only as an option of `git` itself ahead of its subcommand, since `grep -C 3` and `make -C build` name no folder to push from. A diff that cannot be read stays a warning: the messages of those commits were read, and what stops the read, a clone that lacks objects or output past the buffer, is still there on the next try, so a deny would stop every push from that clone until the line `check: diff` is taken out. Costs: a needless stop for a push after `cd "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)"`, after a variable the command sets, or from a folder the same command makes, each of which the reason says how to retype; and up to 45 seconds before a push that cannot be read is refused. Still not read: a folder not found whose name comes after a quote or a line break. Not taken: `onFailure: block`, for the reason of decision 193; a limit the user sets, with no push on record that 45 seconds did not cover; and reading refspecs to send fewer commits through the read. Also not taken, from the same report: an exception to the house rule that a project's code outranks its instruction file (decision 114) for a file that a check or a reviewer enforces. It rests on one case whose cause was not established, a finding of a reviewer that held new files to the instruction file, and the reviewers of this suite read a project's instruction files too, so the exception would apply nearly everywhere and bring back the result decision 114 was written against. Reopens: a push that ran unchecked after the hook was stopped; a push refused for its time on every try, which adds the limit the user sets; needless stops for a folder the command makes or names through `$( )` often enough to be worked around; or a second case of a review finding against code that followed the code, with what the reviewer was given.
